State Jurisdiction

The power of the State over persons, property and events — exercised on five recognised bases (territory, nationality, protection, universality, and the passive personality), split into the power to prescribe and the power to enforce, and examined on both its civil and its criminal side. Built on S.K. Verma's Chapter 7 (State Jurisdiction) and Chapter 9 (The State and the Individual), on the DU LB-205 case material — Abu Salem v. State of Maharashtra (casebook pp. 177–185) and Arrest and Return of Savarkar (France v. Great Britain, PCA 1911) (casebook pp. 186–189) — on the ICJ's Arrest Warrant judgment (case 37), on the Indian Extradition Act, 1962, on the Foreigners Act, 1946 and the refugee/asylum jurisprudence, and on the classic authorities from Lotus and The Franconia to Eichmann, Pinochet and Soering.

☰How to Use These Notes

Topic 6 is the shortest head in the syllabus — five hours — but it is the head that quietly supplies the grammar of every other topic. State responsibility asks whose conduct it is; the law of the sea asks whose law governs the ship; diplomatic relations ask whose courts are excluded; international criminal law asks who may prosecute. Every one of those questions is a jurisdiction question. The topic is therefore best learned not as a list of five heads but as one instrument with two dials: which basis of jurisdiction is being invoked, and which power — to prescribe or to enforce — is being exercised. The red-ink annotation on the syllabus, "Civil" and "criminal" with arrows to head 6.4, is the teacher telling you that extra-territoriality must be dealt with on both sides: the criminal side (does India's penal law reach conduct abroad?) and the civil side (may an Indian court take a dispute with a foreign element, and what happens to a foreign judgment?).

What is inside

Every sub-head of 6.1 to 6.5 is treated in full. Each of the three prescribed cases — Arrest Warrant (case 37), Abu Salem (case 38) and Savarkar (case 39) — is given facts, issues, the arguments of both sides, the decision and the principle evolved, as the standing instruction for this project requires. The classic supporting authorities — Lotus, The Franconia, Eichmann, Pinochet, Joyce, Yunis, Rauscher, Castioni, Soering, the Indian cases from Mubarak Ali to Surinder Kaur Sandhu — are digested in the same way, though more briefly.

What is added

Because the instruction is to place every related order, rule and Act at the point where it arises, the notes carry the Indian Extradition Act, 1962 section by section; the Foreigners Act, 1946; the Extradition (India–Portugal) notification, GSR 822(E) of 13 December 2002; the 1957 European Convention on Extradition and the 2002 European Arrest Warrant; the Commonwealth scheme; the Genocide Convention's Article VII and the torture and hijacking conventions; the treaty practice on double criminality and on assurances against the death penalty; and India's own extra-territorial provisions in the penal, tax, competition, company and information-technology statutes. Real examples are given wherever the law has actually been used.

A note on the three prescribed cases

The DU case material for Topic 6 prints Abu Salem (casebook pp. 177–185) and Arrest and Return of Savarkar (pp. 186–189, including the text of the United Kingdom–France Arbitration Agreement of 25 October 1910 and the award). It does not print a digest of case 37, Arrest Warrant of 11 April 2000 (Democratic Republic of the Congo v. Belgium), which appears in the syllabus by citation only (I.C.J. Reports 2002, p. 3). That case is therefore set out in §6.3 G from the judgment and the Court's own official summary, with a provenance note; nothing in it is inferred. The same convention is followed where a modern development is reported from press or institutional sources rather than from a judgment: the source is named in the text or in Appendix E.

6.0The Problem Stated — What Jurisdiction Is

A. Sovereignty, Equality and the Power to Regulate

Jurisdiction is the power of a State, under international law, to regulate or otherwise to affect people, property and circumstances. The definitions used in the Delhi University notes put it in three ways, and each is worth memorising because each captures a different aspect:

  • Malcolm Shaw (through the class notes): "Jurisdiction concerns the power of the State under international law to regulate or otherwise impact upon people, property and circumstances and reflects the basic principles of State sovereignty, equality of States and non-interference in domestic affairs."
  • D.J. Harris: "State jurisdiction is the power of a State under international law to govern persons and property by its municipal law. It includes both the power to prescribe rules (prescriptive jurisdiction) and the power to enforce them (enforcement jurisdiction)."
  • Oppenheim (as Verma cites it): jurisdiction "concerns essentially the extent of each State's right to regulate conduct or consequences of events".

Three ideas stand behind those definitions. First, jurisdiction is an attribute of sovereignty: "the exercise of jurisdiction by a State is an essential attribute of State sovereignty", as Verma opens Chapter 7. A State which could not make law for what happens on its territory, or for its own nationals, would not be a State in the legal sense at all. Second, jurisdiction is bounded by the sovereignty of others: since all States are equal and none may exercise authority in the territory of another, the bases on which a State may reach out beyond its own territory are limited and must be justified. Third, jurisdiction is the meeting point of international law and municipal law — international law says which claims a State may make; the State's own constitution and statutes say whether and how the claim is actually made. That is why every question in this topic has two layers: the international permission and the domestic provision. A good answer always shows both.

Jurisdiction may be exercised through any organ of the State — by the legislature making a statute, by the executive enforcing it, by the courts deciding a case. The class notes give the Indian illustration: "In India, for instance, the Parliament passes binding statutes, the courts make binding decisions and the administrative machinery of government has the power and jurisdiction (or legal authority) to enforce the rules of law." The three organs correspond to three practical questions, and an examiner will reward a candidate who separates them: legislative jurisdiction (may Parliament enact this?), judicial jurisdiction (may this court try this?), and executive or enforcement jurisdiction (may the police, the tax authority or the navy act, and where?).

B. Prescriptive and Enforcement Jurisdiction the distinction that decides most questions

The single most useful distinction in the topic is between the power to prescribe and the power to enforce. Prescriptive jurisdiction (also called legislative jurisdiction, or "jurisdiction to prescribe") is the competence to make rules — by statute, by delegated legislation, or (in a common-law country) by judicial decision — which purport to govern conduct. Enforcement jurisdiction (jurisdiction "to enforce") is the competence to secure compliance with those rules by executive action or through the courts: to arrest, to seize property, to investigate, to compel attendance, to punish.

The two are governed by different rules, and the difference is not merely academic. Prescriptive jurisdiction may, in recognised circumstances, be exercised over conduct abroad: Parliament may legislate for offences committed outside India, or for conduct abroad that damages India. Enforcement jurisdiction is territorial in a much stricter sense: with few exceptions a State may not exercise its authority in the territory of another State — it may not send its police into a foreign country, arrest a suspect there, seize property there or carry out a search there. The class notes teach the point with an example that is worth reproducing in an examination:

"To give an instance, if a man kills somebody in India and then manages to reach Dubai, the Indian Courts have jurisdiction to try him, but they cannot enforce it by sending officers to Dubai to apprehend him. They must apply to Dubai authorities for his arrest and dispatch to India. If, on the other hand, the murderer remains in India then he may be arrested and tried there, even if it becomes apparent that he is a Chinese national."

Three consequences follow. (i) A State may lawfully prescribe for conduct abroad and yet be practically unable to enforce it; the law is not invalid for that reason — it awaits the presence of the offender, the cooperation of the territorial State, or the assistance of extradition. (ii) The means by which the presence is obtained matters: a person lawfully surrendered under a treaty, a person deported administratively, and a person abducted by agents abroad stand, in international law, in different positions — which is exactly what the Savarkar and Eichmann cases, and the rule of speciality, are about. (iii) Because enforcement requires either control of territory or consent, extradition, mutual legal assistance and the "prosecute or extradite" clauses of treaties are not decorative: they are the machinery that gives prescriptive jurisdiction any bite at all.

Fig. 6.1 · The two powers compared. Prescription may be extra-territorial; enforcement may not, except by consent, treaty or a recognised licence such as hot pursuit. Every problem in Topic 6 is solved by first asking which of the two powers is in issue.

C. Civil and Criminal Jurisdiction

Jurisdiction is exercised on both the criminal and the civil side, and the two differ in their legal technique, their forums and their purposes.

  • Criminal jurisdiction is the State's power to define offences and to try and punish offenders. It is asserted by a State in its own name, as a matter of public order, and it is the side of jurisdiction on which international law has developed its five recognised bases (territorial, nationality, protective, universal, passive personality). The question whether a State "has jurisdiction" in a criminal case is, in the ordinary case, the question whether the offence or the offender is linked to the State in a way that international law permits.
  • Civil jurisdiction is the power of the courts to adjudicate disputes between private parties (or between a private party and a State acting in a commercial capacity) involving a foreign element. Here the municipal court applies private international law — the rules of its own law which decide whether the court has jurisdiction, which law governs the dispute, and whether a foreign judgment will be recognised or enforced. Verma states the general position: "In matters of civil jurisdiction, the municipal courts apply private international law in those cases where a foreign element is involved. But the courts in normal circumstances are reluctant to exercise jurisdiction unless there is a 'substantial connection' between the foreign element and the forum either by allegiance or by domicile, i.e., that the defendant or the facts of the case should have some connection with the forum State. The exercise of jurisdiction without such a connection may be an ultra vires act, which could lead to international responsibility of the State."

The two sides interact more than the separation suggests. A criminal prosecution for double taxation fraud or for a competition-law cartel has a civil counterpart; a civil judgment rendered in breach of State immunity may be unenforceable; and the very same enforcement act — a seizure of cargo, a warrant of arrest — may be measured both against the civil rules of immunity and against the public-law rules on enforcement jurisdiction. The red-ink annotation on the syllabus ("Civil", "criminal", with arrows at 6.4) directs the candidate to keep both in view, and §6.4 does so with a comparison table (Table 6.8).

Exam note — the two distinctions are the skeleton of every answer

When a question says "Discuss the extra-territorial jurisdiction of States", begin with the prescribe/enforce distinction, then split into criminal and civil, then take each basis of jurisdiction in turn with a case. When a question is problem-based (X's nationals commit an act abroad; Y's courts arrest them), identify (i) which State is exercising prescriptive jurisdiction and on what basis; (ii) which State is exercising enforcement jurisdiction and whether it has the territory, the consent, or the presence it needs; (iii) whether any immunity, or the rule of speciality, or a treaty bars the exercise; and (iv) what the remedy of the aggrieved State is. That four-step method answers nearly every problem the examiner can set, including the 2025 paper's extradition question (PYQ 1).

D. The Five Bases on One Page

International law recognises a closed list of accepted bases on which a State may claim jurisdiction. The list is "closed" in the sense that a claim falling outside all of them is unlawful; it is not closed in the sense that the boundaries of each basis are uncontested — the cases in this topic are largely disputes about where those boundaries lie.

Table 6.1 · The five bases of criminal jurisdiction, with the leading authority and the Indian provision
BasisThe link relied onLeading authorityIndian provision / practice
TerritorialityThe offence is committed — wholly or in part — in the State's territory (including its ships and aircraft).Lotus (PCIJ, 1927); The Franconia (1876) and the Territorial Waters Jurisdiction Act, 1878; Mubarak Ali Ahmad v. State of Bombay, AIR 1957 SC 857IPC ss. 2, 3 and 4 (now BNS 2023, s. 1(2)–(4)); punishment of inchoate and continuing offences; Central Bank of India v. Ram Narain, AIR 1975 SC 361
Nationality (active)The offender is the State's national.Joyce v. D.P.P., [1946] AC 347 (allegiance); Blackmer v. United States, 284 U.S. 421 (1932)IPC s. 4; Merchant Shipping Act, 1958; UAPA, 1967; NDPS Act, 1985; Companies Act and tax statutes
Passive personalityThe victim is the State's national.Lotus (the Turkish argument; rejected by the majority as regards the ship, not the person); U.S. v. Yunis (1988); the Hostages Convention, 1979, Art. 5(1)(d)SUA Act, 2002; Anti-Hijacking Act, 2016; prosecutions arising out of the 26/11 attacks, 1999 hijacking and the Enrica Lexie incident
ProtectiveThe conduct threatens the State's security, currency, or other vital interests, even though committed abroad by an alien.Joyce v. D.P.P.; K.T.M.S. Abdul Cader v. Union of India, AIR 1977 Mad. 386; G.B. Singh v. Union of India, AIR 1973 SC 2667Official Secrets Act, 1923; Atomic Energy Act, 1962; UAPA; the currency and counterfeit provisions of the penal law
UniversalityThe crime is one of a small class that offends the international community as a whole, so that any State may prosecute regardless of any other link.Eichmann (Israel, 1961–62); Arrest Warrant (ICJ, 2002); Pinochet (No. 3) (HL, 1999); UNCLOS Art. 105 (piracy)Maritime Anti-Piracy Act, 2022; UNCLOS Art. 105 as part of Indian law; the Geneva Conventions Act, 1960; the Genocide Convention's implementing provisions

Two of these deserve a word at the outset because they are the least understood. The effects doctrine is not a separate basis but a generous form of objective territoriality: it treats conduct abroad as having been "committed" within the State because its substantial effects are felt there; it has been applied most aggressively in competition and securities law (§6.1 C). The passive personality principle is genuinely separate — it takes the nationality of the victim as the link — and although it was once treated as dubious, it is now securely established in the anti-terrorism conventions, which require States to extend jurisdiction to offences committed against their nationals (Table 6.6).

E. Where the Rules Come From

Four sources supply the law of this topic, and an answer that names them will always look well-organised:

  1. Customary international law supplies the framework: territorial supremacy over events within the State; the requirement of a recognised link for claims over events abroad; the prohibition on exercising enforcement authority in the territory of another State; the sovereign equality of States; and the immunity of certain persons and property.
  2. Treaties supply the practice: extradition treaties with their lists of extraditable offences; the sectoral conventions that require States to establish jurisdiction over piracy, hijacking, hostage-taking, torture, genocide and terrorism (the "prosecute or extradite" network); and the jurisdictional clauses of the law-of-the-sea conventions, which allocate criminal and civil jurisdiction over ships between flag State, coastal State and port State.
  3. Municipal law supplies the actual machinery: penal codes, extradition acts, foreigner laws, immigration rules, and the private international law rules on jurisdiction and the recognition of judgments. International law says what a State may claim; the municipal statute says what it does claim, and how.
  4. Judicial decisions supply the boundaries: Lotus on the presumption of freedom; The Franconia on the territorial sea; Eichmann and Arrest Warrant on universal jurisdiction and immunity; Savarkar and Abu Salem on the consequences of irregular surrender and the limits of the rule of speciality.

H. Ten Sentences That Answer Any Jurisdiction Question

The ten sentences
  1. Jurisdiction is an attribute of sovereignty, but it must be exercised within the limits that the sovereignty of other States imposes.
  2. The power to prescribe and the power to enforce are distinct: prescription may be extra-territorial on a recognised basis, enforcement may not be exercised in another State's territory.
  3. Territory is the primary basis, and it operates both subjectively (offence begun within) and objectively (offence completed within).
  4. A substantial effect within the State may be treated as an objective territorial element — the effects doctrine.
  5. Nationality supplies a second basis, resting on the link of allegiance between the State and the individual.
  6. The protective principle allows a State to punish conduct abroad aimed at its security, currency or other vital interests.
  7. Universal jurisdiction excepts a small class of crimes — piracy and the grave international crimes — from the requirement of any link.
  8. Most modern treaty crimes are handled through aut dedere aut judicare, not through pure universal jurisdiction.
  9. Jurisdiction and immunity are different questions: jurisdiction does not imply the absence of immunity, and immunity does not imply the absence of jurisdiction.
  10. Extradition, deportation, rendition and asylum are the four ways in which the presence of the person — the precondition of enforcement — is obtained or refused.

↑ top

6.1Territorial Jurisdiction

A. The Territorial Principle — Why It Comes First

Every State is sovereign within its territory, and the first consequence of that sovereignty is the power to make and apply law to everything that happens there. Verma begins the discussion of the bases of jurisdiction with this ground: "The power of a State to exercise jurisdiction over persons, property or events occurring within its territory is conceded by international law. All persons present, even transiently, and incidents occurring therein are amenable to the State's jurisdiction." Two features of that sentence should be noted. The power does not depend on the person's nationality — it depends on presence: a Chinese national who kills a man in Delhi is tried in Delhi because the killing happened in Delhi, not because of who he is. And the power does not depend on long residence — presence, even transient presence, suffices.

International law's own summary of the principle is the famous passage of the Lotus case: "Jurisdiction is certainly territorial; it cannot be exercised by a State outside its territory except by virtue of a permissive rule derived from international custom or from a convention." Verma gives four reasons why the connection between territory and criminal jurisdiction is so close:

  1. the State where the crime is committed generally has the strongest interest in punishing the criminal;
  2. the local forum is the most convenient one, since the witnesses and other facilities are most probably available there;
  3. the offender is most likely to be found there; and
  4. it avoids the element of double jeopardy — subjecting the same person to two legal systems at the same time.

Those four reasons are worth reproducing because they also explain why the exceptional bases are exceptional. If the State of the situs has the strongest interest, the most convenient forum and (usually) custody of the accused, then the claims of other States — the State of nationality, the State whose security was attacked, the State whose national was the victim — are ordinarily second-best, and international law tolerates them only where the link they rely on is a real one. The whole of the rest of Topic 6 is a study of what counts as a real link.

B. Subjective and Objective Territoriality

Territorial jurisdiction becomes difficult only in the case of the "cross-border" offence: a crime planned or begun in State X and completed in State Y. Verma poses the problem precisely: "Normally, no problem will arise for an offence if it is either exclusively committed within the State territory or outside that territory. But it becomes problematic if the crime has been planned and set in motion in one State and has its effects in the territory of another State. For example, if A in State X shoots and kills B in State Y, or if A obtains money by false pretences by means of a letter posted in State X to B in State Y, an offence is probably committed in both the States."

Two principles resolve the difficulty, mirroring each other:

Table 6.3 · The two faces of territoriality
PrincipleDefinitionIllustration and authority
Subjective territorialityA State has jurisdiction over a crime when it is commenced within the State but completed or consummated abroad. The State claims because the conduct began on its territory.A shoots across a frontier and kills B in the neighbouring State. India legislates for the offence by providing that offences committed beyond India may be tried in India (IPC s. 3); the criminal law does not permit the offender to escape because the bullet crossed the line.
Objective territorialityA State has jurisdiction when a crime is commenced abroad but completed or consummated within its territory. The State claims because an element of the offence (or its effect) occurred on its territory.Verma: "The objective territorial principle is generally accepted and applied, according to which jurisdiction can be exercised when any essential constituent element of a crime is consummated in State territory." Lotus (effect on Turkish vessel); D.P.P. v. Doot, [1973] AC 807 (conspiracy to import drugs; agreement made abroad, effected in England).

The objective principle's test is the constituent-element test: jurisdiction exists if any essential ingredient of the offence took place within the territory. The Harvard Research Draft Convention on Jurisdiction with Respect to Crime, 1935 — which Verma cites — codified the same idea in its Article 3: a crime is committed "in part" within a State's territory. In D.P.P. v. Doot the House of Lords applied it to conspiracy: the respondents, aliens, agreed abroad to import cannabis resin into the United Kingdom and were arrested in England while executing the agreement. Lord Wilberforce observed: "The present case involves international elements — the accused are aliens and the conspiracy was initiated abroad — but there can be no question here of any breach of any rules of international law if they are prosecuted in this country." Because a conspiracy is a continuing offence which endured while the parties were in England, the English courts had jurisdiction.

One of the earliest and most instructive examples of the refusal to apply the objective principle is the Cutting case (1886), which Verma notes. A. K. Cutting, an American citizen, published in a Texas newspaper a statement defamatory of a Mexican, Emigdio Medina, in Mexico. He was arrested in Mexico for the offence committed (by publication) partly in Mexican territory; a diplomatic controversy with the United States followed in which the United States denied that Mexico could punish an act done by an alien abroad. The matter was ultimately resolved diplomatically, and the prosecution was abandoned. The case is worth remembering for two reasons: it shows how long the United States resisted objective territoriality, and it shows that the principle cannot be applied mechanically — the place where the "effect" was felt must be a place where a constituent element of the offence actually occurred. (In modern law a prosecution of the same facts would turn on the law of defamation and on the passive-personality and effects principles discussed below.)

C. The Effects Doctrine

The effects doctrine is objective territoriality pushed to its furthest point. Instead of asking where an element of the offence occurred, it asks where the conduct took effect; and if the effect within the State is substantial and direct, the State claims to prescribe for the conduct abroad. The doctrine is best known in two fields — competition (antitrust) law and securities regulation — and it has generated the sharpest jurisdictional conflicts of the last eighty years.

  • The origin. In United States v. Aluminium Co. of America ("Alcoa"), 148 F.2d 416 (2d Cir. 1945), Judge Learned Hand held that the Sherman Act reaches agreements made abroad by foreign nationals provided they were intended to affect, and did affect, American commerce. The doctrine was refined in the Timberlane and Hartford Fire litigation, where the American courts moved (in the Restatement (Third) of Foreign Relations Law, § 403) toward a "reasonableness" test weighing the interests of the States concerned.
  • The European response. The then European Economic Community accepted the effects doctrine in the Wood Pulp and Dyestuffs cases, and in the Gencor and Boeing/McDonnell Douglas merger decisions applied it to concentrations taking place wholly outside the Community; the General Court in Intel (2014) confirmed that the test is the qualified effects test — whether the conduct is likely to have an immediate and substantial effect within the internal market.
  • The defensive reaction. States on the receiving end enacted blocking statutes (Canada's 1985 Foreign Extraterritorial Measures Act; the United Kingdom's Protection of Trading Interests Act, 1980, with its "claw-back" provision; the EU's 1996 Blocking Regulation against the American sanctions on Cuba, Iran and Libya). The Airbus and IBM disputes of the 1980s, and the French refusal to produce documents, are the classic illustrations of the resulting collision.
  • India's express adoption. Section 32 of the Competition Act, 2002 confers jurisdiction on the Competition Commission of India over an agreement or abuse of dominance "taking place outside India" where it has, or is likely to have, an appreciable adverse effect on competition in the relevant market in India. Section 18(3) of the Companies Act-related provisions, section 75 of the Information Technology Act, 2000, section 3 and the Explanation to section 9 of the Income-tax Act, 1961 (as amended after Vodafone — see §6.4 G) and the Insolvency and Bankruptcy Code's cross-border provisions all contain comparable reach. The Competition (Amendment) Act, 2023 continued that structure.
Real example — where the effects doctrine bit

The "Union Carbide" competition and environmental ripples. The effects doctrine is used most often in cases in which the conduct — a cartel, a merger, a price-fixing arrangement — takes place entirely abroad but the market which suffers is domestic. India's own Competition Commission has applied section 32 to conduct outside India by companies incorporated abroad, in cartel and abuse-of-dominance matters, applying the statutory test of an "appreciable adverse effect on competition within the relevant market in India". For the examination it is enough to know the structure: conduct abroad + substantial effect in India + a statute which expressly says so = an extra-territorial prescription that international law accepts as an application of objective territoriality.

Exam note — effects doctrine and objectivity are not the same thing

The objective territorial principle requires a constituent element of the offence within the territory; the effects doctrine asks only whether a substantial effect was felt there. The first is uncontroversial; the second is contested, and is accepted in practice only where the effect is direct, substantial and foreseeable, and where the State exercising it has enacted a clear statutory reach. In an answer, always distinguish: (i) offence completed within the territory → territoriality proper; (ii) some element within → objective territoriality; (iii) only the effect within → effects doctrine. That progression is the safest way to demonstrate command of the doctrine.

D. Territory of What? Land, Sea, Air, Ships, Aircraft

A State's "territory" for jurisdictional purposes is not only its land. Four extensions matter, and each has its own rules:

  • Internal waters and the territorial sea. The territorial sea is part of the State's territory, and criminal jurisdiction over ships passing through it is governed by Article 27 of UNCLOS 1982 (and, in respect of the 1958 régime, Article 19 of the Geneva Convention on the Territorial Sea). The coastal State may arrest persons or conduct an investigation on board a foreign ship passing through the territorial sea only in the cases listed in Article 27(1) — if the consequences of the crime extend to the coastal State, if the crime disturbs the peace of the country or the good order of the territorial sea, if the assistance of local authorities has been requested, or if the measures are necessary for the suppression of illicit traffic in narcotic drugs. Article 27(2) excludes the coastal State's jurisdiction over crimes committed before the ship entered the territorial sea, when the ship is only passing through; Article 27(5) preserves the coastal State's right to take any steps authorised by its law for the arrest or investigation of a ship passing through the territorial sea after leaving internal waters. This is the law-of-the-sea material re-expressed as a jurisdiction rule, and it is the point at which Topics 5 and 6 meet. (For the full treatment, see the Topic-5 notes, §5.1.1; the point for Topic 6 is that the coastal State's criminal jurisdiction over a passing ship is not the general criminal jurisdiction that it enjoys on land.)
  • Ships on the high seas. A ship on the high seas is subject to the exclusive jurisdiction of its flag State (UNCLOS Art. 92(1)); the flag State's law governs the ship, and — as a matter of municipal fiction recognised in most legal systems — acts done on board are treated as done in the territory of the flag State. Verma notes that "crimes committed on ships or aircraft flying a State's flag are considered to be committed on the State's territory". The exceptions are piracy (Art. 105), slave trade and unauthorised broadcasting (Art. 110 rights of visit), the right of hot pursuit (Art. 111), and the treaty-based powers of intervention on the high seas for pollution (Art. 221 and the 1969 Intervention Convention).
  • Aircraft. A State has jurisdiction over offences committed on board aircraft registered in that State. The Tokyo Convention on Offences and Certain Other Acts Committed on Board Aircraft, 1963, Article 3(1) states the rule; Article 4 allows the State of landing, the State of the operator and the State of the lessee in certain cases to exercise jurisdiction; the Hague Convention for the Suppression of Unlawful Seizure of Aircraft, 1970, and the Montreal Convention, 1971, add the aut dedere aut judicare obligation for hijacking and sabotage. In India the Tokyo Convention is implemented by the Tokyo Convention Act, 1975, and the Hague and Montreal Conventions by the Anti-Hijacking Act, 1982, now replaced by the Anti-Hijacking Act, 2016 (which also implements the Beijing Protocol, 2010 and provides the death penalty where the hijacking results in death). Verma's Chapter 7 discusses the hijacking conventions at length and sets out the Indian practice, including the 1970, 1976, 1981, 1984 and 1999 incidents and the ICAO Council case (Appeal Relating to the Jurisdiction of the ICAO Council, I.C.J. Reports 1972, p. 46).
  • Objects in outer space and installations beyond territorial limits. By Article VIII of the Treaty on Principles Governing the Activities of States in the Exploration and Use of Outer Space, 1967, a State on whose registry an object launched into outer space is carried retains jurisdiction and control over that object and over its personnel while in outer space or on a celestial body. Comparable provisions in modern law govern the State's jurisdiction over its installations on the continental shelf and in the exclusive economic zone (UNCLOS Arts. 60 and 80), and over its aircraft and ships wherever they may be. These are statutes and treaties, not the territorial principle itself, and they are the reason a candidate should write "territory includes the territorial sea, the ships and aircraft of the State, and the areas over which treaty or statute confers jurisdiction".

The S.S. "Lotus" case (France v. Turkey) — the foundational case of prescriptive jurisdiction

Permanent Court of International Justice, Series A, No. 10 (1927); 2 Hudson, World Court Reports 20 · Judgment No. 9 of 7 September 1927 · Bench: President Huber; Vice-President Loder; Judges Lord Finlay, Nyholm, Moore, de Bustamante, Altamira, Oda, Anzilotti, Pessôa, Weiss; Judges ad hoc Feizi-Davuz (Turkey) and de Lapradelle (France) · Decided by the President's casting vote (6:6)

Provenance. The case is not one of the three prescribed cases, but it is in the question bank printed with the course material ("State the facts and the decision in … The Lotus Case", Question 21(6)), and it is the source of the presumption on which every question of extra-territorial jurisdiction turns. The account below follows the judgment (Series A, No. 10) and the discussion in S.K. Verma, Chapter 7.

1. Why the case matters

Lotus is the only judgment of a world court that addresses, as a matter of first principle, the question on what basis may a State extend its criminal law to acts done outside its territory — and it answers it with a presumption of liberty for the State, whose influence (and whose correction) runs through the whole of modern practice. It matters for four propositions: (i) restrictions on State independence are not to be presumed; (ii) a State may not exercise its power in the territory of another State without a permissive rule; (iii) but in respect of prescriptive jurisdiction over acts abroad, international law leaves States a wide discretion limited only by prohibitive rules; and (iv) an effect produced in the territory (there, the sinking of a Turkish ship) may itself found jurisdiction — the objective territorial principle. It is also the case which the later law of the sea has expressly overruled on its facts: the 1952 Brussels Convention for the Unification of Certain Rules relating to Penal Jurisdiction in Matters of Collisions, and then Article 11 of the 1958 High Seas Convention and Article 97 of UNCLOS 1982, took collision cases out of the Lotus régime and gave jurisdiction over them to the flag State and the State of nationality of the offender alone.

2. Facts

(a) On 2 August 1926, the French mail steamer Lotus, bound from Marseilles to Constantinople, collided on the high seas with the Turkish collier Boz-Kourt — the collision occurred some five or six nautical miles north of Cape Sigri on the island of Mitylene, and therefore outside Turkish territorial waters. The Boz-Kourt sank with the loss of eight Turkish sailors and passengers; the Lotus, herself damaged, took the survivors on board and continued to Constantinople.

(b) On arrival, the Turkish authorities, acting under Article 6 of the Turkish Penal Code — which allowed prosecution of a foreigner for an offence committed abroad if the offence was injurious to Turkey or a Turkish national — arrested the officer of the watch of the Lotus, Lieutenant Demons, a French citizen, and the captain of the Boz-Kourt, Hassan Bey, a Turkish national. Both were charged with manslaughter. On 15 September 1926 the Criminal Court of Constantinople convicted them, sentencing Demons to eighty days' imprisonment and a fine of £22 and Hassan Bey to a heavier penalty. (Demons' sentence was later commuted.)

(c) France protested. The two Governments referred the dispute to the Permanent Court of International Justice by special agreement of 12 October 1926, asking whether Turkey had acted contrary to international law and, if so, what reparation was due. The question of enforcement of the judgment in France was expressly reserved.

3. Issues

  1. Had Turkey, by instituting criminal proceedings against Lieutenant Demons, acted in conflict with the principles of international law — and if there was such a conflict, what was the principle?
  2. Did the mere institution of the proceedings, or the arrest and detention of Demons, amount to an exercise of Turkish power in French territory or on a French ship?
  3. What reparation, if any, was due to France?

4. Arguments of the Parties

France argued that a State may not exercise its jurisdiction over an act committed abroad by a foreigner, and in particular may not try the officer of a foreign ship for a collision on the high seas: the ship on the high seas is assimilated to the territory of the flag State, and the flag State (France) alone had jurisdiction. Turkey's law was said to rest on a theory — objective territoriality — that France denied, because on the high seas there is no Turkish territory to be affected. France further relied on the fact that the effect of the collision, so far as Turkey was concerned, was the sinking of a Turkish ship, something it said could not give Turkey penal jurisdiction over a French officer.

Turkey replied that the effects of the wrongful act had occurred on a Turkish vessel, which is to be regarded as Turkish territory, so that the objective territorial principle applied; that jurisdiction was concurrent, not exclusive, so that the flag State's power did not exclude Turkey's; and — the argument which the Court accepted — that in the absence of a prohibitive rule of international law, Turkey was free to exercise jurisdiction. Turkey also argued that nothing in international law prevented the joint exercise of jurisdiction by two States, since the rights of other States are preserved rather than diminished by the exercise of jurisdiction by one of them.

5. The Court's reasoning

The Court posed the question as one of the existence of a prohibition, not of a permission. It observed that international law is the product of the free will of States, and therefore that "restrictions upon the independence of States cannot … be presumed". It drew its key distinction in two famous paragraphs:

"Now the first and foremost restriction imposed by international law upon a State is that — failing the existence of a permissive rule to the contrary — it may not exercise its power in any form in the territory of another State. In this sense jurisdiction is certainly territorial; it cannot be exercised by a State outside its territory except by virtue of a permissive rule derived from international custom or from a convention.

It does not, however, follow that international law prohibits a State from exercising jurisdiction in its own territory, in respect of any case which relates to acts which have taken place abroad, and in which it cannot rely on some permissive rule of international law. Such a view would only be tenable if international law contained a general prohibition to States to extend the application of their laws and the jurisdiction of their courts to persons, property and acts outside their territory, and if, as an exception to this general prohibition, it allowed States to do so in certain specific cases. But this is certainly not the case under international law as it stands at present. Far from laying down a general prohibition to the effect that States may not extend the application of their laws and the jurisdiction of their courts to persons, property and acts outside their territory, it leaves them in this respect a wide measure of discretion, which is only limited in certain cases by prohibitive rules; as regards other cases, every State remains free to adopt the principles which it regards as best and most suitable."

The Court then examined whether there was any rule of international law prohibiting Turkey from prosecuting Demons. France relied on the exclusivity of flag-State jurisdiction on the high seas; the Court held that the practice of States — including the numbers of prosecutions instituted by States in respect of collisions on the high seas and the absence of protests — did not establish any such prohibitive rule. The Court also rejected the argument that the effect on the Turkish ship could not found jurisdiction: the offence produced its effects on the Boz-Kourt, and the Turkish Penal Code treated that as an offence committed in Turkish territory. On the second question, the Court held that Turkey had not exercised power in French territory: Demons was arrested aboard the Lotus while she lay in Turkish waters, and the mere institution of proceedings in a Turkish court was not an act of power on French territory or on the high seas.

6. Decision

By the President's casting vote — the Court being equally divided, six judges finding for France and six for Turkey — the Court held that Turkey had not acted contrary to the principles of international law, and that there was accordingly no question of compensation to France. The order of the Court was accompanied by separate and dissenting opinions on both sides; the dissent of Judge Anzilotti, in particular, argued that jurisdiction to prescribe for acts abroad requires a positive title in international law, and became the fountainhead of the restrictive view which modern practice (especially in respect of the effects doctrine and of universal jurisdiction) has partly accepted.

7. The principle evolved

  • Freedom of prescription subject to prohibition. In the absence of a rule prohibiting it, a State is free to extend its prescriptive jurisdiction to acts abroad. This "Lotus presumption" was the starting point of every later debate — and it is now widely doubted, because modern practice has moved towards requiring a genuine link (see the separate opinions in Arrest Warrant, and the discussion in §6.3).
  • Enforcement remains strictly territorial. The prohibition on the exercise of State power in another State's territory without permission is a rule of general international law and is unaffected by the freedom to prescribe.
  • Objective territoriality acknowledged. An offence whose effects are produced on board a ship (or, by extension, on the territory) of the State may be treated as committed there.
  • The generalisation of Lotus by treaty. The 1952 Brussels Convention on Penal Jurisdiction in Matters of Collisions (Arts. 1–3) and UNCLOS Art. 97 now provide that in the event of a collision or other incident of navigation on the high seas, penal or disciplinary proceedings may be instituted only before the authorities of the flag State or of the State of which the person concerned is a national, and that no arrest or detention of the ship may be ordered by any other State. The point to make in an examination is that the Lotus result was reversed for collisions not by overruling the principle but by creating a prohibitive rule — exactly the mechanism that Lotus itself contemplated.
Reading Lotus against Verma and the class notes

Verma cites Lotus for the proposition that "there is no restriction on the exercise of jurisdiction by any State unless that restriction can be shown by the most conclusive evidence to exist as a principle of international law", and adds the caution that "although it is true that there is no restriction on a State's jurisdiction, nonetheless States generally refrain from exercising their jurisdiction over acts with which they have absolutely no concern". The class notes (which follow Shaw) put the same point structurally: jurisdiction is primarily territorial, may be based on other grounds such as nationality, but enforcement is restricted by territorial factors. Both sources are best quoted together: Lotus for the presumption, the class notes for its practical qualification.

↑ top

E. The Indian Statutory Framework

Indian law asserts territorial jurisdiction as a matter of course and extra-territorial jurisdiction in a series of express provisions. The constitutional foundation matters as much as the statutes, because it disposes of the argument that a law with extra-territorial operation is ultra vires.

  • Article 245 of the Constitution empowers Parliament to make laws "for the whole or any part of the territory of India", and Article 245(2) provides that "no law made by Parliament shall be deemed to be invalid on the ground that it would have extra-territorial operation". The Constitution thus removes the municipal-law objection at the outset; what remains is the question of international law, namely whether the claim of jurisdiction is one that other States are bound to tolerate.
  • The legislative lists distribute the competence: criminal law and procedure (Concurrent List, Entries 1 and 2); foreign affairs and the implementation of treaties (Union List, Entries 10 and 14); citizenship, naturalisation and aliens (Entry 17); admission into, and emigration and expulsion from India, passports and visas (Entry 18); "piracies and crimes committed on the high seas or in the air; offences against the law of nations committed on land or the high seas or in the air" (Entry 20); fishing and fisheries beyond territorial waters (Entry 21); and maritime shipping and navigation (Entries 24 and 25). Article 297 vests in the Union all lands, minerals and other things of value underlying the ocean within the territorial waters, the continental shelf and the exclusive economic zone, and Articles 73 and 253 complete the picture by making the Union's executive power and treaty-implementing power co-extensive with its legislative power.
  • The Indian Penal Code (and the Bharatiya Nyaya Sanhita, 2023). Section 2 of the Code makes every person liable for acts committed within India. Section 3 deals with offences committed beyond India which by Indian law may be tried within India. Section 4 — the central extra-territorial provision — applies the Code to any offence committed by (1) any citizen of India in any place without and beyond India; (2) any person on any ship or aircraft registered in India wherever it may be; and (3) any person in any place without and beyond India committing an offence targeting a computer resource located in India. The Explanation provides that "offence" includes every act committed outside India which, if committed in India, would be punishable under the Code, and the familiar illustration is: "A, who is a citizen of India, commits a murder in Uganda. He can be tried and convicted of murder in any place in India in which he may be found." Sub-section (3) is a modern effects clause grafted onto a nationality clause, and it is worth pointing out in an answer that the legislature has thus used two different bases of jurisdiction in a single section. Sections 125 to 127 of the Code (depredations and warlike acts against the territories or subjects of a friendly power) are further examples of conduct abroad made punishable in India. The Bharatiya Nyaya Sanhita, 2023, which replaced the Code with effect from 1 July 2024, retains the same extra-territorial scheme, and the Bharatiya Nagarik Suraksha Sanhita, 2023 replaces the corresponding provisions of the Code of Criminal Procedure, 1973.
  • Section 188 of the Code of Criminal Procedure, 1973 — the procedural gateway. Where an offence is committed outside India by a citizen of India, whether on the high seas or elsewhere, or by any person (not being such a citizen) on any ship or aircraft registered in India, the offender may be dealt with as if the offence had been committed at any place within India at which he may be found; but no such offence shall be inquired into or tried in India except with the previous sanction of the Central Government. That proviso is the practical expression of the protective/coordination side of extra-territoriality: the Union, not the individual complainant, decides whether a foreign-committed offence will be tried in India. The provision survives in substance in the 2023 legislation.
  • The general statutes of extra-territorial reach. The table below collects the principal ones; each is discussed at the point where its subject-matter arises.
Table 6.4 · Indian statutes with express extra-territorial operation (a working list)
