Read with notification S.O. 67/E of 27 August 1981, which extended the Indian Penal Code and the Code of Criminal Procedure to India's exclusive economic zoneTerritorial jurisdiction proper in the territorial sea; resource and enforcement jurisdiction, plus an express extension of the penal law, in the declared zones beyond it

F. Exemptions from Territorial Jurisdiction

Territorial jurisdiction is complete in principle but not in practice: international law withdraws certain persons, ships and property from the operation of the local law even while they are physically present in the State's territory. Verma lists five classes of exemption, and the list is worth reproducing because it is the syllabus's bridge to Topic 7:

  1. Foreign sovereigns and foreign States — sovereign or State immunity, resting on the equality of States; historically absolute (as in The Schooner Exchange v. McFaddon, 11 U.S. 116 (1812)), now restrictive in most States, so that acts jure imperii (governmental) attract immunity while acts jure gestionis (commercial) do not. The United Nations Convention on Jurisdictional Immunities of States and Their Property, adopted in 2004, embodies the restrictive approach (Art. 5, general rule; Arts. 10–16, exceptions; Art. 21, immunity of property from measures of constraint). In India there is no separate statute: sections 86 and 87 of the Code of Civil Procedure require the consent of the Central Government before a foreign State may be sued, and the practice has been to follow the restrictive theory. In 2025–26 the question of sovereign immunity in Indian law has been under active consideration in the Kuwait Airways litigation (see Topic 7).
  2. Diplomatic representatives and consuls — that is, the immunities of diplomatic agents under the Vienna Convention on Diplomatic Relations, 1961 (Arts. 29, 31 and 39) and of consular officials under the Vienna Convention on Consular Relations, 1963 (Arts. 40–41, 43, and the functional immunity of Art. 5). Topic 7 deals with these in detail; the point for Topic 6 is that the immunity is from jurisdiction, and it does not extinguish the underlying prohibition — it transfers the remedy to the sending State.
  3. Foreign public ships — warships and other State ships used for government non-commercial purposes enjoy immunity from the local jurisdiction while lawfully in the territorial sea or in port; UNCLOS Arts. 32 and 95–96 preserve the immunities, and Art. 30 provides the remedy of requiring a warship to leave. Verma discusses Chung Chi Cheung v. The King, [1939] AC 160 (the local court had jurisdiction over a murder on board a Chinese armed vessel in Hong Kong waters, the immunities being those that international law recognises rather than the product of any imagined extra-territoriality of the ship), and Wildenhus's Case, 120 U.S. 1 (1887) (an offence disturbing the peace of the port).
  4. Foreign armed forces — the position is ordinarily governed by agreement; absent agreement, a visiting force enjoys a measure of immunity in respect of internal discipline and acts done on duty. The NATO Status of Forces Agreement, 1951, supplies the standard model of concurrent jurisdiction, and India's practice — for example the India–Sri Lanka Accord of 1987 in relation to the Indian Peace-Keeping Force — follows the model.
  5. International and inter-governmental organisations — their officials, premises and assets enjoy immunity under their constitutive instruments and headquarters agreements (the classic example being the Convention on the Privileges and Immunities of the United Nations, 1946). This is the immunity that the ICJ considered in its 1949 advisory opinion on Reparation for Injuries, and it is the subject of the 2025 question on the legal personality of the United Nations.
Jurisdiction and immunity are different questions

The most frequently made error in this topic is to say that a diplomat, a foreign minister or a foreign State is "not subject to the law". That is wrong. The prohibition exists, and jurisdiction exists; what the immunity does is bar the exercise of jurisdiction before the courts of the forum while the immunity subsists. The ICJ stated the point in a sentence that should be memorised from Arrest Warrant: "the rules governing the jurisdiction of national courts must be carefully distinguished from those governing jurisdictional immunities: jurisdiction does not imply absence of immunity, while absence of immunity does not imply jurisdiction." The corollary, also stated by the Court, is that "immunity from jurisdiction … does not mean that they enjoy impunity".

G. The Leading Authorities on Territorial and Extra-territorial Criminal Jurisdiction

Five authorities do the work in this part of the topic. Lotus (part 02) states the general principle; The Franconia fixes the status of the territorial sea; Mubarak Ali and Central Bank of India state the Indian rule for foreign offenders; and D.P.P. v. Doot and United States v. Alcoa illustrate objective territoriality and its effects-doctrine extension.

R. v. Keyn (The "Franconia") — the case that gave the territorial sea to the criminal law

Court for Crown Cases Reserved, (1876) 2 Ex. D. 63 · Followed by the Territorial Waters Jurisdiction Act, 1878 (41 & 42 Vict. c. 73) · Discussed in S.K. Verma, Chapter 7, and in the question bank as "Franconia Case", Question 21(10)

Provenance. The account below follows the law reports and the standard histories of the case; the Indian comparison is drawn from the Territorial Waters, Continental Shelf, Exclusive Economic Zone and Other Maritime Zones Act, 1976, and from the discussion of the territorial sea in the Topic-5 notes.

1. Facts

The Franconia was a German steamer. In 1876, while passing through the English Channel within three miles of Dover — that is, within what was then claimed as the territorial waters of the United Kingdom — she collided with the British vessel Strathclyde. A British passenger, Jessie Young, was drowned. The master of the Franconia, Ferdinand Keyn, a German subject, was prosecuted for manslaughter in the English courts and convicted at the Central Criminal Court. He objected that the English courts had no jurisdiction, and the case was argued before the Court for Crown Cases Reserved.

2. Issue and arguments

The issue was whether the English courts had criminal jurisdiction over an act done by a foreigner on board a foreign vessel on the sea within three miles of the coast. The Crown argued that the territorial sea is part of the realm — that "the narrow seas" had always been within the King's jurisdiction, and that the three-mile belt was therefore English territory for all purposes. The defence argued that the offence had been committed on board a foreign ship, that the ship was not part of the territory of England, that the narrow seas belonged to the realm only for the purposes of the Admiralty's civil jurisdiction, and that a crime on a foreign ship could not be tried under the ordinary criminal law.

3. Decision

By seven judges to six, the Court held that it had no jurisdiction: the offence had been committed on board a foreign ship outside the body of an English county, and the criminal law of England did not extend to it. The conviction was quashed. Six judges dissented, taking the view that the territorial sea was within the realm.

4. The legislative correction and the principle evolved

The decision produced an immediate legislative response. The Territorial Waters Jurisdiction Act, 1878 declared that the territorial waters adjacent to the United Kingdom are within the jurisdiction of the Crown, and provided that offences committed within those waters on board foreign ships may be tried in England, subject (in the case of foreign ships) to the consent of the Secretary of State for the Home Department. The Act severed the question of jurisdiction from the question of whether the territorial sea is "territory" in a technical municipal sense, and it became the model for the statutory treatment of the territorial sea elsewhere.

The principle evolved is the modern one, now in Article 2(1) of UNCLOS 1982: "The sovereignty of a coastal State extends, beyond its land territory and internal waters …, to an adjacent belt of sea, described as the territorial sea." That sovereignty is as full as sovereignty on land, subject only to the right of innocent passage and the other provisions of the Convention. In India the same result is reached by a combination of Article 297 of the Constitution and sections 3 and 4 of the 1976 Act, which declare that "the sovereignty of India extends and has always extended" over the territorial sea, its bed and subsoil, and the airspace above; the express words "has always extended" are designed to exclude any argument of the Franconia kind. The comparative sequence — an English court denies jurisdiction in 1876, Parliament confers it in 1878, and a century later the rule is universal in Article 2 — is worth a paragraph in any answer on the territorial principle.

Mubarak Ali Ahmad v. State of Bombay

AIR 1957 SC 857 · Supreme Court of India · Cited in S.K. Verma, Chapter 7, and in Chapter 9 on extradition

Provenance. The facts and the passage quoted below follow S.K. Verma's treatment of the case, which quotes the report (AIR 1957 SC 857 at p. 868) both in Chapter 7 (State Jurisdiction) and again in Chapter 9 (State and Individual: extradition) — the second time on the specific point that the absence of the offender from India does not defeat the jurisdiction of the Indian courts.

Facts. The petitioner was being tried in the Sessions Court at Bombay for forgery and fraud. He fled to Pakistan and thence to England, and was returned to India under the (British) Fugitive Offenders Act. While on trial in Bombay he was served with a further warrant in a cheating case. He objected that he was a Pakistani national and had been outside India during the period when the offence was alleged to have been committed, and that the Indian courts therefore had no jurisdiction.

Holding and principle. The Supreme Court rejected the objection and stated the governing principle, which Verma quotes from the report (AIR 1957 SC 857 at p. 868) and which is the sentence most often reproduced in Indian answers on extra-territoriality: "The fastening of criminal liability on a foreigner in respect of culpable acts or omissions in India which are judicially attributed to him notwithstanding that he is corporeally present outside India at that time is not to give any extra-territorial operation whose locality is in India; that the liability is fastened on the person and the punishment is awarded by the law, if his presence in India for the trial can be secured." The case is authority for three propositions: the location of the offence (and not of the offender's body) determines jurisdiction under the territorial principle; that principle applies to a foreigner as it does to a citizen; and the person may be tried once his presence is secured, by whatever lawful means. It also explains the function of extradition: the offence is Indian, and only the person is abroad.

Central Bank of India v. Ram Narain

AIR 1975 SC 361 · Supreme Court of India · Cited in S.K. Verma, Chapter 7, note 3

Provenance. The proposition below is as stated and cited by Verma, Chapter 7 (State Jurisdiction).

Facts and illustration. The Supreme Court was concerned with the liability of a person in India for acts done abroad, and expressed the rule in the negative form in which it is usually cited: a foreigner cannot be made liable in India for a crime committed abroad, regardless of the fact that after committing the crime the person becomes a citizen of India or acquires domicile there. Verma cites the case for the limits of the territorial principle: liability for a crime is fastened by the law of the place of the crime, and neither later naturalisation nor later domicile converts a foreign offence into an Indian one. The case stands at the other end of the spectrum from Mubarak Ali: Mubarak Ali shows that the absence of the offender does not defeat jurisdiction over an offence committed in India; Central Bank of India shows that the presence or citizenship of the offender does not create jurisdiction over an offence committed abroad. Both illustrate the same rule — the primacy of the place of the offence.

K.T.M.S. Abdul Cader v. Union of India

AIR 1977 Mad. 386 (Full Bench) · Madras High Court · Cited in S.K. Verma, Chapter 7 (protective principle) and in the DU class notes under head 6.4

Provenance. The class notes for Topic 6 rely on this case, with a citation to S.K. Kapoor, International Law, p. 215, for the extra-territoriality discussion; the account below follows the notes and Verma.

Facts. The petitioners — one of them a foreign citizen — were alleged to have been dealing in smuggled goods. The State of Madras apprehended a threat to the safety and security of the country and passed detention orders under the Maintenance of Internal Security (Amendment) Ordinance, 1974 and the Conservation of Foreign Exchange and Prevention of Smuggling Activities Act, 1974. Because the petitioners were residing abroad, the warrants could not be served, and proclamations were issued under the Code of Criminal Procedure, followed by detention orders. The petitioners challenged the orders on the ground that the Act had no extra-territorial application and that the State Government could not order the detention of persons who were not in India when the orders were passed.

Holding and principle. The High Court rejected the contention. Three propositions were stated:

  • Extra-territorial operation is not a ground of invalidity. "The power of Parliament to make a law in relation to the topic entrusted to it is plenary, that a law passed by Parliament under Article 245 cannot be invalidated merely on the ground that it has extra-territorial operation, and that such a law cannot be questioned on the ground that it may not be found capable of enforcement outside its territories." The two senses in which the expression is used were identified: laws in respect of acts and events which take place inside the State but have operation outside, and laws with reference to the nationals of a State in respect of their acts outside.
  • Nationality carries jurisdiction abroad. "Though a State's jurisdiction is mainly territorial, its jurisdiction can extend in respect of things or acts done by its nationals, even outside its territories."
  • The protective principle is available against a foreigner. The State "has also jurisdiction which may properly be called protective jurisdiction to deal with a foreign national whose acts have jeopardised or are to jeopardise its safety or public order … If such a protective jurisdiction is not given to a sovereign State, its stability and existence itself can be shaken by acts or things done by foreigners outside its territory." The Court added the practical qualification: action against a foreigner outside the territory may be ineffective, but the impotence of enforcement does not affect the competence to prescribe — "the jurisdiction of the State to take action against a foreigner mainly depends upon its competency and not upon its effective execution".
Real example — where India has actually used extra-territorial criminal jurisdiction

The Enrica Lexie incident (2012): two Italian marines on board the Italian-flagged tanker Enrica Lexie fired on an Indian fishing vessel, the St. Antony, off the Kerala coast, killing two Indian fishermen. India asserted criminal jurisdiction; Italy denied it, invoking the exclusive jurisdiction of the flag State. The Supreme Court held that India had jurisdiction and that Article 97 of UNCLOS did not apply, because Part VII of the Convention applies only to the high seas and the incident occurred in India's contiguous zone/exclusive economic zone. The Annex VII tribunal which later heard the dispute found that the incident had occurred on the high seas, that Article 97 did not apply because the shooting was not an "incident of navigation", and that the marines were entitled to immunity as State officials. The case is the perfect illustration of the two lessons of this section: jurisdiction is measured by the place and nature of the act, and a finding of jurisdiction does not settle the question of immunity. The full treatment is in the Topic-5 notes, §5.1.4 (case 34).

Cyber offences: section 75 of the Information Technology Act, 2000 has been applied in cases of offences committed from abroad through servers and systems located abroad where the affected computer resource is in India — the statutory embodiment of objective territoriality. In practice the difficulty is not jurisdiction but the securing of evidence and the presence of the accused, which requires the mutual legal assistance machinery — in Indian practice, letters rogatory under s. 166A of the Code of Criminal Procedure, 1973 (s. 113 of the Bharatiya Nagarik Suraksha Sanhita, 2023) and the mutual legal assistance treaty network administered by the Ministry of Home Affairs.

Tax: the controversy over the Vodafone transaction (a transfer of shares in a Cayman Islands company holding Indian telecom assets) produced a judgment of the Supreme Court holding that the transaction was not taxable under the law as it stood (2012), followed by retrospective legislation, followed by the Taxation Laws (Amendment) Act, 2021 which withdrew the retrospective charge — an illustration of how far the effects-based assertion of jurisdiction can travel, and of the cost of being wrong. See §6.4 G.

H. Examination Angle for 6.1

How territorial-jurisdiction questions are set, and how to answer them

Type 1 — the theoretical question. "Discuss the territorial principle of jurisdiction with reference to the different theories." Structure: (i) territory as the primary basis, with the four reasons in Verma and the Lotus dictum; (ii) subjective territoriality; (iii) objective territoriality and the constituent-element test; (iv) the effects doctrine and its contested status; (v) the extensions of "territory" (territorial sea, ships, aircraft, installations); (vi) Indian provisions (IPC ss. 3 and 4; s. 188 CrPC; the 1976 Act; IT Act s. 75); (vii) the exemptions from territorial jurisdiction, listed briefly with a cross-reference to immunities.

Type 2 — the problem question. A shoots in State X and the victim dies in State Y; B posts a fraudulent letter from State P to State Q; C, an alien, conspires abroad to smuggle goods into India. Answer with the constituent-element test, name the competing claimants, note that concurrent jurisdiction is lawful, and identify the practical question — who has custody of the accused and who will in fact try him (D.P.P. v. Doot; Mubarak Ali).

Type 3 — the "extra-territorial operation" question. "Can Parliament legislate with extra-territorial effect?" Answer with Article 245(2), Abdul Cader (the plenary power and the two senses of extra-territoriality), and the caveat that constitutional validity does not settle international legality: the claim must still rest on one of the recognised bases, and its enforcement in another State's territory requires consent.

The five points that earn marks. (1) The place of the offence, not the nationality of the offender, is the primary connector. (2) Any constituent element suffices for objective territoriality, and an effect may suffice under the effects doctrine. (3) Prescription may travel; enforcement may not. (4) Territory includes the territorial sea and the State's ships and aircraft by express rule. (5) Immunity is a separate question from jurisdiction.

↑ top

6.2Jurisdiction based on Nationality, and the Protective Principle

A. Nationality as a Title to Jurisdiction

The second basis of jurisdiction is personal rather than territorial. The class notes put the foundation in a sentence: "Since every State possesses sovereignty and jurisdictional powers and since every State must consist of a collection of individual human beings, it is essential that a link between the two be legally established. The link connecting the State and its people in its territory is provided by the concept of nationality." Nationality is thus the legal expression of allegiance: the person owes fidelity to the State, and the State in return may make law for the person and (subject to international law) protect the person.

Three features of the nationality basis must be kept in view:

  1. It is a basis for prescription, and only occasionally for enforcement. A State may legislate for its nationals abroad — Blackmer v. United States, 284 U.S. 421 (1932), in which the United States Supreme Court upheld the power of the United States to compel one of its citizens resident in France to return and testify, on the ground that citizenship carries duties as well as rights. But it may not send officials abroad to enforce that legislation; enforcement remains territorial. This is the same division the class notes draw for the man who kills in India and escapes to Dubai.
  2. The State's own law determines who is its national; international law intervenes only at the margins, principally through the "genuine link" doctrine associated with Nottebohm (I.C.J. Reports 1955, p. 4), which concerns the opposability of nationality in diplomatic protection rather than the competence to legislate. For jurisdiction purposes, the practical questions are (i) whether the person was a national at the time of the act, and (ii) whether the other links (domicile, residence, the place of the act) make the claim a reasonable one.
  3. Nationality extends to ships and aircraft. The flag State's jurisdiction over its ships on the high seas (UNCLOS Art. 92) and the State of registration's jurisdiction over its aircraft (Tokyo Convention, 1963, Art. 3(1)) are applications of the same idea: the vessel or aircraft is treated as carrying the State's legal order with it. Verma's observation that "crimes committed on ships or aircraft flying a State's flag are considered to be committed on the State's territory" belongs here as well.

B. Active Nationality — the Nationality Principle Proper

Under the active nationality principle (sometimes simply "the nationality principle"), a State may prescribe and punish conduct of its nationals wherever committed. Verma states the principle in the pair of forms in which it is usually described: jurisdiction "by reference to the nationality either of the person committing the offence even with respect to events occurring entirely abroad or with reference to the nationality of the person injured by the offence" — the first limb being active, the second passive.

Two qualifications are commonly met:

  • Common-law restraint. As the class notes observe, "common law countries tend to restrict the crimes over which they will exercise jurisdiction over nationals abroad to very serious crimes" — typically treason, murder, offences of violence and sexual offences against children. The Indian Penal Code is not so restrained on paper: section 4 applies the whole Code to offences by citizens abroad, subject to the procedural requirement of sanction under section 188 of the Code of Criminal Procedure, 1973, which operates in practice as the restraint.
  • No requirement of double criminality in principle. Unlike extradition, the nationality principle does not require that the conduct be criminal in the State where it occurred. Most legal systems, however, require it in practice, or require that the conduct be punishable as a serious offence abroad, to avoid punishing an Indian for something lawful where it was done. Indian law contains an echo of the same concern in the sanction requirement: the Central Government will not ordinarily authorise a prosecution for conduct which was lawful at the place where it occurred.

The active nationality principle is the least controversial of the extra-territorial bases — the State is asserting authority over its own people, not over foreigners — and it is the reason why dual nationals and persons who acquire a new nationality during proceedings cause such difficulty in practice.

C. Passive Personality

Under the passive personality principle the State claims jurisdiction because the victim is its national. The principle has had a turbulent history. The Cutting case (1886) — an American editor in Texas convicted in Mexico for libelling a Mexican — produced a protest from the United States which is generally regarded as the birth of the objection to passive personality. The protests of the period reflected a real fear: if every State may punish for an injury to its own national wherever committed, then every act becomes potentially punishable in many States, and the offender can never be sure which law governs him.

The modern position is that passive personality is accepted, but narrowly, and mainly through treaty.

  • The hostage-taking conventions embody it: the International Convention against the Taking of Hostages, 1979, Article 5(1)(d), requires each State Party to establish jurisdiction where the hostage is a national of that State. The Torture Convention, 1984, Article 5(1)(c), and the Terrorist Bombings Convention, 1997, Article 6(2)(a), contain the same formula; the aircraft conventions include comparable provisions.
  • The United States, which had opposed the principle in 1886, adopted it in the 1980s in the terrorism statutes and applied it in United States v. Yunis (the hijacking prosecution discussed in §6.3 F), where both universal jurisdiction (the Hostages Convention) and passive personality were available.
  • In the practice of the courts the principle is used most often in cases of terrorism against nationals abroad, and it is now generally accepted that it may be exercised where the alleged victim is a national and the offence is one of the grave crimes, but not for ordinary offences.

India's use of the principle. Indian law asserts jurisdiction over offences against Indian nationals abroad principally through the sectoral statutes — the Suppression of Unlawful Acts against Safety of Maritime Navigation and Fixed Platforms on Continental Shelf Act, 2002 (which, following the 1988 SUA Convention, takes jurisdiction over offences against Indian ships and Indian nationals), the Anti-Hijacking Act, 2016, and the provisions of the Unlawful Activities (Prevention) Act, 1967 — rather than through the general penal law. The reason is practical: a prosecution under those statutes engages the aut dedere aut judicare machinery of the convention, which gives the assertion of jurisdiction an international footing and a route to obtaining custody of the accused.

D. The Protective Principle

The protective principle allows a State to punish conduct abroad which is aimed at its security or other vital interests, even though the conduct was committed by an alien outside its territory and even though no element of the ordinary offence took place within its borders. The class notes (following Shaw and the Max Planck Encyclopedia) define it as "the principle of international criminal jurisdiction permitting a State to grant extra-territorial effect to legislation criminalizing conduct damaging to national security or other central State interests". The Exam Capsule puts the same point in the language an examiner expects: "States have the right to exercise jurisdiction over crimes against its security and integrity or its vital economic interests under international law … Permits a State to punish acts prejudicial to its security, even when they are committed by foreigners abroad, e.g. plot to overthrow its government, espionage, forging its currency, etc."

Two justifications are given for the principle, and both should appear in an answer:

  1. The offences "may be so grave that they may have far reaching consequences to the State against which they are directed"; and
  2. "If jurisdiction is not exercised by the affected State, many such offences would escape punishment altogether because they did not contravene the law of the place where they were committed or because extradition would be refused by reason of the political character of the offence, or otherwise."

The second justification is the important one, because it explains the relationship between the protective principle and the political-offence exception in extradition: the State protects itself precisely in the cases where the ordinary machinery of cooperation will not work. The principle is therefore wider than self-defence in the Charter sense and narrower than a general licence: the interests protected must be genuinely "vital" (national security, territorial integrity and political independence, the currency, the integrity of the State's immigration controls and, increasingly, its essential economic interests), the conduct must be aimed at those interests, and the claim must be exercised consistently and with due regard to the interests of other States. Its critics argue that "vital interests" is a self-defining category, and that the principle can become a unilateralism of last resort; its defenders reply that the alternative — a right of every State to shelter conduct aimed at the destruction of another — is worse.

E. The Authorities

Joyce v. Director of Public Prosecutions

[1946] AC 347 · House of Lords · Verma, Chapter 7; the case of "Lord Haw-Haw"

Provenance. The facts and the passages below follow Verma's account and the report; the case is the standard illustration of both the protective principle and the idea of allegiance.

Facts. William Joyce was born in the United States to Irish parents and became a naturalised British subject, obtaining a British passport in 1933 (in fact by false statements as to his birth). He left the United Kingdom for Germany before the war and, during the war, broadcast propaganda against Britain from Germany, working for the German Government. When the war was over he was convicted of treason — an offence which requires a duty of allegiance to the Crown — and he objected that he was not a British subject when he committed the acts, and that the acts had taken place abroad.

Arguments. Joyce relied on the territorial principle and on the absence of the requisite allegiance at the material time: an alien abroad owes nothing to the Crown, and the exercise of the King's jurisdiction abroad would conflict with the sovereignty of the State where the acts took place. The Crown argued that Joyce, by holding a British passport and claiming the protection of the Crown, owed allegiance to the Crown, and that the crime of treason against the sovereign was one which the sovereign's courts must be able to punish wherever committed, since the security of the State itself was attacked.

Decision and principle. The House of Lords upheld the conviction. The Court stated that no principle of international law demands "that a State should ignore the crime of treason committed against it outside its territory. On the contrary, a proper regard for its own security requires that all those who commit that crime, whether they commit it within or without the realm, should be amenable to its laws." Because Joyce held a British passport and was entitled to the protection of the Crown, he owed allegiance to the Crown at the material time — even though the passport had been obtained by fraud. The case is the classic authority for two propositions: the protective principle itself, and the proposition that allegiance, not nationality in a formal sense, is the true foundation of the personal jurisdiction which a State may exercise in matters affecting its own security.

Stegeman v. United States; G.B. Singh v. Union of India

Stegeman v. U.S., 425 F.2d 984 (9th Cir. 1970) · G.B. Singh v. Union of India, AIR 1973 SC 2667 · both cited in S.K. Verma, Chapter 7

Stegeman is cited by Verma for the proposition that the protective principle has been applied in American practice to offences committed abroad against the security interests of the United States; the case illustrates that a State may punish conduct abroad by an alien where the conduct attacks the State's own functioning.

G.B. Singh v. Union of India is the Indian authority to which Verma points: the Supreme Court recognised the protective principle by stating that the first duty of a State is to survive. The proposition is important for Indian practice because it underpins the constitutional tolerance of legislation such as the Unlawful Activities (Prevention) Act which reaches conduct abroad; the survival of the State is treated as the justification for a jurisdiction that the territorial principle alone would not support.

Abdul Cader (§6.1 G) supplies the fullest Indian statement: the Madras High Court's reasoning that without a protective jurisdiction "the stability and existence itself [of the State] can be shaken by acts or things done by foreigners outside its territory", coupled with the qualification that the competence is exercised against persons abroad only if their presence can later be secured.

Fig. 6.3 · The three personal connecting factors of head 6.2. In every problem question, identify which arrow is being relied on — and then ask whether the State can in fact lay hands on the accused.

F. Indian Practice

  • Legislation. The principal provisions are collected in Table 6.4: IPC s. 4; s. 188 CrPC (with the sanction of the Central Government); the UAPA and the NDPS Act; the Official Secrets Act, 1923; the Atomic Energy Act, 1962; the Anti-Hijacking Act, 2016; the SUA Act, 2002; the Maritime Anti-Piracy Act, 2022; and the taxation statutes.
  • Prosecutions. In practice the Indian authorities prefer, where possible, to prosecute under provisions whose international foundation is secure: a hijacking is prosecuted under the 2016 Act because the Hague Convention requires jurisdiction; an act of maritime terrorism under the 2002 Act because the SUA Convention does. The general provisions (IPC s. 4 read with s. 188 CrPC) are used where the offender is an Indian citizen and the sanction can be obtained — for example in cases of offences committed in Nepal or the Gulf by Indian nationals, or offences at sea on Indian ships.
  • Counterfeit currency and terrorism finance. The prosecution of persons abroad for circulating fake Indian currency notes, and the attachment proceedings under the Prevention of Money-Laundering Act, 2002 against property representing the proceeds of terrorism, are the clearest Indian examples of the protective principle in action: the conduct is aimed at the Indian economy and the Indian State, and the offenders are typically foreign nationals outside India.
  • The limits observed in practice. India has generally refrained from exercising jurisdiction over the ordinary crimes of foreigners committed abroad, in line with Central Bank of India v. Ram Narain; and where the accused is abroad, India's practice has been to use extradition or deportation rather than to attempt enforcement abroad.

G. Examination Angle for 6.2

The answer skeleton for 6.2
  1. Nationality as a link. Define allegiance; distinguish nationality for jurisdiction (determined by the State's own law) from nationality for diplomatic protection (Nottebohm, genuine link).
  2. Active nationality. The offender's nationality; the State may prescribe for its nationals abroad (Blackmer); common-law restraint; IPC s. 4 and s. 188 CrPC; the practical problems of dual nationality and of sanctions.
  3. Passive personality. The victim's nationality; the Cutting controversy; modern acceptance by treaty (Hostages Convention Art. 5(1)(d); Torture Convention Art. 5(1)(c)); U.S. v. Yunis; India's sectoral statutes.
  4. Protective principle. Definition; the two justifications (gravity; the possibility that the offence escapes punishment altogether); the interests protected; Joyce on allegiance; G.B. Singh ("the first duty of a State is to survive"); Abdul Cader; the criticism that "vital interests" is self-defined, and the answer to it (proportionality, consistency, and regard for the interests of other States).
  5. Conclusion. Rank the bases: territory first, nationality second, protection third, passive personality fourth — and end with the enforcement point: prescription without custody is a claim waiting for a treaty or a deportation.

The sentence that lifts an answer. "The protective principle rests not on sovereignty but on necessity: it is the jurisdiction a State must have if it is not to be destroyed from outside by acts which no other State's law forbids."

↑ top

6.3Universal Jurisdiction

A. The Idea and Its Two Forms

Universal jurisdiction is the competence of a State to prosecute and punish certain offenders irrespective of any link of territory, nationality or interest. The class notes adopt the standard definition: "the competence of the State to prosecute alleged offenders and to punish them if convicted, irrespective of the place of commission of the crime and regardless of any link of active or passive nationality or other grounds of jurisdiction recognised by international law". The rationale is not the interest of the prosecuting State but the interest of the community of States: the crimes in question are "particularly offensive to the international community as a whole", and the purpose is "to ensure that no such offence goes unpunished" (Exam Capsule).

Two features of the principle must be stated correctly at the outset, because examiners probe them:

  • It is exceptional — it requires a permissive rule, not a mere absence of prohibition. The class notes put it bluntly: "Ordinarily, the universality principle is contrary to international law. However, international law allows States to exercise universal jurisdiction over certain acts which threaten the international community as a whole and which are considered criminal throughout the world." This is the opposite of the Lotus presumption: for universal jurisdiction a State must be able to point to a permissive rule — custom, as for piracy and war crimes, or a treaty, as for the modern convention crimes. The reason is obvious: a claim to try a foreigner for acts done abroad, when the State has no interest of its own, is the strongest intrusion upon the sovereignty of other States, and it is permitted only where the whole community has agreed that the crime is everyone's business.
  • It comes in two forms, and only the first is "pure". In its pure form (universal jurisdiction proper) any State may prosecute a person for the crime, whatever the circumstances, as with piracy. In its far more common treaty form, the convention obliges a State on whose territory the suspect is found either to extradite him or to submit the case to its authorities for prosecution — aut dedere aut judicare, the formula discussed in section E. The class notes capture the distinction: "contrary to their being delicts jure gentium (which can be tried by all States), these crimes are dealt on the principle of aut punire, aut dedere … They are the cases of concurrent jurisdiction and sometime remain controversial. The universality principle in these cases can serve as a basis only for auxiliary competence."
Eichmann · Pinochet Aut dedere aut judicare The State where the suspect is found must extradite, or submit the case for prosecution Hague 1970 Art. 7 · Torture Art. 7 Hostages Art. 8 · Bombings Art. 8 Ordinary bases Territory · nationality · protection · passive personality — each with its own link and its own limits Where the contested line runs (1) Is the list of universal crimes closed? Custom recognises piracy and grave breaches of the laws of war; treaty adds genocide, torture, hostage-taking, terrorism, apartheid, hijacking and maritime terrorism. (2) May universal jurisdiction be exercised in absentia? Belgium tried to do so in the Arrest Warrant case; the ICJ declined to rule on the point, and several judges doubted it. (3) Who decides that the exercise is bona fide? The African Union's complaint about indictments of African leaders produced the 2008–2012 debates and the practical test of "subsidiarity".

Fig. 6.4 · The spectrum. The further left, the weaker the link required — and the narrower the class of crimes.

B. Piracy — the Original Universal Crime

Piracy is the oldest and the least contested case of universal jurisdiction, because the pirate is the classic hostis humani generis — the enemy of all mankind — who operates outside any State's protection and outside any State's effective control. The law is codified in Articles 100 to 107 and 110 of UNCLOS 1982, which reproduce Articles 14 to 22 of the 1958 Geneva High Seas Convention, which in turn rest on centuries of custom and on the Harvard Research Draft of 1932.

  • Definition (Art. 101). Piracy consists of any illegal acts of violence or detention, or any act of depredation, committed for private ends by the crew or passengers of a private ship or aircraft, and directed (i) on the high seas, against another ship or aircraft, or persons or property on board, or (ii) against a ship, aircraft, persons or property in a place outside the jurisdiction of any State; and it includes voluntary participation in the operation of a pirate ship with knowledge of the facts, and inciting or intentionally facilitating such acts.
  • The three elements that carry the marks. Private ends — acts of a warship or a rebel group acting for political ends are not piracy, which is why politically motivated hijacking is dealt with under the SUA Convention and the anti-terrorism conventions rather than as piracy. Two ships — an act directed against the same ship (mutiny, or a hijack by persons already on board) is not piracy under Article 101(a). High seas or outside any State's jurisdiction — an attack within the territorial sea is "armed robbery against ships", punishable by the coastal State under its own law, and not piracy at all.
  • The duty to cooperate (Art. 100). "All States shall cooperate to the fullest possible extent in the repression of piracy on the high seas or in any other place outside the jurisdiction of any State." This is a duty of cooperation, not a duty to legislate.
  • Universal jurisdiction proper (Art. 105). "On the high seas, or in any other place outside the jurisdiction of any State, every State may seize a pirate ship or aircraft, or a ship or aircraft taken by piracy and under the control of pirates, and arrest the persons and seize the property on board. The courts of the State which carried out the seizure may decide upon the penalties to be imposed." This is universal jurisdiction in its purest form — no link, no permission, no presence requirement in advance. Article 106 makes a State liable for an unlawful seizure without adequate grounds; Article 107 restricts the right of seizure to warships, military aircraft, or other government ships and aircraft clearly marked and identifiable as being on government service and authorised to that effect; Article 110 confers the right of visit over a ship suspected of piracy or the slave trade.
  • Piracy jure gentium and attempt. The Privy Council held in In re Piracy Jure Gentium, [1934] AC 586, that an attempt to commit piracy is itself piracy jure gentium, i.e. that the customary crime is committed even if the intended robbery does not succeed — a useful authority for the proposition that the customary offence is not confined to its completed statutory form.
Real example — piracy law in operation, 2008 to 2026

Somali piracy and the Security Council's resolutions. Between 2008 and 2011 Somali piracy in the Gulf of Aden and the Indian Ocean produced a remarkable body of law. Beginning with Resolution 1816 (2008), the Security Council, acting under Chapter VII and with the consent of the Transitional Federal Government of Somalia, authorised States to enter Somalia's territorial sea and use "all necessary means" to repress acts of piracy and armed robbery at sea — an exceptional extension, because piracy proper can be committed only on the high seas. The resolutions were renewed repeatedly (1838, 1846, 1851, 1897, 1950, 2023, 2077), and were accompanied by the Djibouti Code of Conduct (2009) and the Contact Group on Piracy off the Coast of Somalia. The legal formula used for prosecutions is the one in Article 105: the seizing State tries the pirates in its own courts — as Kenya, Seychelles, Mauritius, the Netherlands, Germany, the United States and India have done.

India's implementing statute and the first convictions. Parliament enacted the Maritime Anti-Piracy Act, 2022 to give effect to the UNCLOS piracy provisions in Indian law, defining piracy and related offences (including conspiracy, attempt and abetment), providing for universal jurisdiction on the basis of presence in India and other Indian links, and vesting jurisdiction in a designated Sessions Court. In July 2026 that Court sentenced forty-four Somali nationals to life imprisonment in the two cases arising out of the rescue operations by the Indian Navy — the MV Ruen (recaptured in March 2024, with the pirates brought to Mumbai) and the FV Al-Kambar 786 (rescued in March 2024) — the first convictions under the 2022 Act. The sequence is a complete illustration of Article 105 at work: the Indian Navy seized, Indian courts tried, and Indian law punished, without any Indian link other than the seizure and the presence of the offenders.

C. War Crimes, Genocide and Crimes against Humanity

  • War crimes — the grave-breaches régime. The four Geneva Conventions of 1949 do not merely permit universal jurisdiction; they require it. In each Convention the High Contracting Parties undertake "to enact any legislation necessary to provide effective penal sanctions" for grave breaches, and to search for persons alleged to have committed them and to "bring such persons, regardless of their nationality, before [their] own courts", or to hand them over for trial to another Party concerned which has made out a prima facie case (Arts. 49, 50, 129 and 146 of Conventions I to IV respectively). The grave breaches are enumerated in the following articles (wilful killing, torture, inhuman treatment, biological experiments, wilfully causing great suffering, extensive destruction of property, compelling service in hostile forces, denial of fair trial, taking of hostages, unlawful deportation). Additional Protocol I of 1977 (Arts. 85 to 88) extended the list and the obligation; the Rome Statute of 1998 (Arts. 8 and 12 to 14) carries the substantive law forward. In India the Geneva Conventions Act, 1960 gives effect to the Conventions, creates the offence of a grave breach, and confers jurisdiction on Indian courts over such breaches — including when committed abroad.
  • Genocide. The Convention on the Prevention and Punishment of the Crime of Genocide, 1948, requires States to enact penal legislation (Art. V), provides that persons charged with genocide "shall be tried by a competent tribunal of the State in the territory of which the act was committed, or by such international penal tribunal as may have jurisdiction" (Art. VI), and provides that genocide and the related acts "shall not be considered as political crimes for the purpose of extradition" (Art. VII) — that is, the political-offence exception, otherwise an obstacle to extradition, is removed. The Convention's Article IX confers jurisdiction on the ICJ in disputes relating to its interpretation — the basis of the Court's jurisdiction in Bosnia and Herzegovina v. Serbia and Montenegro (2007) and in The Gambia v. Myanmar (provisional measures, 2020; preliminary objections dismissed, 2022), where the Court accepted that the obligations are owed erga omnes partes and that any party may invoke them.
  • Crimes against humanity. There is no general convention. The prohibition is customary and is implemented through national law, the ad hoc tribunals (the ICTY and ICTR Statutes) and the International Criminal Court. The International Law Commission's Draft Articles on Prevention and Punishment of Crimes against Humanity (2019) propose that each State establish jurisdiction on the familiar grounds of territory, registry and nationality, and contemplate the establishment of jurisdiction based on the presence of the alleged offender — the model, in other words, of Article 7 of the Torture Convention.
  • The International Criminal Court. The ICC exercises jurisdiction over genocide, crimes against humanity, war crimes and aggression where the State of the territory or of the offender's nationality has accepted the Court's jurisdiction (Art. 12), or where the Security Council has referred the situation (Art. 13(b)). Article 27 makes official capacity irrelevant before the Court, and Article 98 preserves the law of State and diplomatic immunity as between States. The arrest-warrant practice of the Court — including the warrants issued in 2023 and 2024 in respect of the situations in Ukraine and the Middle East — has made the practical problem of the Statute unmistakable: the Court has jurisdiction, but no police force. International jurisdiction and enforcement are different things, just as they are within the municipal system.

D. Torture, Apartheid, Hijacking and Terrorism

Beyond the core three, universal or quasi-universal jurisdiction attaches to a growing list of treaty crimes. Verma's list is worth reproducing: "Universality principle finds expression in a limited way in a number of treaties on matters concerning the international community, such as genocide, drug traffic, trafficking in women and children, counterfeiting of currency, taking of hostages, torture, apartheid, attacks on diplomats and hijacking." The principal instruments are collected in Table 6.6; three deserve a word here.

  • Torture. The Convention against Torture, 1984, requires States to criminalise torture (Art. 4) and to establish jurisdiction (Art. 5) where the offence is committed in its territory or on board a ship or aircraft registered there, or by a national, or against a national, or where the suspect is present in its territory and it has not extradited him; Article 6 requires the State which takes a suspect into custody to notify the interested States; Article 7 requires the State where the suspect is found, if it does not extradite, to "submit the case to its competent authorities for the purpose of prosecution". The clause is the modern model of aut dedere aut judicare, and it was applied with real consequences by the International Court of Justice in Questions relating to the Obligation to Prosecute or Extradite (Belgium v. Senegal), I.C.J. Reports 2012, p. 422: the Court found that Senegal had breached Articles 6(2) and 7(1) by failing, after Belgium's request, either to prosecute or to extradite Mr Hissène Habré. Senegal then created the Extraordinary African Chambers, which convicted Mr Habré on 30 May 2016 and sentenced him to life imprisonment; the conviction was confirmed on appeal in 2017. The case is the best example in contemporary practice of the difference between having jurisdiction and having to use it.
  • Apartheid. The International Convention on the Suppression and Punishment of the Crime of Apartheid, 1973, declares apartheid and the policies and practices of racial segregation and discrimination "a crime against humanity" (Art. I) and provides that international criminal responsibility attaches to individuals regardless of their official position (Art. III), and that persons charged "may be tried by a competent tribunal of any State Party to the Convention which may acquire jurisdiction over the person of the accused or by an international penal tribunal" (Art. V) — an express grant of universal jurisdiction.
  • Hijacking and terrorism. The Hague Convention, 1970 (Arts. 4, 7 and 8), the Montreal Convention, 1971, the Hostages Convention, 1979, the SUA Convention, 1988, and the Terrorist Bombings Convention, 1997, all combine three devices: a wide jurisdictional mandate (territory, registration, nationality of the offender, nationality of the victim, presence of the offender), an obligation to take custody (the "custody" provisions), and the "prosecute or extradite" clause in the strongest form — the Hague Convention's Article 7 requires a State which does not extradite "without exception whatsoever and whether or not the offence was committed in its territory, to submit the case to its competent authorities for the purpose of prosecution". The words "without exception whatsoever" are designed to deprive the political-offence exception of any application to hijacking.

E. Aut dedere aut judicare — Universal Jurisdiction's Cousin

The maxim descends from Grotius's aut dedere aut punire: the State which holds the offender must either surrender him or punish him. Its modern form is deliberately weaker, because it speaks of prosecution rather than punishment: the obligation is to "submit the case to the competent authorities for the purpose of prosecution", with the authorities taking their decision "in the same manner as in the case of any ordinary offence of a serious nature under the law of that State" (Hague Convention 1970, Art. 7; Torture Convention, Art. 7; Terrorist Bombings Convention, Art. 8). Three consequences follow, and they are the material on which questions in this area are usually set:

  1. The obligation is triggered by presence, not by the gravity of the crime. It is the State "in the territory of which the alleged offender is found" that must act. This is why extradition requests and deportations matter so much: the State which obtains custody of the suspect acquires a legal duty to deal with him, or to send him elsewhere.
  2. Prosecutorial discretion survives, but not as a licence to do nothing. The obligation is to submit the case to the authorities, not to convict; but the ICJ made clear in Belgium v. Senegal that inaction is a breach. The obligation is an obligation of means — it requires an investigation capable of leading to prosecution, and (in the Court's view) the State must first establish its jurisdiction if it has not already done so.
  3. It is a mechanism that relocates jurisdiction rather than creating universal jurisdiction. Verma's phrase is exact: "The universality principle in these cases can serve as a basis only for auxiliary competence." The State which prosecutes under aut dedere aut judicare is not asserting the universal interest of the community in the pure sense; it is performing a delegated, treaty-based function at the place where the fugitive happens to be.

F. The Treaty Basis of Universal Jurisdiction (Table 6.6)

Table 6.6 · The treaty crimes, their jurisdictional clauses and the type of obligation each creates

The structural point to state in an answer

The modern law of universal jurisdiction is best described as two circles and a bridge. The inner circle is the small class of crimes for which custom permits any State to try the offender without any link — piracy and grave breaches of the laws of war (and, on most accounts, genocide and crimes against humanity). The outer circle is the much larger class of treaty crimes for which each State has a duty triggered by the presence of the suspect. The bridge is the principle of aut dedere aut judicare, which converts the presence of a suspect into a legal obligation. Exam answers that draw this structure are almost always better received than answers that simply list conventions.

↑ top

G. Case 37 — Arrest Warrant of 11 April 2000 (Democratic Republic of the Congo v. Belgium)

Arrest Warrant of 11 April 2000 (Democratic Republic of the Congo v. Belgium)

International Court of Justice, Judgment of 14 February 2002, I.C.J. Reports 2002, p. 3 · Case 37 in the DU syllabus · Bench: President Guillaume; Vice-President Shi; Judges Oda, Ranjeva, Herczegh, Fleischhauer, Koroma, Vereshchetin, Higgins, Parra-Aranguren, Kooijmans, Rezek, Al-Khasawneh and Buergenthal; Judges ad hoc Bula-Bula (Congo) and Van den Wyngaert (Belgium); Registrar Couvreur

Provenance and a warning about the record. The DU case material prints digests of Abu Salem and Savarkar but does not print a digest of case 37; the case appears in the syllabus by citation only, and "Yerodia" occurs in the other printed materials only twice (in S.K. Verma's discussion of the universality principle). The account below is therefore drawn from the judgment itself and from the Court's official summary and operative paragraph; every proposition stated is taken from those sources. The case is treated here in full because the syllabus prescribes it by name, and because it is the single most important authority on the interaction of jurisdiction with immunity.

1. Why the case is in the syllabus

Arrest Warrant is the case that separates two questions which students (and sometimes Governments) run together. The first is whether a State has jurisdiction to try a person for a crime committed abroad; the second is whether that person enjoys immunity from the exercise of that jurisdiction. The Congo's original complaint attacked both: Belgium's claim to universal jurisdiction under its 1993 Law, and the violation of the immunity of its Minister for Foreign Affairs. In the event, the Congo's own final submissions abandoned the universal-jurisdiction argument and rested on immunity alone, and the Court — bound by the non ultra petita rule — confined its decision to immunity, expressly declining to rule in the operative part on whether Belgium's assertion of universal jurisdiction was lawful. The case therefore stands for four things: the immunity of the "troika" (Head of State, Head of Government, Minister for Foreign Affairs) before foreign national courts, even for international crimes; the distinction between jurisdiction and immunity; the proposition that immunity is not impunity; and — by its silences — the still-open question of universal jurisdiction in absentia. For head 6.3 it is the case that shows where the outer limit of universal jurisdiction lies; for head 6.4 it is the case that shows how immunity limits the extra-territorial reach of national criminal law.

2. Facts

(a) The legislation. By the Law of 16 June 1993, as amended by the Law of 19 February 1999, Belgium made punishable under its own law grave breaches of the Geneva Conventions of 1949 and of the Additional Protocols, and serious violations of international humanitarian law, including crimes against humanity. Article 7 of the Law conferred on the Belgian courts jurisdiction over such offences "wheresoever they may have been committed" — the widest statutory assertion of universal jurisdiction then in force in any country. Article 5 of the Law provided that the immunity attaching to a person's official capacity under international law would not prevent its application.

(b) The warrant. On 11 April 2000 an investigating judge of the Brussels tribunal de première instance issued an "international arrest warrant in absentia" against Mr Abdulaye Yerodia Ndombasi, charging him, as perpetrator or co-perpetrator, with offences constituting grave breaches of the Geneva Conventions and the Additional Protocols and with crimes against humanity. The alleged acts lay in speeches made by Mr Yerodia in 1998 in the Democratic Republic of the Congo which, in the view of the Belgian authorities, incited racial hatred and led to attacks on persons of Tutsi origin. At the time the warrant was issued Mr Yerodia was the Minister for Foreign Affairs of the Congo. The warrant was transmitted to the Congolese authorities and was circulated internationally through Interpol with a request that he be located and provisionally arrested with a view to extradition to Belgium. The warrant contained an exception for the case of an official visit by Mr Yerodia to Belgium, and he never in fact suffered arrest there.

(c) The proceedings. On 17 October 2000 the Congo filed an Application instituting proceedings against Belgium, relying on the Parties' declarations under Article 36(2) of the Statute (Belgium's of 17 June 1958; the Congo's of 8 February 1989), and asking the Court to declare that Belgium must annul the warrant. A request for provisional measures was rejected by Order of 8 December 2000, in which the Court nonetheless held that the issues should be determined as soon as possible. After the Application, Mr Yerodia ceased to be Minister for Foreign Affairs and then ceased to hold any ministerial office. Written pleadings were filed in 2001 (the Congo's Memorial, then Belgium's Counter-Memorial; a second round of pleadings was dispensed with by agreement) and public hearings were held from 15 to 19 October 2001.

3. Issues

  1. Did the Court have jurisdiction, or had the case become moot with Mr Yerodia's departure from office?
  2. Had the dispute been transformed into an action of diplomatic protection, so that the local-remedies rule applied?
  3. Assuming Belgium had jurisdiction to issue the warrant, did the issue and international circulation of the warrant violate the immunity from criminal jurisdiction and the inviolability which the Congo's incumbent Minister for Foreign Affairs enjoyed under international law?
  4. What were the consequences — was the Congo entitled to an order requiring Belgium to cancel the warrant?
  5. And — the question the Court did not decide — was Belgium's exercise of universal jurisdiction over offences committed abroad by a non-national against non-nationals consistent with international law?

4. Arguments of the Parties

The Democratic Republic of the Congo argued, in its Application, on two grounds: first, that the universal jurisdiction which Belgium attributed to itself under Article 7 of the Law violated "the principle that a State may not exercise its authority on the territory of another State" and the principle of sovereign equality of States in Article 2(1) of the Charter; and secondly, that Belgium's non-recognition of the immunity of a Minister for Foreign Affairs in office violated "the diplomatic immunity of the Minister for Foreign Affairs of a sovereign State". In its Memorial and at the hearing the Congo rested on the second ground alone, submitting that by issuing and internationally circulating the warrant Belgium had violated the rule of customary international law concerning the absolute inviolability and immunity from criminal process of incumbent foreign ministers; that a formal finding of unlawfulness was itself appropriate satisfaction; that the violations precluded any State (including Belgium) from executing the warrant; and that Belgium should be required to recall and cancel it and to inform the authorities to whom it had been circulated.

Belgium advanced four objections and a defence on the merits. On jurisdiction and admissibility it argued (i) that with Mr Yerodia out of office there was no longer a "legal dispute" within the meaning of the Parties' declarations; (ii) that the case had become without object; (iii) that the Congo's final submissions differed materially from the dispute as brought, so that the Court lacked jurisdiction or the application was inadmissible; and (iv) that the case had become one of diplomatic protection in which the individual had not exhausted his remedies in Belgium. It also argued, subsidiarily, that the non ultra petita rule confined the Court to the issues in the final submissions. On the merits, Belgium maintained that the mere issue of an arrest warrant does not violate the immunity of the person named until it is executed; that States are free to confer universal jurisdiction on their courts in the absence of a prohibitive rule, as Lotus showed; that its legislation had been applied without discrimination against Congolese and non-Congolese alike (the warrant in this case being one of several against Mr Pinochet, Mr Sharon, Mr Habré, Mr Castro and others); and, decisively for the shape of the case, that immunities cannot shield a person suspected of war crimes or crimes against humanity — relying on the Nuremberg and Tokyo Charters, the Statutes of the ICTY and the ICTR, Article 27 of the Rome Statute, and on the House of Lords' decision in the Pinochet litigation.

5. The Court's reasoning

(a) Jurisdiction, mootness and admissibility (paras. 22–44). The Court held that its jurisdiction must be determined as at the date on which the Application was filed, and that a State which had jurisdiction then retains it, whatever later events may do to the merits of the claim. Mr Yerodia's departure from office had not extinguished the dispute: the Congo continued to assert that the warrant was and remained unlawful and still sought its cancellation, while Belgium continued to deny any illegality. The case was therefore not moot, and Belgium's first and second objections were rejected. Nor had the dispute been transformed into an action of diplomatic protection: the Congo had never sought to invoke Mr Yerodia's personal rights, and the dispute still concerned the lawfulness of the warrant and the violation of the Congo's own rights; as the Congo was not acting in the protection of a national, the exhaustion of local remedies did not apply. Belgium's third objection failed because the facts underlying the Application had not changed in a way that transformed the dispute, and the Congo's final submissions arose directly out of the subject-matter of the Application. As to the non ultra petita point, the Court accepted that it could not rule in the operative part on the question of universal jurisdiction, but held that this did not preclude it from addressing aspects of that question in its reasoning.

(b) The basis of the immunity: custom, not the diplomatic conventions (paras. 47–55). The Court observed that international law has long recognised that certain holders of high-ranking office — Head of State, Head of Government and Minister for Foreign Affairs — enjoy immunities from jurisdiction in other States, both civil and criminal. The Vienna Convention on Diplomatic Relations, 1961, and the New York Convention on Special Missions, 1969, though cited by the Parties, contain no provision specifically defining the immunities of Ministers for Foreign Affairs; they provide "useful guidance" only. The Court therefore decided the question "on the basis of customary international law".

(c) The content of the immunity. Immunity is functional, not personal: it is accorded "not for their personal benefit, but to ensure the effective performance of their functions on behalf of their respective States". A Minister for Foreign Affairs, who is by the nature of his functions required to travel internationally, must enjoy, "throughout the duration of his or her office", full immunity from criminal jurisdiction and inviolability in other States, protecting him "against any act of authority of another State which would hinder him or her in the performance of his or her duties". The Court drew no distinction between acts performed in an "official" capacity and those claimed to be performed in a "private" capacity, nor between acts performed before the person took office and acts committed during it: to permit a prosecution in any of those cases would prevent him from exercising his functions, and "even the mere risk" of proceedings during travel could deter him from travelling at all.

(d) No exception for international crimes (the heart of the case). Belgium argued that immunities can never protect persons suspected of war crimes or crimes against humanity. The Court examined State practice — national legislation, and the few decisions of national higher courts, including the House of Lords in the Pinochet case and the French Court of Cassation — and concluded that it "has been unable to deduce from this practice that there exists under customary international law any form of exception to the rule according immunity from criminal jurisdiction and inviolability to incumbent Ministers for Foreign Affairs, where they are suspected of having committed war crimes or crimes against humanity". It reviewed the instruments creating the international criminal tribunals — Article 7 of the Charter of the International Military Tribunal at Nuremberg, Article 6 of the Tokyo Charter, Article 7(2) of the ICTY Statute, Article 6(2) of the ICTR Statute and Article 27 of the Rome Statute — and held that those provisions, directed as they are to those tribunals, do not establish an exception in customary international law before national courts. The Nuremberg and Tokyo judgments and the ICTY decisions cited by Belgium, the Court added, had not dealt with the immunity of incumbent Ministers for Foreign Affairs before national courts, and were therefore not at variance with its findings.

(e) Jurisdiction and immunity distinguished; immunity and impunity distinguished. In the passage that every answer on this case should quote, the Court held: "the rules governing the jurisdiction of national courts must be carefully distinguished from those governing jurisdictional immunities: jurisdiction does not imply absence of immunity, while absence of immunity does not imply jurisdiction." It went on to observe that although the conventions on the suppression of serious crimes impose obligations of prosecution or extradition, and thus require States to extend their criminal jurisdiction, such extension "in no way affects immunities under customary international law". The immunity, however, "does not mean that they enjoy impunity in respect of any crimes they might have committed, irrespective of their gravity". Jurisdictional immunity may bar prosecution "for a certain period or for certain offences"; it cannot exonerate the person from all criminal responsibility. The Court then listed the four circumstances in which immunity is no bar to prosecution:

  1. where the person is tried in his own country, before its own courts;
  2. where the State which he represents or represented waives the immunity;
  3. where the person, after ceasing to hold office, no longer enjoys immunity in other States in respect of acts committed prior to or subsequent to the period of office, or in respect of acts committed during that period in a private capacity (the Court did not say whether war crimes and crimes against humanity can fall into this last category — a silence that several judges criticised); and
  4. where the person is subject to criminal proceedings before certain international criminal courts which have jurisdiction (such as the ICTY, the ICTR and the ICC).

(f) Application to the facts (paras. 62–71). The Court examined the terms of the warrant and noted that it was an act of the Belgian judicial authorities intended to enable the arrest on Belgian territory of an incumbent Minister for Foreign Affairs; that it was enforceable as such ("all bailiffs and agents of public authority … to execute this arrest warrant"); and that it asserted that the position of Minister for Foreign Affairs "does not entail immunity from jurisdiction and enforcement". The fact that the warrant excepted official visits to Belgium, and that Mr Yerodia was never arrested in Belgium, did not save it: "given the nature and purpose of the warrant, its mere issue violated the immunity which Mr Yerodia enjoyed". The international circulation of the warrant from June 2000 — whose admitted purpose, Belgium accepted, was "to establish a legal basis for the arrest of Mr. Yerodia … abroad and his subsequent extradition to Belgium" — likewise "effectively infringed Mr. Yerodia's immunity … and was furthermore liable to affect the Congo's conduct of its international relations"; it was no answer that Mr Yerodia had not been arrested or that his diplomatic activity had not been significantly interfered with.

6. Decision (paragraph 78 of the Judgment)

  • By fifteen votes to one — the Court rejected Belgium's objections relating to jurisdiction, mootness and admissibility; found that it had jurisdiction to entertain the Application filed on 17 October 2000; found that the Application was not without object and that the case was not moot; and found the Application admissible. (Judge Oda dissented on all four.)
  • By thirteen votes to three — the Court found that the issue against Mr Yerodia of the arrest warrant of 11 April 2000, and its international circulation, "constituted violations of a legal obligation of the Kingdom of Belgium towards the Democratic Republic of the Congo, in that they failed to respect the immunity from criminal jurisdiction and the inviolability which the incumbent Minister for Foreign Affairs of the Democratic Republic of the Congo enjoyed under international law". (Judges Oda and Al-Khasawneh and Judge ad hoc Van den Wyngaert dissented.)
  • By ten votes to six — the Court found that Belgium "must, by means of its own choosing, cancel the arrest warrant of 11 April 2000 and so inform the authorities to whom that warrant was circulated".

Belgium complied: the warrant was cancelled and the authorities concerned were informed. Belgium also amended its legislation in 2003, narrowing the reach of universal jurisdiction in Belgian law — a legislative response which is itself part of the history of the case.

7. The separate and dissenting opinions

  • President Guillaume (separate opinion) doubted whether customary international law permits the exercise of universal jurisdiction in the wide sense Belgium had claimed, and criticised the breadth of the 1993 Law.
  • Judges Higgins, Kooijmans and Buergenthal (joint separate opinion) examined the various bases of jurisdiction and the arguments for and against universal jurisdiction, and took the view that its exercise in absentia — without the presence of the accused — is, at the least, a matter of serious doubt; they would not have ordered the cancellation of the warrant.
  • Judge Al-Khasawneh (dissenting) argued that the Court should have addressed the applicants' original jurisdiction claim, and that immunity cannot be considered in isolation from the jurisdiction whose exercise it presupposes.
  • Judge ad hoc Van den Wyngaert (dissenting) delivered the opinion which is most often cited in the literature. She regretted that the Court, unlike the House of Lords in Pinochet, had not qualified the category of acts which a former Minister might commit in a "private capacity"; submitted that for some crimes under international law — acts of genocide, for instance, which can only be committed with the means of a State — immunity should never apply, before either international or national courts; observed that the judgment "dramatically increases the number of persons that enjoy international immunity from jurisdiction", and that a bad-faith government might appoint persons to cabinet posts in order to shelter them from prosecution; and concluded that the Court, "in its effort to close one box of Pandora for fear of chaos and abuse, may have opened another one: that of granting immunity and thus de facto impunity to an increasing number of government officials".

8. The principle evolved

  1. The troika immunity. Incumbent Heads of State, Heads of Government and Ministers for Foreign Affairs enjoy full immunity from the criminal jurisdiction of other States' courts, and inviolability, while in office — and, on the reasoning adopted, regardless of the gravity of the alleged crime and whether the acts are characterised as official or private. The rationale is functional: unimpeded performance of the office, in the interest of the State represented and of the international system.
  2. Jurisdiction ≠ immunity. A State may have jurisdiction on any recognised basis and still be unable to exercise it against a particular person; conversely, the absence of an immunity does not create a jurisdiction that does not otherwise exist. The two questions must be argued separately — which is exactly the point the Court makes for a candidate answering head 6.4.
  3. Immunity ≠ impunity. The four situations listed by the Court are the standard list of escape routes: prosecution in the home State; waiver; prosecution after the end of office (with the unresolved question about during-office acts of a private character); and prosecution before an international criminal court.
  4. The question of universal jurisdiction remains open. Because the Congo abandoned the point in its final submissions, the Court said nothing in the operative part about the legality of Belgium's universal jurisdiction, and expressly noted that it was confined by the non ultra petita rule. The case is therefore authority on immunity, and only an obiter commentary on jurisdiction.
  5. The relationship with the later case law. The Court's approach was carried into Certain Questions of Mutual Assistance in Criminal Matters (Djibouti v. France), I.C.J. Reports 2008, p. 177, and into Jurisdictional Immunities of the State (Germany v. Italy: Greece intervening), I.C.J. Reports 2012, p. 99, in which the Court held that a State's immunity from the civil jurisdiction of foreign courts is not displaced even where the claim alleges grave violations of international humanitarian law — the civil counterpart of the Arrest Warrant rule. Together the two cases draw the line at which the assertion of jurisdiction by national courts stops.
non ultra petita rule confined the Court's operative findings to immunity.

Fig. 6.5 · The two-step structure of Arrest Warrant. An answer which separates jurisdiction from immunity, and then applies the four "escape routes", covers everything the examiner can ask on case 37.

The five ways case 37 is examined
  1. "Discuss the Arrest Warrant case." Facts → issues → arguments → holding (with the votes) → principle (troika immunity; jurisdiction ≠ immunity; immunity ≠ impunity) → the undecided universal-jurisdiction point.
  2. "Does immunity bar prosecution for international crimes?" Answer with the majority's finding of no exception before national courts; the four escape routes; the criticism of Judges Higgins, Kooijmans, Buergenthal and Van den Wyngaert; and the subsequent confirmation in Germany v. Italy (2012) on the civil side.
  3. "Distinguish jurisdiction from immunity." Quote the sentence from the judgment; illustrate with Enrica Lexie (jurisdiction existed; immunity nevertheless displaced it — Topic 5, case 34) and with Pinochet (no immunity ratione materiae for torture, but the forum State's law and the extradition framework still had to be satisfied).
  4. "What did the Court decide about universal jurisdiction?" Nothing in the operative part; the non ultra petita rule; the Congo's withdrawal of the point; the separate opinions as evidence of the Court's divisions.
  5. "Is the decision consistent with the fight against impunity?" Present both sides: the functional rationale and the systemic interest in stable inter-State relations; against that, the dissent's warning about "de facto impunity", the possibility of cynical appointments by bad-faith governments, and the practical alternative (prosecution after the end of office, before international courts, or in the home State).
