H. Eichmann and Pinochet — the Two Poles of Universal Jurisdiction

The two great national decisions on universal jurisdiction stand at opposite ends of the spectrum: Eichmann is the case in which a newly founded State claimed the right to try a foreigner for crimes committed abroad against persons who were not its citizens, and Pinochet is the case in which the House of Lords held that a former Head of State had no immunity in respect of an international crime committed more than a decade earlier and thousands of miles away. The first is the high-water mark of the principle; the second is the case in which it was narrowed to a treaty-based, act-specific foundation.

Attorney-General of the Government of Israel v. Adolf Eichmann

District Court of Jerusalem, 36 I.L.R. 5 (1961); Supreme Court of Israel (Criminal Appeal No. 336/61), 36 I.L.R. 277 (1962) · Verma, Chapter 7; and Question 21(4) of the question bank printed with the course material

Provenance. The passage quoted below is the one reproduced by Verma from the District Court's judgment, and the facts are taken from the judgment as summarised in the case literature. The abduction therefore belongs to the same story as Savarkar (case 39): Verma's footnote on Eichmann cites the Permanent Court of Arbitration's Savarkar award precisely on the question of "illegally obtained custody".

1. Facts

(a) The man. Adolf Eichmann was an officer of the German SS who, as head of the Jewish Department of the Gestapo, was responsible for the organisation of the deportation of millions of Jews from German-occupied Europe to the extermination camps. After the Second World War he escaped Allied custody, fled to Argentina and lived there under the assumed name Ricardo Klement.

(b) The capture. In 1960 Israeli agents located and abducted him in Buenos Aires, and he was brought secretly to Israel. Argentina protested, and the Security Council, in Resolution 138 (1960) of 23 June 1960, held that the action had infringed Argentine sovereignty and requested Israel to make appropriate reparation; Israel and Argentina subsequently settled the matter by a joint declaration recording that the incident had been an irregular act for which Israel expressed regret. Eichmann was tried in Jerusalem under the Nazis and Nazi Collaborators (Punishment) Law, 1951.

2. Issues

  1. Did Israel have jurisdiction over offences committed in Europe, before the State of Israel existed, by a German national, against persons who were not Israeli citizens?
  2. Did the illegality of his capture deprive the Israeli courts of jurisdiction — the rule of male capto bene judicato (he who has been badly captured may nevertheless be well judged)?
  3. Was the 1951 Law an impermissible retrospective criminal statute?
  4. Could he shelter behind superior orders or the official character of his acts?

3. Arguments

The defence contended that the crimes had been committed outside Israeli territory, that a State may not try crimes committed by aliens abroad unless there is a jurisdictional link, that the abduction was an act of State lawlessness which tainted the entire proceeding, and that the punishment of acts committed before the 1951 Law was made (and before the State existed) violated the principle of legality. The prosecution relied on the universal character of the crimes — crimes against the Jewish people, crimes against humanity and war crimes — on the protective interest of the State of Israel as the State of the victims, and on the practice of the international military tribunals at Nuremberg and Tokyo (where jurisdiction had been exercised over acts committed in many countries and by nationals of a defeated State).

4. Decision and the principle evolved

The District Court and, on appeal, the Supreme Court of Israel upheld the conviction; Eichmann was sentenced to death on 15 December 1961, the appeal was dismissed on 29 May 1962, and the sentence was carried out on 31 May 1962. On jurisdiction the Court reasoned in the passage reproduced in Verma: "The State of Israel's 'right to punish' the accused derives … from two cumulative sources: a universal source … which vests the right to prosecute and punish crimes of this order in every State within the family of nations; and a specific or national source, which gives the victim nation the right to try any who assault its existence." The Court rejected the argument that the unlawfulness of the capture defeated jurisdiction, holding that the question of how the accused came to be within the jurisdiction concerned relations between States and not the competence of the court to try him; and it rejected the retroactivity objection on the footing that the acts were crimes under international law at the time and that the 1951 Law merely provided a forum for their punishment.

Four principles emerge, and each should be stated in an answer:

  1. Universal jurisdiction over crimes against humanity and war crimes is firmly established. Eichmann is the leading modern authority, alongside the Nuremberg principles, for the proposition that the horrors of total war created a class of crimes which every State may punish.
  2. The "victim nation" theory. The Israeli court combined the universal source with a national one — the link of the victims to the prosecuting State. It is because of that combination that Eichmann is often described as an imperfect example of pure universal jurisdiction: the State had its own interest, so the case did not have to rest on universality alone.
  3. Irregular capture does not defeat jurisdiction — but it creates responsibility. The Israeli courts' approach has become the general practice (see also U.S. v. Yunis and U.S. v. Alvarez-Machain); the Security Council's Resolution 138 shows the other half of the coin: the abducting State commits an internationally wrongful act and owes reparation even though its courts may try the accused. This is exactly the structure of the Savarkar award — no duty to restore, but an "irregularity" on the record.
  4. Legality and retrospectivity. The court's answer — that the conduct was already criminal under international law — is the seed of the modern doctrine of individual criminal responsibility and of Article 15 of the ICC Statute.

R. v. Bow Street Metropolitan Stipendiary Magistrate, ex parte Pinochet Ugarte (No. 3)

[2000] 1 A.C. 147 (House of Lords) · with Pinochet (No. 1) [2000] 1 A.C. 61 (25 November 1998) and Pinochet (No. 2) [2000] 1 A.C. 119 (15 January 1999) · Discussed in the present judgment in Arrest Warrant and in S.K. Verma's footnote on torture (Ch. 7)

Provenance. The account follows the reported speeches and the standard case literature; the date of 8 December 1988 is the date on which section 134 of the Criminal Justice Act, 1988 came into force in the United Kingdom, and it is with reference to acts after that date that the majority in Pinochet (No. 3) held immunity to be displaced.

1. Facts

Senator Augusto Pinochet Ugarte, who had been President of Chile from 1973 to 1990 and thereafter remained head of the army until 1998, came to the United Kingdom in September 1998 for medical treatment. A Spanish investigating judge had been proceeding against him, and Spain sought his extradition for the alleged torture, hostage-taking, murder and genocide of Spanish and Chilean victims during his period in power. On a provisional warrant issued by the Bow Street Metropolitan Stipendiary Magistrate he was arrested in London. He claimed immunity as a former Head of State.

The first appeal reached the House of Lords in Pinochet (No. 1) (November 1998), where a majority of three to two held that he did not enjoy immunity in respect of crimes against humanity and torture. That decision was set aside and a fresh hearing ordered in Pinochet (No. 2) (January 1999) because one of the majority Law Lords, Lord Hoffmann, had a connection with Amnesty International, which had been given leave to intervene — a decision of the highest importance for the principle that justice must not only be done but be seen to be done. In Pinochet (No. 3) (24 March 1999), a differently constituted committee of seven Law Lords held, by six to one, that Senator Pinochet was not entitled to immunity in respect of acts of torture committed after 8 December 1988, when the Criminal Justice Act, 1988 (section 134) came into force in the United Kingdom, giving effect to the Torture Convention of 1984 and creating an offence of torture triable in the English courts whatever the nationality of the offender and wherever the act was committed.

2. Issues

  1. Does a former Head of State enjoy immunity ratione materiae from the criminal jurisdiction of the English courts in respect of acts done in the exercise of his official functions?
  2. Is that immunity displaced where the acts alleged are international crimes, in particular torture?
  3. If so, for acts committed at what time — before or after the Torture Convention was implemented in the law of the forum State?

3. Arguments

Senator Pinochet argued that a former Head of State retains immunity ratione materiae in respect of acts performed in an official capacity; that torture, however cruel, was an act done by State officials in the exercise of State authority; and that to allow the English courts to try him would be to sit in judgment on the acts of a foreign State. The Crown (acting for Spain) argued that the immunity which the rule of international law recognises is immunity in respect of acts which international law leaves to the State; and that acts prohibited by international criminal law — torture, hostage-taking, crimes against humanity — cannot be "official functions" for this purpose, since no State has the power to authorise them. Support for the Crown's argument came from the Torture Convention's prosecution/extradition obligation and from the practice of the Pinochet litigation in other States.

4. Decision and the principle evolved

The majority held, by six to one, that the immunity of a former Head of State did not bar the extradition proceedings in respect of conduct amounting to torture committed after the date on which the Torture Convention was implemented in English law (8 December 1988); the extradition could not proceed in respect of earlier conduct. The reasoning differed from speech to speech — Lord Browne-Wilkinson, Lord Hope, Lord Hutton, Lord Saville, Lord Phillips and Lord Millett in the majority; Lord Goff of Chieveley dissenting — but the common thread was that the international community's prohibition of torture, combined with the Convention's requirement that States establish jurisdiction and prosecute or extradite, meant that acts of torture could not be treated as the official functions of a State. The Home Secretary then authorised the extradition proceedings to continue in respect of the post-1988 allegations; but Senator Pinochet was found to be medically unfit to stand trial, and he returned to Chile in March 2000, where the Chilean courts stripped him of immunity and prosecuted him until his death in 2006.

Three principles are to be taken from the case:

  1. Immunity ratione materiae is not absolute for international crimes — but the exception is narrowly drawn. On the reasoning of the majority the displacement of immunity depended on the specific treaty obligation and on its implementation in the law of the forum; a general claim that international crimes never attract immunity did not command the House.
  2. Universal jurisdiction, extradition law and the presence of the accused must all be aligned. The case shows the practical shape of universal jurisdiction: an offence made triable in the forum by statute, an extradition request by a third State, and the physical presence of the accused. Take away any one element and the machinery stops.
  3. The interface with Arrest Warrant. The ICJ in Arrest Warrant considered Pinochet and the decisions of national higher courts and held that they established no exception to the immunity of an incumbent Minister for Foreign Affairs; the House of Lords had been dealing with a former Head of State and with conduct covered by an express statutory scheme. The two cases are therefore not in conflict: one marks the limit of immunity ratione personae, the other the possible limit of immunity ratione materiae.
ratione materiae The treaty: Torture Convention 1984 — jurisdiction + prosecute or extradite (Arts. 5 and 7) The statute: Criminal Justice Act 1988, s. 134 — in force 8 December 1988 Held (6:1): no immunity for torture after 8 December 1988; extradition could not proceed for earlier acts Practical sequel: found unfit to stand trial; returned to Chile (2000); prosecuted there until his death in 2006 The narrow exception: immunity is displaced only where an express treaty obligation has been implemented in the forum's law, and only for conduct after the date of implementation.

Fig. 6.6 · The two poles of universal jurisdiction. Read them together with case 37: Arrest Warrant is the rule for the incumbent; Pinochet is the arguable exception for the former official; Eichmann is the practice of trying him in the receiving State whatever the manner of his arrival.

Other authorities worth one line each

U.S. v. Yunis (No. 2), 924 F.2d 1086 (D.C. Cir. 1991) — the United States prosecuted Fawaz Yunis, a Lebanese national, for the 1985 hijacking of a Jordanian airliner at Beirut on which United States nationals were passengers, and for hostage-taking. He had been lured onto a yacht in international waters and arrested by FBI agents. The court upheld jurisdiction, relying on the Hostages Convention of 1979 (whose Article 5(1)(d) covers the victim's nationality and Article 8 requires prosecution or extradition) and on the passive personality principle, and held that the manner of obtaining custody did not deprive the court of jurisdiction. The case is the standard modern illustration of both aut dedere aut judicare and of the passive personality principle.

U.S. v. Alvarez-Machain, 504 U.S. 655 (1992) — a Mexican national was abducted from Mexico to face trial in the United States for the murder of a DEA agent. The Supreme Court held that the extradition treaty between the two States did not prohibit the abduction and that the court's jurisdiction was not defeated (the Ker–Frisbie doctrine, i.e. male capto bene judicato). The decision provoked strong protest and a change of treaty practice, and it is the modern American counterpart of the Savarkar problem — the echo of case 39 in contemporary practice.

German practice. Section 1 of the Code of Crimes against International Law (Völkerstrafgesetzbuch), 2002, confers jurisdiction over genocide, crimes against humanity and war crimes even when committed abroad by foreigners against foreigners; the resulting prosecutions in Germany — including the conviction, in January 2022, of a former Syrian intelligence officer for crimes against humanity committed in Syria — are now among the most-cited examples of universal jurisdiction being exercised in practice, and they show that the doctrine is live even in States which do not prosecute in absentia.

I. The Debate — Abuse, Order and the African Union

Universal jurisdiction is the most politically contested doctrine in this topic, because it is the point at which the criminal law of one State passes judgment on the officials of another. Four criticisms are made, and each has a standard answer:

  1. It invites political prosecutions. A State may issue warrants or open investigations against the leaders of States with which it has political disputes, and the warrant itself — even if it never leads to a trial — may paralyse the targeted government. Answer: the remedy is not to abolish the jurisdiction but to insist on the safeguards which the doctrine itself contains: a genuine criminal-law basis (not a political one), an independent prosecutor and judge, the principle of subsidiarity (the State with the stronger link should ordinarily act first), and the requirement of the presence of the accused, favoured by the joint separate opinion in Arrest Warrant and by the Institut de Droit International's 2005 Kraków Resolution, which took the view that universal criminal jurisdiction presupposes the presence of the alleged offender in the territory of the prosecuting State.
  2. It ignores sovereignty and the immunity of officials. Answer: immunities are preserved by Arrest Warrant for the incumbent troika, and the exceptional displacement of immunity ratione materiae after Pinochet is narrow and treaty-based. Jurisdiction is not the same thing as immunity.
  3. It creates conflicts and forum-shopping. Several States may claim jurisdiction at once (passive personality, presence, universal) and the accused may find himself the object of competing requests. Answer: the treaty clauses themselves supply the answer — the State which has custody decides whether to prosecute or extradite, and extradition law can resolve competing requests by reference to the seriousness of the offence, the place of the crime and the nationality of the accused.
  4. It is applied selectively. The criticism most sharply pressed by the African Union after indictments of African leaders in European courts. In 2008 the AU Assembly adopted a decision requesting the Commission and the Member States to examine the question of the abuse of universal jurisdiction and to ensure that indictments of African leaders do not undermine the sovereignty of African States; the AU–EU Technical Ad Hoc Expert Group established to consider the matter reported in 2009, and its recommendations — that universal jurisdiction be exercised in a manner consistent with international law including immunities, that it be exercised only where the accused is present and by the State with the strongest link, and that it be exercised only by a State able and willing to try the accused with due process guarantees — have since been the basis of the AU's position. The response of the other side is that the AU's criticism was directed at national courts while the same States support the International Criminal Court, and that the correct remedy for the perceived hypocrisy is universality in practice — including prosecutions in the States of the region concerned (as the Habré trial in Senegal and the Malabo Protocol's International Criminal Law Section, not yet in force, were meant to demonstrate).

Two structural developments should be noted as part of the same debate. First, several States narrowed their statutes in the 2000s: Belgium amended its 1993 Law in 2003 (introducing a link requirement and other restrictions), Spain reformed Article 23 of the Organic Law on the Judiciary in 2009 and again in 2014, and the United Kingdom and others rely on treaty-based offences rather than on general universality. Secondly, the International Criminal Court has absorbed much of the political demand for universality, with the consequence that the national doctrine is now invoked chiefly where the Court's jurisdiction is unavailable — because a State is not a party to the Statute, or because the Security Council is deadlocked.

J. India and Universal Jurisdiction

  • No general statute. India has no law conferring universal criminal jurisdiction in the wide form; there is no Indian equivalent of the Belgian Law of 1993 or of section 1 of the German Code of Crimes against International Law.
  • Sectoral legislation. Universal or treaty-based jurisdiction exists where India has implemented a convention: the Geneva Conventions Act, 1960 (grave breaches, with provision for trial in India of acts committed outside India); the Anti-Hijacking Act, 2016 and the SUA Act, 2002 (with the aut dedere aut judicare machinery); the Maritime Anti-Piracy Act, 2022 (piracy under UNCLOS Articles 100 to 107, whose first convictions under the Act were recorded in July 2026, in the Ruen and Al-Kambar cases); and the provisions of the Unlawful Activities (Prevention) Act, 1967 that reach offences abroad.
  • The general penal law. The ordinary route for offences abroad remains IPC sections 3 and 4 with section 188 of the Code of Criminal Procedure — that is, the territorial and nationality bases, not universality.
  • India's position in the debates. India is not a party to the Rome Statute and has not accepted the ICC's jurisdiction; it has consistently supported international cooperation against terrorism, including the "prosecute or extradite" obligations of the terrorist-bombing, hostage-taking and maritime conventions, while resisting the exercise of criminal jurisdiction over Indian officials by the courts of other States. In the Sixth Committee of the General Assembly India has taken the position that universal jurisdiction must be exercised within the framework of international law — in good faith, only for the crimes for which States have accepted the obligation, and subject to the immunities which international law confers.
  • The practical lesson for an Indian answer. The Indian material shows the two-way character of the doctrine: India invokes treaty-based universality to prosecute pirates and maritime terrorists, and India invokes the law of immunity and the territorial principle to resist the exercise of jurisdiction over its own officials abroad. Both positions are consistent, because the treaty-based and the immunity-based rules are different rules.

K. Examination Angle for 6.3

Model skeleton for the standard questions on 6.3

"What is universal jurisdiction? Discuss with the help of decided cases." (i) Definition — no link required; the rationale (crimes offensive to humanity; no safe haven). (ii) The two forms: pure (piracy, war crimes) and treaty-based (aut dedere aut judicare). (iii) The crimes: piracy (UNCLOS Arts. 100–107); grave breaches of the Geneva Conventions; genocide; crimes against humanity; torture; apartheid; hijacking; terrorism. (iv) The cases: Eichmann (universal + victim nation; irregular capture no bar); Arrest Warrant (universal jurisdiction undecided; immunity decided); Pinochet (no immunity for torture after the implementing statute); Yunis (hostage-taking, passive personality, abduction no bar); Belgium v. Senegal (the duty to prosecute or extradite actually enforced — Habré convicted). (v) The limits: immunity; the presence of the accused; subsidiarity; the abuse debate. (vi) India: sectoral statutes; the absence of a general statute; the MAPA 2022 convictions of 2026.

"Discuss the Arrest Warrant case." Use the eight headings of the case block in §6.3 G — and do not forget the votes, the four "escape routes" in paragraph 61, and the point that the Court did not decide universal jurisdiction.

"Is universal jurisdiction a threat to the sovereignty of States?" Take both sides: the sovereignty/immunity/abuse critique (the AU, the narrowing statutes, the fear of politically motivated warrants) and the answer (immunities are preserved; the crimes are a closed list; presence and subsidiarity; the alternative is impunity; and the ICC absorbs much of the demand). Conclude with the modern synthesis: universal jurisdiction has moved from a claim of last resort in the 1960s to a treaty-anchored, presence-based doctrine in the 2020s — narrower than Eichmann, wider than Lotus.

Note also: the question bank asks for "the facts and decision in the Eichmann case" and "the Lotus case" — two questions that are answered by digesting the case blocks in §6.1 G and §6.3 H respectively, and any answer that quotes the District Court's "two cumulative sources" passage and the PCIJ's "restrictions upon the independence of States cannot be presumed" passage will score well.

6.4Extra-territorial Jurisdiction of States — Civil and Criminal

The teacher's red-ink note, and what it requires

On the syllabus image the words Civil and criminal are written in red, with arrows, beside head 6.4 ("Extra-territorial Jurisdiction of State"). The instruction implicit in the annotation is that extra-territoriality must be prepared on both sides: the criminal side — the reach of penal law to conduct abroad, and the means by which an offender outside the territory becomes triable within it; and the civil side — the power of a State's courts to adjudicate disputes with a foreign element, the recognition and enforcement of foreign judgments, and the limits which immunity places upon both. The class notes take the same view, and cite K.T.M.S. Abdul Cader for the proposition that the expression "extra-territorial operation" is used in two senses in Indian constitutional practice. This section follows that structure: first the concept and the two powers, then the criminal doctrines, then the civil rules, then the management of conflict between States.

A. What "Extra-territorial" Means

The expression is used in three different ways, and an examination answer should say which one it is using.

  1. Extra-territorial operation of a law — a statute which, by its own terms, governs conduct, persons or things outside the territory of the enacting State. The class notes quote the Madras High Court in K.T.M.S. Abdul Cader v. Union of India, AIR 1977 Mad. 386, for the two senses in which the expression is normally used: "firstly, laws in respect of acts and events which take place inside the State but have operation outside, and secondly, laws with reference to the nationals of a State in respect of their acts outside." To these must be added the two modern statutory techniques: laws which attach to conduct abroad on the basis of effects within the State (Competition Act, s. 32; IT Act, s. 75; Income-tax Act, s. 9), and laws which implement treaty obligations to try or extradite (the Anti-Hijacking Act, 2016; the SUA Act, 2002; the MAPA 2022).
  2. Extra-territorial jurisdiction over persons or property — the competence of a State's courts or executive to deal with persons, acts and property situated outside its territory. This is the sense in which the phrase is used in the present head of the syllabus.
  3. Extra-territorial enforcement — the actual exercise of State authority abroad: arrest, seizure, service of process, investigation, execution of judgments. This is the sense in which the phrase most often appears in disputes, because it is here that international law imposes its clearest prohibition.

The constitutional question is settled in India by Article 245(2) of the Constitution, which provides that no law made by Parliament shall be deemed invalid on the ground that it would have extra-territorial operation. The question of international law is not settled by that provision at all: a statute may be valid under the Constitution and still amount to a breach of international law if the jurisdiction it claims is not one that international law recognises, or if its enforcement abroad violates the sovereignty of another State.

B. The Prescribe/Enforce Divide Restated

Everything in this section follows from two propositions of the Lotus judgment:

"…the first and foremost restriction imposed by international law upon a State is that — failing the existence of a permissive rule to the contrary — it may not exercise its power in any form in the territory of another State. In this sense jurisdiction is certainly territorial…

It does not, however, follow that international law prohibits a State from exercising jurisdiction in its own territory, in respect of any case which relates to acts which have taken place abroad… Far from laying down a general prohibition…, it leaves them in this respect a wide measure of discretion, which is only limited in certain cases by prohibitive rules…"

The first proposition is the rule of enforcement jurisdiction: it is a prohibition, and it is not displaced merely because the State has a good prescriptive title. The second is the rule of prescriptive jurisdiction: it is a freedom, qualified by prohibitive rules (immunity, the treaty clauses, the rule on collisions which became Article 97 of UNCLOS, and, on the modern view, the requirement of a genuine link). Three practical consequences follow, and they should be carried through the whole of this section:

  • A State may not enforce abroad through its own officials. Police officers, tax inspectors, competition investigators and process-servers may not operate in another State; nor may a State seize property there. Hence the machinery of mutual legal assistance, letters rogatory (s. 166A of the Code of Criminal Procedure, 1973 and its successor provisions), the service of foreign process through diplomatic channels (Order V Rule 25–26 of the Code of Civil Procedure), and the treaty networks for the recognition of judgments and arbitral awards. Where a State disregards the prohibition — as in abductions — it commits an internationally wrongful act and owes reparation, even though its courts may then be competent to try the accused (Eichmann, Yunis, Alvarez-Machain, and Savarkar, case 39).
  • Prescription abroad is effective only through custody. A prescriptive claim is a claim about what law applies; a prosecution, however, requires the person. The whole of head 6.5 — extradition, deportation, rendition, asylum — is the machinery by which presence is obtained or refused. That is the link between the two halves of this topic.
  • Conflict between two prescriptive claims is resolved by the rules of cooperation and restraint, not by hierarchy. Concurrent jurisdiction is lawful; what is unlawful is the enforcement of one State's claim within another State's territory without consent. The two States must therefore negotiate, extradite, cooperate or tolerate; section H below sets out the techniques they use.

C. Criminal Extra-territoriality — the Doctrines

The criminal side of extra-territoriality is the sum of the five bases (Table 6.1), applied to a particular statute. The following propositions are the ones which answers need:

  1. Start with an express provision. Indian law does not apply its penal law abroad by inference: it does so by statute (IPC ss. 3 and 4; the sectoral Acts). A court asked to apply an Indian penal provision to conduct abroad will look for the express words, and will in a doubtful case lean against extra-territorial application — the presumption being that Parliament does not legislate for the whole world.
  2. Identify the basis. For each statute, ask which of the five bases it embodies. IPC s. 4(1) is nationality; s. 4(2) is the flag/registration basis (a form of territoriality); s. 4(3) is effects; the Competition Act is effects; the Anti-Hijacking Act is treaty/universal; the MAPA 2022 is universal.
  3. Distinguish conduct from consequence. For objective territoriality and the effects doctrine the place of the effect matters; for nationality and universality it does not.
  4. Watch the immunities. Even a valid extra-territorial prescription cannot be exercised against a person who enjoys immunity (case 37) — jurisdiction and immunity are separate questions. The same is true of the inviolability of diplomatic premises and archives (Topic 7).
  5. Watch the procedural gate. In India, section 188 of the Code of Criminal Procedure requires the sanction of the Central Government before any offence committed outside India may be inquired into or tried; without the sanction, the prosecution is barred (and the sanction is the executive's instrument of policy, including comity).
  6. Watch the double-criminality and double-jeopardy questions. A State may punish its national for conduct abroad only where it is satisfied that the conduct was criminal in substance; and international law leaves it to each State to decide whether a foreign acquittal or conviction bars a second prosecution. The ICCPR's Article 14(7) bars double jeopardy within the same legal order (the Human Rights Committee's decision in A.P. v. Italy, Communication No. 204/1986, is the authority), not between different States; Indian courts generally treat the bar in section 300 of the Code of Criminal Procedure as applying to a previous trial by an Indian court, so that a foreign acquittal does not by itself bar prosecution in India unless a statute or treaty provides otherwise. The remedy against abuse is the rule of ne bis in idem as a factor in extradition law (a State may refuse extradition where the fugitive has already been tried for the same offence — Verma cites section 31 of the Extradition Act for the time-bar, and the refusal of extradition in respect of a person already tried and convicted in the requested State).

D. Criminal Extra-territoriality in Indian Law

Three clusters of provisions do the work, and the real examples belong to them:

  • The penal law itself: IPC ss. 3 and 4 with s. 188 CrPC (now under the Bharatiya Nyaya Sanhita, 2023 and the Bharatiya Nagarik Suraksha Sanhita, 2023). The scheme is nationality-based, with two statutory extensions — Indian ships and aircraft; and offences abroad targeting a computer resource in India (the 2008 amendment, which is an effects provision).
  • The economic and regulatory statutes: the Income-tax Act, 1961 (s. 9 and its Explanations; the Vodafone litigation); the Competition Act, 2002 (s. 32, the effects doctrine); the Information Technology Act, 2000 (s. 75); the Prevention of Money-Laundering Act, 2002 (which reaches the proceeds of crime wherever situated, and enables attachment of property abroad through the mechanism of mutual assistance); the Foreign Exchange Management Act, 1999 and the securities laws (which apply to persons resident in India and to branches and agencies abroad owned or controlled by them).
  • The security statutes: the Unlawful Activities (Prevention) Act, 1967, the Official Secrets Act, 1923, the Atomic Energy Act, 1962, the NDPS Act, 1985 — the protective principle in statutory form — and the maritime and aviation statutes which implement treaty jurisdiction.
Real examples — criminal extra-territoriality in action

The Vodafone controversy and the cost of an untenable claim. (i) In 2007 a Netherlands company, Vodafone International Holdings B.V., bought the entire share capital of a Cayman Islands company whose downstream subsidiaries held Indian telecommunications assets. (ii) The Income-tax Department issued a demand for capital-gains tax, treating the transfer of the Indian assets as a transfer subject to Indian tax, and the Supreme Court in Vodafone International Holdings B.V. v. Union of India, (2012) 6 SCC 613, held that the transaction, as the law then stood, was not taxable in India: the "look at" approach adopted by the Department would amount to an extra-territorial application of the Act and would defeat the separate legal personality of the foreign companies. (iii) Parliament then amended the Act with retrospective effect from 1962, and, after international arbitration proceedings and diplomatic friction, the Taxation Laws (Amendment) Act, 2021 withdrew the retrospective charge and provided for the refund of amounts already collected. (iv) The episode is the best Indian illustration of the principle that an extra-territorial prescription which the other State does not accept produces not revenue but conflict — and of the fact that bilateral investment treaties can convert a jurisdictional overreach into international responsibility.

Counterfeit currency and terror finance. Prosecutions under the Unlawful Activities (Prevention) Act, 1967 and IPC sections 489A to 489E for circulating fake Indian currency notes printed abroad, and the attachment under the Prevention of Money-Laundering Act, 2002 of the foreign assets of persons accused of financing terrorism, show the protective principle at work in Indian practice: the conduct and the accused are abroad, and the interest injured is India's currency and security.

The Enrica Lexie sequence. India's assertion of criminal jurisdiction over the Italian marines, the Supreme Court's judgment of 2013 upholding it, the ITLOS order of 2015 and the Annex VII award of 2020 which ended in immunity rather than in the absence of jurisdiction, are treated in the Topic-5 notes (§5.1.4, case 34) and summarised at §6.1 G above. The sequence is the clearest modern demonstration that jurisdiction and immunity are two different arguments, and that a State may win the first and lose the second.

E. Civil Extra-territoriality — the Private International Law Framing

On the civil side, "extra-territoriality" appears not as a claim of public law but as an ordinary incident of litigation: any dispute with a foreign element requires the court to answer three questions, and each of them has its own rules:

  1. Jurisdiction — is this court competent to hear this dispute? The question may turn on the defendant's residence or business in the forum, on the place where the cause of action arose, on the defendant's submission, on a contractual choice of court, or on a statutory assignment of jurisdiction (for example the special statutes conferring admiralty jurisdiction in India under the Admiralty (Jurisdiction and Settlement of Maritime Claims) Act, 2017, or the substantive Acts on insurance, carriage of goods by sea and merchant shipping).
  2. Choice of law — which law governs the merits? India has no general statute of private international law; the courts apply the common-law rules, subject to the express provisions of statutes (for example the Indian Contract Act's treatment of foreign contracts, the Sale of Goods Act and the Carriage of Goods by Sea Act, 1925) and of treaties (the Hague Rules, the Warsaw/Montreal régime for carriage by air).
  3. Recognition and enforcement — will a judgment given elsewhere be recognised or executed here, and will an Indian judgment be recognised or executed abroad? This is the stage at which the sovereignty of the other State reasserts itself, because the enforcement of a judgment is an exercise of the forum's own coercive power (Lotus, first proposition).

Verma's general proposition belongs here and should be quoted: "In matters of civil jurisdiction, the municipal courts apply private international law in those cases where a foreign element is involved. But the courts in normal circumstances are reluctant to exercise jurisdiction unless there is a 'substantial connection' between the foreign element and the forum either by allegiance or by domicile … The exercise of jurisdiction without such a connection may be an ultra vires act, which could lead to international responsibility of the State." The Supreme Court has said the same thing in its own idiom: in Surinder Kaur Sandhu v. Harbax Singh Sandhu, AIR 1984 SC 1224, the Court observed that the modern theory of conflict of laws prefers the jurisdiction of the State which has "the most intimate contact with the issues of the case" — a proposition the Court applied in a custody dispute with an English element.

Two limits on the civil side come from public rather than private law and are treated in Topic 7: State immunity (a foreign State cannot be sued in India without the consent of the Central Government: Code of Civil Procedure, ss. 86 and 87) and immunity from execution (even where jurisdiction exists, the property of a foreign State may not be attached or executed against except in the cases the law allows). The Kuwait Airways litigation, which has been before the courts in this jurisdiction in the current period, is the leading contemporary instance.

F. Civil Extra-territoriality — the Indian Rules in Outline

Table 6.7 · The Indian civil rules with a foreign element
QuestionRuleComment
When may an Indian court hear a suit involving a foreign party?Sections 9, 15 to 20 of the Code of Civil Procedure, 1908: the courts have jurisdiction over suits of a civil nature except where expressly barred; a suit may be instituted where the defendant resides, or carries on business, or personally works for gain, or where the cause of action arises, in whole or in part.There is no general statutory doctrine of forum non conveniens; the Supreme Court discussed the English practice and the comity owed to foreign courts in Modi Entertainment Network v. W.S.G. Cricket Pte. Ltd., (2004) 7 SCC 492, in the context of anti-suit injunctions.
Service of process on a foreign defendantOrder V, Rules 25 to 27 CPC: service out of India through the court's process, and substituted service where the defendant cannot be served in the ordinary way; service abroad in conformity with any applicable treaty or through diplomatic channels.Service abroad is the classic case of a "civil enforcement" act which touches the sovereignty of another State; hence the treaty practice and the requirement of the foreign court's or the executive's assistance.
When is a foreign judgment conclusive?Section 13 CPC: a foreign judgment is conclusive as to any matter thereby directly adjudicated, except where it (a) was pronounced by a court without jurisdiction; (b) was not given on the merits; (c) appears on the face of the proceedings to be founded on an incorrect view of international law or a refusal to recognise the law of India where applicable; (d) was obtained in proceedings opposed to natural justice; (e) was obtained by fraud; or (f) sustains a claim founded on a breach of any law in force in India. Section 14 raises a presumption of the competence of the foreign court.The list shows the two ideas at work: reciprocity/comity (recognition as the norm) and the protection of the forum's fundamental policies (the exceptions). The Supreme Court applied the section to a foreign matrimonial decree in Y. Narasimha Rao v. Y. Venkata Lakshmi, (1991) 3 SCC 451.
How is a foreign judgment enforced?Section 44A CPC: where a decree has been passed by a superior court in a "reciprocating territory" notified by the Government, it may be filed in a District Court which may declare it enforceable, and it is then executed as if it were a decree of that court. Decrees of non-reciprocating territories require a fresh suit on the judgment.Reciprocal enforcement is the standard international technique: enforcement abroad is the one remedy which a court cannot provide without the other State's cooperation.
Arbitral awardsArbitration and Conciliation Act, 1996, Part II (ss. 44 to 52): New York Convention awards are enforceable in India; the grounds for refusal in section 48 are exhaustive and construed narrowly — a point settled by Shri Lal Mahal Ltd. v. Progetto Grano S.p.A., (2014) 11 SCC 639, and Vijay Karia v. Prysmian Cavi E Sistemi S.R.L., (2020) 11 SCC 1.This is the most successful modern example of States cooperating in cross-border enforcement; it is also the route by which many extra-territorial civil disputes now reach enforcement in India.
Suits against a foreign StateCPC ss. 86 and 87: no foreign State may be sued without the consent of the Central Government certified in writing by a Secretary to the Government, which consent is ordinarily withheld except in the cases listed (where the foreign State has itself sued, or carries on trade within the jurisdiction, or the proceeding relates to immovable property in India, etc.); waiver has to be shown.The Indian counterpart of sovereign immunity; the restrictive theory (immunity for jure imperii, not for jure gestionis) is followed in practice. Topic 7 deals with this head in full.
Anti-suit injunctionsAn Indian court may restrain a party from proceeding in a foreign court where the foreign proceedings are oppressive or vexatious and the Indian court is the natural forum, and where the injunction will not interfere with the foreign court's own process; the discretion is exercised with regard for comity (Modi Entertainment, 2004).The civil-side counterpart of the conflict-management techniques discussed in section H.

G. Two Real Examples — Bhopal and Vodafone

Real example 1 — Bhopal: a civil claim crossing three legal orders

On the night of 2–3 December 1984, methyl isocyanate gas escaped from the Union Carbide India Ltd pesticide plant at Bhopal, killing several thousand people and injuring many thousands more. The litigation which followed is the textbook illustration of civil extra-territoriality. (i) Claims were filed in the United States against the parent, Union Carbide Corporation; the United States District Court for the Southern District of New York in In re Union Carbide Corporation Gas Plant Disaster at Bhopal, 634 F. Supp. 842 (1986), dismissed them on the ground of forum non conveniens — the Indian forum was the more appropriate one — but conditioned the dismissal on the corporation's submission to the jurisdiction of the Indian courts, its waiver of defences on the merits, and its agreement to produce documents. (ii) India, meanwhile, enacted the Bhopal Gas Leak Disaster (Processing of Claims) Act, 1985, giving the Union the exclusive right to represent all the victims, and the Union instituted proceedings in the Indian courts. (iii) The claims were settled in 1989 for US$470 million; the settlement was upheld by the Supreme Court in Union Carbide Corporation v. Union of India, (1991) 4 SCC 584. (iv) Attempts to reopen the settlement continued for decades; the curative petitions were dismissed by the Supreme Court in 2023. The case is the best illustration for answer-writing of three propositions: that jurisdiction is a matter of the forum's own rules and of comity; that the enforcement of a claim against a foreign defendant ultimately depends on the defendant's submission or on the foreign State's cooperation; and that the "effect" (here, the harm done in India) may be located entirely in one State while the defendant and its assets sit in another.

Real example 2 — Vodafone: a fiscal claim crossing the world

The Vodafone story, set out at §6.4 D, also has a civil/investment side: the treaty-based arbitration proceedings brought in respect of the retrospective tax demanded of the acquirer, the international pressure which they generated, and the eventual legislative withdrawal of the retrospective charge in 2021. For the civil lawyer the significance is that a dispute which began as a question of statutory construction (does the Income-tax Act reach the indirect transfer of Indian assets between two foreign companies?) ended as a question of State responsibility and investment protection — the same movement from prescription to responsibility that the Lotus structure predicts.

H. Conflicts of Jurisdiction and How They Are Managed

Because the bases of jurisdiction overlap, two or more States may each have a lawful claim to prescribe, and occasionally to adjudicate, the same conduct. International law does not rank them by hierarchy; it manages the conflict in seven ways, which can be set out as a checklist for problem questions.

  1. Comity and mutual restraint. The general practice of States is to refrain from exercising jurisdiction over matters with which they have no genuine concern — the practical qualification which the class notes and Verma both attach to the Lotus principle.
  2. Priority by the strength of the link. Where several States are competent, the State with the strongest connection (normally the territorial State) is left to act; the notion of "subsidiarity" advanced in the universal-jurisdiction debate is the same idea.
  3. Treaty allocation. The law of the sea allocates jurisdiction over ships by the flag, the coastal State and the port State (UNCLOS Arts. 27, 28, 73, 92, 94, 97); the aviation conventions allocate jurisdiction over offences on board aircraft (Tokyo Convention, Art. 3); the treaty crimes allocate jurisdiction among all parties and then require the State with custody to prosecute or extradite. The most recent example of a conflict-management treaty is the 2018 CLOUD Act and its bilateral agreements on cross-border access to electronic evidence, which replaced the practice of unilateral seizure of data abroad (the issue in United States v. Microsoft, the "Microsoft Ireland" case).
  4. Immunities and privileges. Jurisdiction may be valid and unexercisable at the same time: the immunity of a diplomat, of a State's property, of a warship, or of the troika (case 37).
  5. Procedural conditions and the sanction requirement. The requirement of the Central Government's sanction for the trial of offences committed abroad (s. 188 CrPC) is an internal device by which the executive avoids conflict with another State; similar devices exist in other systems (the requirement of the Attorney-General's or the Home Secretary's consent for prosecutions under extra-territorial statutes, as in the Territorial Waters Jurisdiction Act, 1878 in England).
  6. Blocking statutes and claw-back provisions. Where a State regards a foreign prescription as an excess, it may prohibit compliance with the foreign order and provide for the recovery of judgments paid abroad (the United Kingdom's Protection of Trading Interests Act, 1980; Canada's Foreign Extraterritorial Measures Act, 1985; the EU's Blocking Regulation, 1996). The technique converts a jurisdictional dispute into a negotiation.
  7. Judicial restraint and reasonableness. The most influential modern test is section 403 of the American Law Institute's Restatement (Third) of Foreign Relations Law, which permits a State to prescribe for conduct abroad only where the exercise is "reasonable", weighing the link of the conduct to the State, the character of the activity, the extent to which other States have a legitimate interest, the likelihood of conflict, and the importance of the regulation to the international system. It is not a rule of Indian law, but it is the formulation which international lawyers use to argue about the effects doctrine, and it is worth naming in an answer.

I. Civil versus Criminal Extra-territoriality (Table 6.8)

Table 6.8 · The civil and the criminal sides of extra-territoriality compared
Point of comparisonCriminal extra-territorialityCivil extra-territoriality
Who claimsThe State, in its own name, through prosecutionA private party (or the State as a litigant), through a court of civil jurisdiction

Bases relied onTerritoriality (subjective, objective, effects), nationality (active and passive), protection, universality, treaty jurisdictionThe defendant's residence/business, the place of the cause of action, submission, choice of court, statutory assignment (admiralty, insurance, carriage)
Central distinctionPrescription may travel; enforcement may not (arrest abroad requires consent or custody)Adjudication may be possible without any enforcement; the judgment remains paper until it is executed where the assets are

The question to ask firstIs there a statute, a basis and a body? (presence or custody)Is there jurisdiction, which law applies, and where are the assets?

J. Examination Angle for 6.4

How the red-ink annotation should shape an answer

If the question says "extra-territorial jurisdiction of States", answer on both sides. Structure: (1) meaning — the two senses in Abdul Cader and the three modern techniques (effects, nationality, treaty); (2) the constitutional position (Art. 245(2)) and the international position (the Lotus rules); (3) the criminal side — the bases, the Indian statutes, s. 188 CrPC, and the immunity/procedural limits, with Vodafone, Enrica Lexie or the counterfeiting prosecutions as examples; (4) the civil side — the three PIL questions, the Indian provisions of the CPC, the exceptions in s. 13, s. 44A, State immunity, arbitration; (5) conflict management — comity, treaty allocation, blocking statutes, reasonableness; (6) conclusion — the two propositions of Lotus reconcile everything: prescription may travel, enforcement may not, and cooperation is therefore the only means by which the first becomes effective.

If the question is a problem on the civil side (an Indian company suing a foreign supplier for a cartel, or a foreign decree sought to be executed in India), the answer must mention: the CPC's place-of-suing rules; whether the defendant has submitted; the six exceptions in s. 13; whether the decree is from a reciprocating territory (s. 44A) or requires a fresh suit; any State-immunity objection; and the arbitration alternative if the contract contains a clause.

If the question is a problem on the criminal side, run the four-step method from §6.0 C: basis → power → immunity → remedy; and remember the two "traps" that examiners set — the accused who is abroad (prescription without custody), and the accused who enjoys immunity (jurisdiction without power to exercise it).

↑ top

6.5Extradition, Deportation and Asylum

A. Extradition — Definition and Nature

Extradition is the surrender by one State to another of a person accused or convicted of an offence, in order that he may be tried or punished by the State which claims him. The definitions used in the course material are worth collecting, because each contributes a different element:

  • Chief Justice Fuller in Terlinden v. Ames (184 U.S. 270, at 289), quoted by Ganguly J. in Abu Salem: "the surrender by one nation to another of an individual accused or convicted of an offence outside of its own territory, and within the territorial jurisdiction of the other, which, being competent to try and to punish him, demands the surrender."
  • Professor M. Cherif Bassiouni, quoted in the same judgment: "in contemporary practice extradition means a formal process through which a person is surrendered by one State to another by virtue of a treaty, reciprocity or comity as between the respective States."
  • The Exam Capsule: "Extradition is the handing over of an alleged offender or convicted criminal who has escaped before completing his prison term by one State to another."
  • The class notes (following Shaw): "The practice of extradition enables one State to hand over to another State suspected or convicted criminals who have fled to the territory of the former. It is based upon bilateral treaty law and does not exist as an obligation upon States in customary law."

Four features of the institution should be stated at the outset, because they organise everything that follows:

  1. It is a remedy for the territorial limitation of jurisdiction. Because a State's enforcement jurisdiction stops at its frontier and the offender may be beyond it, extradition is the machinery by which the State that has the best claim to try him obtains his person. It is, in other words, the bridge between prescription and enforcement sketched at §6.4 B.
  2. It is an act of the executive, performed on the basis of treaty. The judgment in Abu Salem states the standard formula: "Though extradition is granted in implementation of the international commitment of the State, the procedure to be followed by the courts in deciding whether extradition should be granted and on what terms is determined by the municipal law of the land." The international obligation is discharged through a domestic statutory process; that is why the same case is simultaneously a case on international law and on section 21 of the Extradition Act, 1962.
  3. The remedy is not the punishment; it is the transfer. Extradition decides where the accused will be tried, not whether he is guilty. That is why the rule of speciality, the assurances exacted from the requesting State, and the principle of non-inquiry (see below) all have the same function: to protect the terms on which the transfer was made.
  4. It is a two-State obligation with a third interest — the individual's. The classical view is that extradition is a matter between States, and the individual is its object. The modern view, reflected in Soering and in the human-rights case law, is that the individual has a protected interest at least against surrender to torture or to a penalty which would violate his fundamental rights. The most recent Indian discussion of the same tension is Abu Salem (Ganguly J.'s reference to the "emerging Human Rights movements in the post World War II scenario" and the need to "strike a fair balance").
The 2025 examination question, and how to handle its first sentence

The paper of May–June 2025 (paper code LB-205, question 3) asks: "Extradition is a matter of courtesy. Discuss the underlying principles of the Extradition under International Law and the role of municipal law provisions in extradition proceedings. Is extradition possible to those states with whom a Country has no bilateral or multilateral extradition treaty, if they are party to an international convention? Argue with the help of decided cases." The first sentence is not an accurate statement of the modern law, and the answer must say so while conceding the historical truth within it. The classical position was that no duty to extradite exists apart from treaty — the American Supreme Court in Factor v. Laubenheimer, 290 U.S. 276 (1933), said that "the legal right to demand his extradition and correlative duty to surrender him to the demanding country exist only when created by treaty"; and it follows that in the absence of a treaty the surrender, if it occurs at all, is an act of courtesy, reciprocity or comity. But three developments have qualified the classical position: (i) where a bilateral treaty exists, the duty is legal and the requested State may be held to it; (ii) where a multilateral convention in force between the two States imposes an obligation to prosecute or extradite, the duty is legal even without a bilateral treaty — the point of the third part of the question; and (iii) even in the absence of any treaty at all, a State may surrender a fugitive as an exercise of executive discretion, either by an ad hoc arrangement (rendition) or by applying its own extradition statute on the basis of reciprocity, which is exactly what India did in the Abu Salem case. The answer is developed in §6.5 E below.

B. Sources of the Duty to Extradite

Extradition is governed by a dense network of instruments and by the domestic statutes which implement them. Five layers should be distinguished, because a question may ask about any one of them.

  1. Bilateral treaties and arrangements. These are the ordinary source. India has concluded extradition treaties with a large number of States — the Ministry of External Affairs maintains the authoritative list of treaties and of extradition arrangements — and also has arrangements based on the Commonwealth scheme and on multilateral conventions. A treaty must be implemented by the Requested State in its own law; the inability of the Executive to surrender a fugitive in the absence of statutory power is the reason why the Extradition Act, 1962 exists.
  2. Multilateral conventions with extradition clauses. The Genocide Convention, 1948 (Art. VII — genocide is not a political offence for extradition purposes); the European Convention on Extradition, 1957 (with its Additional Protocols of 1975, 1978, 2010 and 2012); the Arab League Extradition Agreement, 1952; the Inter-American Convention, 1981; the ECOWAS Convention, 1994; the Commonwealth Scheme Relating to the Rendition of Fugitive Offenders (1966, revised 2002); the SAARC Regional Convention on the Suppression of Terrorism, 1987 and its Additional Protocol of 2004; and the sectoral anti-terrorism conventions — the Hague Convention, 1970 (Arts. 7 and 8), the Montreal Convention, 1971, the Hostages Convention, 1979, the Torture Convention, 1984, the SUA Convention, 1988 and the Terrorist Bombings Convention, 1997 — each of which provides that the offence is deemed an extraditable offence in any extradition treaty between the parties, and permits the convention itself to be treated as the legal basis for extradition in the absence of a treaty. The UN Model Treaty on Extradition, 1990 (General Assembly Resolution 45/116) and the model clauses of the UNTOC (2000, Art. 16) and the UNCAC (2003, Art. 44) supply the standard drafting.
  3. "Surrender" regimes: the European Arrest Warrant and its equivalents. Council Framework Decision 2002/584/JHA of 13 June 2002 (in force from 1 January 2004) replaced extradition between the member States of the European Union with a system of surrender based on mutual recognition of judicial decisions: the political-offence exception is abolished, double criminality is dispensed with for a list of thirty-two categories of offence, and strict time limits (generally sixty, or ninety, days) apply. The Court of Justice of the European Union has qualified the system by human-rights requirements — see Aranyosi and Căldăraru (2016) on prison conditions and the two-step test, and LM (2018) on systemic deficiencies in the fairness of trials — and since 2020 the United Kingdom's relations are governed by the corresponding title of the EU–UK Trade and Cooperation Agreement. The lesson for the comparative part of an answer is that "extradition" and "surrender" are different legal techniques resting on different justifications: the first on treaty and reciprocity, the second on mutual recognition and mutual trust.
  4. The law of the requested State. Whatever the international source, the transfer must be lawful under the requested State's own statute: the Indian Extradition Act, 1962 and the Extradition (India–Portugal) notification, GSR 822(E) of 13 December 2002, in the case of Abu Salem; the Extradition Act of the United Kingdom; the relevant provisions of the Portuguese law in the same case; the Fugitive Offenders legislation of Commonwealth States; and so on.
  5. Custom and general principles. There is no customary duty to extradite, but there are customary and general principles which govern the manner of extradition: the rule of speciality (now almost universal in treaty practice), the political-offence exception (in its historical core), the requirement that a person may not be surrendered to face a penalty which international law prohibits, and the principle of non-inquiry (the requested State does not supervise the requesting State's justice system, but the principle is not absolute).

C. The General Principles of Extradition

Verma's Chapter 9 and the class notes agree on the list of principles, and the table below sets them out with their authorities and their Indian provisions. Three of them are developed further in section D (speciality, as the governing rule in Abu Salem), and two in section I (abduction, in Savarkar).

Table 6.9 · The principles of extradition at a glance
PrincipleContentAuthorityIndian provision / practice
No extradition without a treaty, in principleThere is no duty in customary international law to surrender a fugitive; in the absence of a treaty the surrender is an act of courtesy, reciprocity or executive discretion — but it may still be made.Factor v. Laubenheimer (1933); the class notes ("It is based upon bilateral treaty law and does not exist as an obligation upon States in customary law"); Abu Salem (Ganguly J. quoting Bassiouni: surrender "by virtue of a treaty, reciprocity or comity")Extradition Act, 1962: powers exercisable when a treaty is in force (Ch. II), or where the Act is applied by notification under s. 3, or where a convention is treated as a treaty under s. 3(4) — see §6.5 D and §6.5 H
Extraditable personA State may seek the extradition of its own nationals or of nationals of a third State. A requested State is under no obligation to extradite its own nationals, and many States refuse; the refusal does not license impunity, because the alternative is prosecution at home (aut punire aut dedere).Verma, Ch. 9 ("A requested State is not obliged to extradite its own nationals, though it is not an absolute rule, and a State can extradite its own nationals on the basis of reciprocity"); the Smart Notes: "States refuse to surrender its nationals"No statutory bar in India; the position depends on the treaty. Where India refuses, it must be prepared to prosecute — the same logic as the treaty crimes
Extraditable crime and the rule of double criminalityThe conduct must be criminal under the law of both States. Practice uses two techniques: a list of extraditable offences attached to the law or the treaty, and a general clause with a minimum penalty (the "minimum-penalty" or "eliminative" approach). Substantial similarity is enough; the punishment need not be identical.Verma, Ch. 9 (Rambabu Saxena v. State, AIR 1950 SC 155 — extradition allowed even for offences not named in the treaty where the treaty authorised additional offences); the Smart Notes (Jacob Factor's case; Eisler)Second Schedule to the Extradition Act lists the offences for Commonwealth cases and non-treaty cases; where a treaty exists, the treaty's own list applies; the India–Canada Treaty of 1987 uses the one-year minimum penalty formula (Verma)
Rule of specialityThe requesting State may try and punish the surrendered person only for the offence for which extradition was sought and granted; otherwise the consent of the surrendering State is required ("unfettered by the terms of the surrender").U.S. v. Rauscher, 119 U.S. 407 (1886); R. v. Corrigan, [1931] 1 K.B. 527 (same facts, different label — jurisdiction upheld); the doctrine "partakes of doctrines of both double criminality and reciprocity" (Exam Capsule)Section 21 of the Extradition Act, 1962 — and, in the casebook, case 38 (Abu Salem), where the Supreme Court held that trial for "lesser offences" is permitted by s. 21(b)
Political-offence exceptionPersons accused of political offences are not extradited; the exception is of customary origin and is the source of most litigation; it does not protect war criminals, genocide and crimes covered by the terrorism conventions ("without exception whatsoever").Re Castioni [1891] 1 Q.B. 149; Re Meunier [1894] 2 Q.B. 415; ex parte Kolczynski [1955] 1 Q.B. 540; Cheng v. Governor of Pentonville Prison [1973] A.C. 931; the attentat clause (Belgium, 1856); Genocide Convention Art. VIIExtradition Act, 1962: the Central Government may refuse extradition for political reasons (s. 29) and the Act's restrictions on surrender; offences listed in the Second Schedule are outside the exception
Prima facie caseThe requested State's magistrate must be satisfied that there is a prima facie case of the crime alleged before the fugitive is committed.Verma, Ch. 9, citing the Tarashov extradition case (1963) and s. 7(3) of the ActSection 7(3) of the Extradition Act, 1962 (inquiry by the magistrate); s. 9 (warrant on the magistrate's own initiative)
Time-barA person is not extradited if the prosecution for the offence has, under the law of the requesting State, become time-barred.Verma, Ch. 9 (which cites the time-bar clause of the Act as the authority for the rule)Section 31(1)(b), Extradition Act, 1962
Priority of the requested State's own proceedingsA fugitive who is accused of an offence in the requested State, or who is serving a sentence there, will not be surrendered until he has been discharged — by acquittal, by expiry of sentence or otherwise.The practice of States in refusing priority to the requesting StateSection 31(1)(d), Extradition Act, 1962
Previous trial in the requested StateA requested State may refuse extradition where the fugitive has already been tried and convicted or acquitted by its own courts for the same offence, or is being prosecuted there; this is the domestic application of ne bis in idem, and it is reinforced in treaty practice by the provision that extradition shall not be granted where the offence has been the subject of a final judgment in the requested State.The general treaty practice (the UN Model Treaty on Extradition, 1990, Art. 4(1)(e)); Verma's discussion of the restrictions on surrenderThe principle is given effect through s. 31(1)(d) (pending proceedings or sentence) and through the executive's power to refuse under s. 29 in a fit case
Human-rights limitsA person may not be surrendered where there is a real risk that he will be subjected to torture or inhuman or degrading treatment, or (in many systems) to a flagrantly unfair trial. Assurances from the requesting State may be taken into account but must be shown to be reliable.Soering v. United Kingdom (1989) 11 E.H.R.R. 439; Chahal v. United Kingdom (1996) 23 E.H.R.R. 413; Saadi v. Italy (2008); UNCAT Art. 3; ICCPR Arts. 6, 7 and 14India's practice of giving assurances (the "solemn assurance" in Abu Salem that the death penalty would not be imposed); the constitutional jurisprudence on Art. 21 in extradition matters
Non-inquiryThe requested State does not, as a rule, inquire into the fairness of the criminal justice system of the requesting State, or supervise the conduct of the trial after surrender; the rule is not absolute and yields where the procedure is primâ facie contrary to fundamental principles of justice.Canada v. Schmidt, [1987] 1 S.C.R. 500, quoted by Ganguly J. in Abu Salem: "The judicial process in a foreign country must not be subjected to finicky evaluations against the rules governing the legal process in this country"Stated in Abu Salem (Ganguly J.'s concurring judgment), where the Portuguese courts awaited the Indian Supreme Court's decision rather than inquiring themselves
ReciprocityThe requesting State must be able to offer reciprocity; where there is no treaty, reciprocity supplies the legal basis for the surrender in many systems (and for the application of the domestic statute by notification).Verma's discussion of India's request to Portugal; the class notes on the Abu Salem "principle of reciprocity"Notification GSR 822(E) of 13 December 2002 applying the Extradition Act, 1962 (except Chapter III) to Portugal, and the express reliance on reciprocity in the Indian request
Aut punire aut dedereWhere extradition is refused, the requested State should punish the offender itself — the maxim which links extradition to universal jurisdiction and to the treaty crimes.The class notes; the Torture Convention Art. 7; Questions relating to the Obligation to Prosecute or Extradite (Belgium v. Senegal), I.C.J. Reports 2012, p. 422The Indian practice of prosecuting where surrender is refused; the sectoral statutes which reproduce the obligation

Two clarifications will earn marks if they are given precisely. First, the double-criminality rule compares offences, not their punishment: a fugitive may be surrendered although the sanctions differ, but a State may refuse where the punishment in the requesting State would be of a kind it does not permit (the death-penalty point, dealt with by assurances). Second, the political-offence exception is not a general licence: it is an exception which the requested State's courts and executive define, and it has been progressively narrowed by the terrorism conventions and by the attentat clause (the attempt on the life of a Head of State or his family, first introduced by Belgium in 1856, is not to be treated as a political offence). Verma's summary of the case law is the clearest available statement of how the exception works in practice, and it is reproduced in section H below with the cases digested.

↑ top

D. Extradition in Indian Law — the Extradition Act, 1962

The Extradition Act, 1962 (Act 34 of 1962), which came into force on 5 January 1963, consolidated and amended the earlier law — the Indian Extradition Act, 1903 and the Fugitive Offenders Act, 1881 — and was substantially amended by Act 66 of 1993. Its structure is simple: it has five Chapters, and each does one job.

Table 6.10 · The Extradition Act, 1962 — section map
ChapterSectionsWhat each provision does (the sections an examiner expects)
I — Preliminary1Short title, extent (whole of India), commencement.
2Definitions: "extradition offence" (s. 2(c)) — for a treaty State, an offence provided for in the treaty; for a non-treaty State, an offence punishable with imprisonment of not less than one year under the law of India or of the foreign State — and includes a composite offence (s. 2(a): an act or conduct occurring wholly or partly in a foreign State or in India whose effects or intended effects, taken as a whole, would constitute an extradition offence in India or in a foreign State); "extradition treaty" (s. 2(d)) — a treaty, agreement or arrangement, including one made before 15 August 1947 which extends to and is binding on India; "foreign State" (s. 2(e)); "fugitive criminal" (s. 2(f)) — a person accused or convicted of an extradition offence within the jurisdiction of a foreign State, including a person who, while in India, conspires, attempts, incites or participates as an accomplice in the commission of an extradition offence in a foreign State; "treaty State" (s. 2(j)).
3Application of the Act. s. 3(1): the Central Government may, by notified order, direct that the provisions of the Act other than Chapter III shall apply to a named foreign State or part of it. s. 3(3): where the notified order relates to a treaty State, it must set out the treaty in full, cannot outlast the treaty, and may be made subject to modifications to implement the treaty. s. 3(4): where there is no extradition treaty with a foreign State, the Central Government may, by notified order, treat any Convention to which India and that State are parties as an extradition treaty, providing for extradition in respect of the offences specified in that Convention. This is the statutory answer to the question whether extradition is possible without a bilateral treaty.
II — Extradition to foreign States to which Chapter II applies4Requisition for surrender, made to the Central Government by the diplomatic representative of the foreign State at Delhi, or by that Government through India's diplomatic representative, or in the mode settled by arrangement. There is no power to act on a request from a foreign court or police agency directly.
5Order for magisterial inquiry: the Central Government may, if it thinks fit, order a Magistrate who would have had jurisdiction had the offence been committed within his local limits to inquire into the case. The word "may" is the source of the executive's discretion (Verma notes that s. 3(1) gives the Government discretion — the "politics" of extradition).
6On receipt of the order under s. 5, the Magistrate shall issue a warrant for the arrest of the fugitive criminal.
7Procedure before the Magistrate — the heart of the Act. s. 7(1): the inquiry is conducted in the same manner and with the same jurisdiction and powers as nearly as may be as if the case were triable by a court of session or High Court. s. 7(2): evidence is taken in support of the requisition and on behalf of the fugitive, including evidence to show that the offence is of a political character or is not an extradition offence. s. 7(3): if no prima facie case is made out, the Magistrate shall discharge the fugitive. s. 7(4): if a prima facie case is made out, he may commit the fugitive to prison to await the orders of the Central Government and shall report the result of the inquiry with the fugitive's written statement, if any.
8Surrender: on receipt of the report, the Central Government, if of opinion that the fugitive ought to be surrendered, issues the warrant for his custody and removal and delivery to a named person at a named place. The surrender itself is an executive act; the magistrate only commits.
9Power of a Magistrate to issue a warrant on his own initiative where it appears that a person within his jurisdiction is a fugitive criminal, on such information and evidence as would justify a warrant had the offence been committed locally; he must forthwith report to the Central Government, and the person arrested cannot be detained for more than three months unless an order under s. 5 is received.
10Reception in evidence of exhibits, depositions, copies, certificates of fact and judicial documents — if duly authenticated; and the conditions of authentication (signature of a judge, magistrate or officer; certification; authentication by oath or official seal of a Minister). This is the provision which makes extradition work in practice, because the evidence is ordinarily produced by the requesting State in documentary form.
11Chapter II does not apply to States to which Chapter III applies.
III — Return of fugitives to States with extradition arrangements12Application of the Chapter by notified order to foreign States with which there is an extradition arrangement; the Government may apply it with modifications.
13–17Liability of such fugitives to be apprehended and returned; apprehension under an endorsed warrant (s. 15 — endorsed by the Central Government where the warrant was issued by a person having lawful authority) or a provisional warrant (s. 16 — issued by any magistrate; the Government may discharge the person; remand not exceeding seven days at a time pending production of an endorsed warrant); s. 17 — the magistrate, if satisfied that the endorsed warrant is duly authenticated and the offence is an extradition offence, commits the fugitive to prison and certifies the committal to the Central Government; if not satisfied, he may detain or release on bail pending the Government's orders.
18Return by warrant: the Central Government may issue the warrant of custody and removal to the foreign State concerned.
IV — Surrender or return of accused or convicted persons from foreign States (India as the requesting State)19Mode of requisition by India — by the Central Government to the diplomatic representative of the foreign State at Delhi, or to that Government through India's representative, or as arranged; and the prescribed form of warrant for a person suspected to be in a State to which Chapter III applies. This is the provision under which India's own requests — including the request to Portugal in Abu Salem — are framed.
20Conveyance of the surrendered person into India and delivery to the proper authority to be dealt with according to law.
21Accused or convicted person surrendered or returned by a foreign State not to be tried for certain offences — the statutory rule of speciality: such a person shall not, until he has been restored or has had an opportunity of returning to that State, be tried in India for an offence other than — (a) the extradition offence in relation to which he was surrendered or returned; or (b) any lesser offence disclosed by the facts proved for the purposes of securing his surrender or return, other than an offence in relation to which an order for his surrender or return could not lawfully be made; or (c) the offence in respect of which the foreign State has given its consent. Mischief of the provision: it protects the surrendering State's bargain and prevents a "blanket extradition". The scope of clause (b) is the question decided in case 38.
V — Miscellaneous22–28Every fugitive criminal is liable to be arrested and surrendered whether the offence was committed before or after the commencement of the Act, and whether or not a court in India has jurisdiction to try that offence (s. 22); jurisdiction as to offences committed on the high seas or in the air on board a vessel or aircraft which comes into an Indian port or aerodrome (s. 23); discharge by the High Court if the fugitive is not conveyed out of India within two months of committal (s. 24); application of the ordinary bail provisions (s. 25); abetment, conspiracy, attempt, incitement and complicity treated as the extradition offence itself (s. 26); lawfulness of custody and re-taking on escape (s. 27); property found on the fugitive, to be delivered up subject to third-party rights (s. 28).
29Power of the Central Government to discharge any fugitive criminal — if it appears that, by reason of the trivial nature of the case, or because the application was not made in good faith or in the interests of justice, or for political reasons or otherwise, it is unjust or inexpedient to surrender, the Government may stay the proceedings and cancel any warrant.
