This is the political-offence and policy filter in executive form.
30Simultaneous requisitions from more than one State: the Central Government may surrender to such State as it thinks fit — the statutory rule of priority, decided by reference to the circumstances of the case.
31Restrictions on surrender. A fugitive shall not be surrendered — (a) if the offence is of a political character, or if he proves that the requisition or warrant was in fact made with a view to try or punish him for an offence of a political character; (b) if the prosecution for the offence is, according to the law of the requesting State, barred by time; (c) unless provision is made by the law of that State or in the treaty that the fugitive will not be tried for an offence other than the extradition offence, a lesser offence of the kind described, or the offence to which the Central Government has consented — that is, reciprocal speciality; (d) if he is accused of some offence in India other than the offence for which surrender is sought, or is undergoing sentence in India, until he is discharged — the rule of priority of the domestic prosecution; (e) until fifteen days have elapsed from the date of committal. s. 31(2): the offences specified in the Schedule are not to be regarded as political offences; s. 31(3): the Central Government may add to or omit from the Schedule. Section 32 provides that sections 29 and 31 apply without modification to every foreign State, notwithstanding the terms of any notified order.
33–34Cs. 33: the Act does not affect the provisions of the Foreigners Act, 1946 or any order made under it — the statutory confirmation that extradition and deportation are distinct (see section J below). s. 34: an extradition offence committed by any person in a foreign State shall be deemed to have been committed in India, and such person shall be liable to be prosecuted in India — a general extra-territorial penal jurisdiction created by the Act itself. s. 34A: where the Central Government is of the opinion that a fugitive cannot be surrendered pursuant to a request, it may take steps to prosecute him in India — the aut dedere aut judicare rule in statutory form. s. 34B: provisional arrest on an urgent request; discharge after sixty days if no request for surrender is received. s. 34C: where the fugitive has committed an offence punishable with death in India and the law of the surrendering State does not provide the death penalty for such an offence, he shall be liable only to imprisonment for life — the statutory form of the death-penalty assurance which India gives in practice (and which was given to Portugal in Abu Salem).
35Every notified order and notification under the Act is to be laid before each House of Parliament.
36–37Rule-making power; and the repeal of the Indian Extradition Act, 1903 and related enactments.

The 1993 amendment — five changes worth naming

  1. The Commonwealth/non-Commonwealth distinction was abolished. The separate regime for "Commonwealth countries" was deleted; Chapter III now applies to foreign States with which India has an "extradition arrangement", and Chapter II applies to the rest.
  2. Conventions became potential extradition treaties. Section 3(4) was inserted: a multilateral convention to which India and a foreign State are parties may be treated by notified order as an extradition treaty for the offences specified in it — the mechanism anticipated in the answer to the 2025 paper's question.
  3. The rule of speciality was rewritten (s. 21) to permit trial for a "lesser offence", and a corresponding requirement was written into the restrictions on surrender (s. 31(1)(c)), so that India both gives and demands the same protection.
  4. Extra-territorial prosecution and the "prosecute or extradite" alternative entered the Act (ss. 34 and 34A).
  5. The death penalty and the Schedule were dealt with: s. 34C provides for life imprisonment where the surrendering State does not provide the death penalty; and s. 31(2) with the Schedule excludes a list of offences (principally those under the anti-terrorism conventions) from the political-offence exception, with power to amend the list by notification.

The process in one picture (Fig 6.7)

India's treaty and convention network

The number changes from year to year, and the figures should be given as of a stated date. According to the Ministry of External Affairs' reply in Parliament of 19 December 2025, India has extradition treaties with 48 countries and extradition arrangements with 12 countries; in the preceding five years India sent 137 extradition requests, 25 fugitives were successfully extradited, and three requests were refused. In August 2026 the Union Cabinet approved the signing of extradition treaties with Kenya, Kazakhstan and the Kyrgyz Republic and gave ex post facto approval to the treaty signed with Seychelles in June 2026, which would take the number of treaty partners to 52. The same parliamentary record notes that the majority of the 274 fugitives returned to India from 36 countries between 2019 and 2026 came back through deportation rather than extradition — a fact worth remembering, because it is the practical proof of the distinction drawn in section J below.

India's multilateral engagements are the second half of the network: it is a party to the Genocide Convention, 1948 (Art. VII); the Tokyo, Hague and Montreal Conventions on aviation; the Hostages Convention, 1979; the Torture Convention, 1984 (which India signed on 14 October 1997 but has not ratified — the consequence being that its Article 3 prohibition of return to torture binds India through customary law, the ICCPR and Article 21 rather than through the Convention); the SUA Convention, 1988 and its 2005 Protocol; the Terrorist Bombings Convention, 1997; the UNTOC, 2000 (ratified 2011, Art. 16); the UNCAC, 2003 (ratified 2011, Art. 44); the SAARC Regional Convention on Suppression of Terrorism, 1987 with its 2004 Additional Protocol; the BIMSTEC Convention of 2009; and the International Convention for the Suppression of Acts of Nuclear Terrorism, 2005. India is not a party to the Rome Statute, the 1957 European Convention on Extradition, the 1951 Refugee Convention or its 1967 Protocol, and the Refugee Convention is not directly applicable in Indian courts — a point taken up in section K.

Extradition without a treaty — the answer in one box (the third limb of the 2025 question)

Where India has no bilateral extradition treaty with the State holding the fugitive, extradition is still possible on four separate bases, and each should be named in the answer:

  1. By notification applying the Act on the basis of reciprocity — section 3(1)–(3) of the Extradition Act, 1962: the Central Government may direct by notified order that the Act (except Chapter III) shall apply to a named foreign State. This is precisely what was done in Abu Salem by GSR 822(E) dated 13 December 2002, after India and Portugal found themselves without a treaty; the courts of Portugal recognised the notification, and the surrender followed.
  2. By treating a multilateral convention as an extradition treaty — section 3(4) of the Act, inserted in 1993: "Where there is no extradition treaty made by India with any foreign State, the Central Government may, by notified order, treat any Convention to which India and a foreign State are parties, as an extradition treaty made by India with that foreign State providing for extradition in respect of the offences specified in that Convention."
  3. Under the conventions themselves, which by their own terms make the specified offences extraditable between the parties — genocide (Art. VII of the 1948 Convention), torture (Art. 7 of the 1984 Convention), hostage-taking (Art. 9 of the 1979 Convention), terrorist bombing (Art. 11 of the 1997 Convention), offences against the safety of civil aviation (Arts. 7 and 8 of the Hague Convention, 1970; Art. 7 of the Montreal Convention, 1971), and unlawful acts against the safety of maritime navigation (Art. 11 of the SUA Convention, 1988) — with the obligation, where extradition is refused, to submit the case for prosecution.
  4. By an ad hoc arrangement or as an act of comity — the classical "courtesy" basis, which is what remains of the proposition in the question; and, at the level of practice, by the co-operation arrangements (extradition arrangements, of which India has twelve) and by the use of deportation where surrender is not available.

Conclusion for the answer. Courtesy is the historical foundation and the residual basis; it is not the modern foundation. Where a treaty, a convention or a notified order applies, the surrender is a legal obligation, and refusal in the face of it engages State responsibility — which is why the better formulation is that extradition is governed by treaty and assisted by courtesy, and that a State which cannot surrender must either prosecute (s. 34A) or accept that the fugitive remains at large in its territory.

Real examples — the Indian machinery at work (2024–2026)

1. The United States: Tahawwur Rana. Rana, a Canadian citizen of Pakistani origin, was sought for his alleged role in the 2008 Mumbai attacks. He had been acquitted in the United States of material-support charges, and he resisted extradition on the ground of double jeopardy. The Ninth Circuit Court of Appeals rejected the plea, holding that the offences charged in India were different from those on which he had been tried, and the United States Supreme Court declined review; the Secretary of State allowed the extradition, and he was brought to India in April 2025. The case is the modern illustration of (i) the distinction between the domestic acquittal of the requested State and the extradition offence of the treaty; (ii) non bis in idem as an argument in extradition; and (iii) the fact that a request may be granted years after it was made.

2. The United Kingdom: Sanjay Bhandari, Vijay Mallya, Nirav Modi. Bhandari was ordered to be extradited by the Westminster Magistrates' Court in 2020, his appeals failed, and he was brought to India in July 2024 — the first successful extradition from the United Kingdom to India in many years. Mallya's extradition was ordered in December 2018 and upheld by the High Court in April 2020, and Nirav Modi's extradition was ordered in February 2021 and upheld on appeal in 2022; both remain in the United Kingdom, their cases prolonged by asylum and human-rights proceedings. The trio shows the three variables in every extradition: the court's order, the executive's decision, and the collateral remedies available to the fugitive.

3. Deportation as the practical alternative. The parliamentary data cited above — 274 fugitives brought back from 36 countries between 2019 and 2026, the majority by deportation — explains why every answer on this topic should contain a paragraph on the extradition/deportation distinction. Where the treaty process is slow or unavailable, the executive routes are removal on grounds of illegal entry or visa breach, cancellation of travel documents, and persuasion of the host State to expel. The risk, and the reason the distinction matters in law, is that deportation does not carry the safeguards of extradition: no prima facie inquiry, no rule of speciality, no political-offence exception, and no restriction on re-extradition to a third State, unless a treaty or the host State's own law imports them.

4. Requests refused or obstructed. The extradition of persons who have obtained citizenship or asylum in the requested State is the commonest cause of failure; so is the delay of the judicial process. The casebook's own Abu Salem and the Portuguese annulment proceedings of 2015 illustrate the further risk that the requesting State may be accused of breaching the assurances on which the surrender was granted — a risk that s. 34C and the speciality rule of s. 21 are designed to reduce.

E. Human Rights and Extradition — Soering, Chahal and Assurances

Ganguly J.'s concurring judgment in Abu Salem identified the tension which this subsection resolves: extradition law is "the meeting point of two divergent trends" — the expansion of human-rights protection after 1945, and the need of the international community to curb transnational crime. The modern law answers the tension in five propositions, each of which has a leading case.

1. The starting point — extradition itself is lawful, but it may not expose the fugitive to prohibited treatment

Soering v. United Kingdom

European Court of Human Rights (Plenary), Application No. 14038/88, judgment of 7 July 1989, (1989) 11 E.H.R.R. 439

Provenance. Not a casebook case; included because the Indian judgment in Abu Salem cites it as the leading statement of the human-rights limit on extradition.

Facts. Jens Soering, a German national, was detained in England on charges of cheque frauds. The United States sought his extradition to face trial in Virginia for the murders of the parents of his girlfriend, offences which carried the death penalty; under the applicable law the prosecution could ask for the death penalty and, if a capital sentence were imposed, the average time spent on death row before execution in Virginia was between six and eight years. The applicant resisted extradition on the ground, among others, that his surrender would expose him to inhuman and degrading treatment contrary to Article 3 of the European Convention on Human Rights because of the conditions on death row (the "death row phenomenon") and the length of the wait.

Decision and principle. The Court held, by a large majority, that the decision by a Contracting State to extradite a fugitive may give rise to an issue under Article 3 "where substantial grounds have been shown for believing that the person concerned, if extradited, faces a real risk of being subjected to torture or to inhuman or degrading treatment or punishment in the requesting country". The Court rejected the argument that extradition involves the responsibility of the requesting State alone: "the extradition of a person to a country where he faces a real risk of being subjected to torture or inhuman or degrading treatment… would be inconsistent with the fundamental values of democratic societies". The death penalty itself was not, in 1989 and on the Convention's own terms, a violation of Article 3; but the combination of a capital sentence with the "death row phenomenon" — the manner and duration of the wait — could cross the threshold, and on the facts of the case the Court found that extradition to face that risk would violate Article 3. The judgment also contains the balancing passage quoted by Ganguly J. in Abu Salem: the search "for a fair balance between the demands of the general interest of the community and the requirements of the protection of the individual's fundamental rights", and the warning that the establishment of safe havens for fugitives would endanger the State obliged to harbour them and undermine the foundations of extradition.

The rule in one line. Extradition to a real risk of torture or inhuman or degrading treatment is prohibited; the prohibition is engaged by the foreseeable consequences of the surrender, not by the requesting State's law in the abstract; and the assessment is of a "real risk", evaluated on the individual's circumstances.

2. The prohibition is absolute — Chahal

Chahal v. United Kingdom, (1996) 23 E.H.R.R. 413. Karamjit Singh Chahal, a Sikh separatist, had lived in England since 1974; the Home Secretary decided to deport him on national-security grounds on the basis of secret evidence, and India gave assurances about his safety. The Court held: (i) Article 3 does not permit a balancing of the risk of ill-treatment against the reasons advanced for removal, "however undesirable" the individual's activities, and the Home Secretary had no power to weigh the threat to national security against the risk; (ii) the assurances given by India were not a sufficient safeguard, because the applicant's allegations of a real risk had to be examined independently of the assurances; and (iii) the absence of any effective remedy allowing the national-security assessment to be reviewed was a violation of Article 13. The case is the origin of the proposition, now standard in the law of extradition and deportation alike, that the absolute character of the prohibition of torture is not qualified by the seriousness of the offence or the alleged danger of the individual.

3. Assurances are relevant but not conclusive — Saadi and Othman

Saadi v. Italy, Application No. 37201/06, Grand Chamber judgment of 28 February 2008. The applicant, a Tunisian suspected of terrorism, was to be deported to Tunisia under a diplomatic assurance from the Tunisian authorities. The Grand Chamber held that diplomatic assurances do not by themselves absolve the State from its Article 3 obligation: the Court must examine whether the assurances are in practice a sufficient guarantee, in the light of the general human-rights situation in the receiving country, the source of the assurances and the treatment that the individual would receive. The Court reaffirmed the "real risk" test and the absolute nature of Article 3, and held that the applicant had not established that he would be at real risk; but the reasoning establishes the framework within which every contemporary extradition-and-assurance question is argued. In Othman (Abu Qatada) v. United Kingdom, Application No. 8139/09, judgment of 17 January 2012, the Court set out the conditions under which assurances will be treated as adequate: they must have been given in good faith, they must relate to the specific risk, and there must be an objective, effective and independently verifiable system of protection in the receiving State.

4. The death penalty, and the practice of conditional surrender

Three propositions should be distinguished. (i) Under the European Convention, Article 1 of Protocol No. 6 and Article 2 of Protocol No. 13 have abolished the death penalty; the Strasbourg Court held in Al-Saadoon and Mufdhi v. United Kingdom (2010) that Article 3 now prohibits the death penalty tout court. (ii) Under the ICCPR, the Human Rights Committee held in Judge v. Canada (2003) that a State party which has abolished the death penalty may not extradite a person to face it without assurances, and the earlier Kindler v. Canada (1993) case shows the contrary view of the majority of that period. (iii) Under Indian law, the problem is handled by statute: section 34C of the Extradition Act, 1962 — where the fugitive has committed an offence punishable with death in India and the law of the surrendering State does not provide the death penalty for such an offence, he "shall be liable for punishment of imprisonment for life only for that offence". This is the statutory form of the assurance which India gave Portugal on 25 May 2003 in the Abu Salem case, and it is the practice through which India secures the surrender of persons from States which have abolished capital punishment — the request from such a State being framed on the footing that the maximum penalty in India will be life imprisonment.

5. The rule of non-inquiry — how far is the requested State's court to look?

The counterpart of the human-rights limit is the rule of non-inquiry stated in section A: the requested State does not superintend the justice system of the requesting State. In Abu Salem, Ganguly J. records the rule in its classical form — quoting La Forest J. in Canada v. Schmidt, [1987] 1 S.C.R. 500 — and then adds the qualification: "In a given situation, the requested State may question the procedures in the requesting State if they are prima facie contrary to fundamental principles of justice and there is a high risk of the fugitive being prejudiced by the process of extradition." The modern European law states the same exception in a narrower form: extradition to a "flagrant denial of justice" may violate Article 6 of the Convention in the exceptional case (Öcalan v. Turkey, 2005, para. 205; applied in Ahorugeze v. Sweden, 2011), and Soering itself left open the possibility that an unfair trial could engage Article 3.

Table 6.13 · The four European authorities on extradition, expulsion and human rights
CaseFacts in one lineHoldingUse in an Indian answer
Soering (1989)German national to be extradited from England to Virginia to face a capital charge and six to eight years on death rowExtradition to a real risk of inhuman or degrading treatment violates Article 3; the consequences must be foreseeable; a balancing passage on extradition and safe havensThe leading statement of the human-rights limit; quoted in Abu Salem (Ganguly J.)
Chahal (1996)Sikh separatist to be deported from the UK on national-security grounds despite assurances from IndiaArticle 3 is absolute; the risk cannot be balanced against the danger posed by the individual; assurances require independent scrutiny; Article 13 violationFor deportation and expulsion questions; the basis of the non-refoulement argument in Indian litigation
Saadi (2008)Tunisian terror suspect to be deported under diplomatic assurancesAssurances are relevant but not sufficient; the real-risk test must be applied to the individual's actual circumstancesTo argue that assurances must be scrutinised, not accepted at face value
Othman (2012)Abu Qatada to be deported to Jordan under assurancesConditions for adequate assurances: good faith, specificity, an objective, effective and verifiable protection systemFor the standard by which India's assurances to a requesting State (or another State's assurances to India) should be judged
How to use all this in an answer on extradition

A complete answer on extradition should carry a short paragraph of this kind: "Extradition is the surrender of a fugitive for trial, and it is lawful in itself. But it is limited by the human-rights obligations of the surrendering State. A State may not surrender a person where substantial grounds exist for believing that he faces a real risk of torture or inhuman or degrading treatment (Soering; Chahal; Article 3, Torture Convention; Article 7, ICCPR). Assurances from the requesting State are relevant but must be specific, given in good faith and independently verifiable (Saadi; Othman). Accordingly, States surrender on terms: India gives and receives assurances about the death penalty, and section 34C of the Extradition Act, 1962 limits the punishment to life imprisonment where the surrendering State does not provide for the death penalty. Beyond these limits, the requested State does not inquire into the fairness of the requesting State's system (Canada v. Schmidt), except where the procedure would be prima facie contrary to fundamental principles of justice and there is a high risk of prejudice (Abu Salem, Ganguly J.)." Four authorities, four propositions, and the statutory provision: that is a complete answer.

↑ top

F. Case 38 — Abu Salem Abdul Qayoom Ansari v. State of Maharashtra

Abu Salem Abdul Qayoom Ansari v. State of Maharashtra

Common judgment of the Supreme Court of India dated 10 September 2010 in Criminal Appeal Nos. 1142–1143 of 2007 and Writ Petition (Criminal) No. 171 of 2006 (and Criminal Appeal No. 990 of 2006), delivered by P. Sathasivam J. and M. Ganguly J. · The syllabus cites the case as Abu Salem v. State of Maharashtra (2011) S.C.C. 214 · Printed in the DU case material at pp. 177–185 · The prosecution arose out of the Bombay Bomb Blast Case No. 1 of 1993 (RC No. 1(S/93)/CBI/STF)

Provenance. The account below follows the judgment as printed in the DU case material, and the parallel treatment in S.K. Verma's Chapter 9 (pp. 244–245) and in the class notes, which summarise the facts and the holding. The post-2010 developments noted at the end of this block are drawn from public reporting and are flagged as such.

1. Why the case is in the syllabus

Abu Salem is the Indian case on extradition: it is the only decision in the case material in which the Supreme Court works through the international law of extradition and the machinery of the Extradition Act, 1962 together. It is prescribed because it decides, from the inside, three questions which examiners set year after year:

  1. Can an Indian court try a person who was surrendered by a foreign State only for certain offences? Yes — but only within the limits of the rule of speciality, and section 21 of the Extradition Act allows trial for the offences for which extradition was granted and for lesser offences made out by the same facts.
  2. What happens when there is no extradition treaty with the State which holds the fugitive? The Extradition Act may still be applied by notification under section 3 on the basis of reciprocity; and a multilateral convention (there, the International Convention for the Suppression of Terrorist Bombings) may itself be relied upon as a basis for the request.
  3. Does the rule of speciality give the extradited person a right which he can enforce in the courts of the requesting State? It is a rule of international law implemented by section 21 of the Act, and the courts will measure the trial against it — but they will not accept the extravagant version of the rule, nor will they inquire into the fairness of the trial in the requesting State (the rule of non-inquiry).

2. Facts

(a) The crime. On 12 March 1993 a series of bomb explosions took place in Mumbai. The explosions killed 257 persons, injured 713 persons of various degrees, and destroyed property worth more than Rs 27 crore. The bombs were placed at vital Government installations, public places and crowded places — the Bombay Stock Exchange, the Air India building, near Shiv Sena Bhavan, the Plaza cinema, Zaveri Bazar and Sheikh Memon Street — and suitcases filled with RDX were planted in three five-star hotels (the Sea Rock, the Juhu Centaur and the Airport Centaur); hand-grenades were thrown at Sahar International Airport and at the Fishermen's Colony, Mahim. The object, as the Court recorded, was to overawe the Government established by law, to strike terror among the public at large, and to damage the peace and harmony among different sections of the people. Twenty-seven criminal cases were registered in the police stations concerned.

(b) The conspiracy and the charge-sheet. Investigation disclosed a single conspiracy hatched between December 1992 and 5 April 1993, under which a large quantity of arms — AK-56 rifles, pistols, hand-grenades, ammunition and RDX — was smuggled into India by sea at Dighi Jetty and Shekhadi ports in Maharashtra during January and February 1993, stored at various places (including Village Sansrod in Bharuch district, Gujarat) and distributed to co-accused in Mumbai. Abu Salem was entrusted with the transportation, storage and distribution of the smuggled arms and ammunition, and in the second week of January 1993 he brought AK-56 rifles, ammunition and grenades from Sansrod to Mumbai on the instructions of the absconding accused Anees Ibrahim Kaskar and distributed them among the co-accused. On completion of the investigation a single charge-sheet was filed on 4 November 1993 before the specially created Designated Court at Mumbai against 189 accused persons, including 44 absconders. Abu Salem was one of the absconders.

(c) Absconding and the Red Corner Notice. Because he had absconded, the Designated Court issued Proclamation No. 15777 of 1993 on 15 September 1993; he was declared a proclaimed offender on 15 October 1993; a non-bailable warrant was issued; and the Interpol Secretariat General at Lyons issued Red Corner Notice No. A-103/3-1995. On 19 June 1995 the Designated Court ordered that the evidence of witnesses be recorded against the absconding accused in their absence under section 299 of the Code of Criminal Procedure, 1973 — a step which later became important to the argument about the separation of his trial.

(d) Detention in Lisbon and the request for extradition. On 18 September 2002 Abu Salem was detained by the Portuguese police at Lisbon on the basis of the Red Corner Notice. In December 2002, on receiving intimation of the detention, the Government of India submitted a request for his extradition in nine criminal cases — three of the CBI, two of the Mumbai Police and four of the Delhi Police — relying on the International Convention for the Suppression of Terrorist Bombings and on an assurance of reciprocity as applicable in international law. The relevant facts of the cases were enclosed in duly sworn affidavits of the concerned police officers, the letter of requisition was issued under the signature of the then Minister of State for External Affairs, and the affidavit-in-support was affirmed by the Senior Superintendent of Police, CBI/STF.

(e) The legal basis of the request: no treaty, but a notification. There was no extradition treaty between India and Portugal. The Government of India therefore issued Gazette Notification GSR 822(E) dated 13 December 2002 under section 3 of the Extradition Act, 1962, making the provisions of the Act applicable to the Republic of Portugal except Chapter III — the chapter dealing with the return of fugitive criminals to Commonwealth countries — and doing so expressly on the basis of reciprocity. The Court of Appeals of Lisbon recognised the principle of reciprocity and the applicability of the Act; the Supreme Court of Justice of Portugal granted extradition by order dated 27 January 2005 for the offences mentioned in paragraph 1 of that order; and the Constitutional Court of Portugal approved. Abu Salem (and his co-accused Monica Bedi) were brought to India in November 2005.

(f) The charges, the separation of trial and the challenge. In the Bombay Bomb Blast Case No. 1 of 1993 the Designated Court framed a charge of criminal conspiracy on 18 March 2006 under section 120B of the Penal Code read with sections 3(2)(i), (ii), 3(3), 3(4), 5 and 6 of the Terrorist and Disruptive Activities (Prevention) Act, 1987 (TADA) and numerous Penal Code and Arms Act and Explosives Act provisions; and on 16 April 2007 charges were framed against him under sections 120B, 302, 307, 387 and 382 of the Penal Code and sections 3(2)(i), 3(2)(ii), 3(3), 3(5) and 5 of TADA. By order dated 13 June 2006 the Designated Court separated his trial from the main trial. He appealed under section 19 of TADA and filed a writ petition under Article 32 of the Constitution seeking, among other things, a declaration that the charges were violative of the rule of speciality and of section 21 of the Extradition Act, 1962, and an order prohibiting the respondents from prosecuting him for offences for which he had not been extradited.

3. Issues

  1. Whether the criminal courts in India had jurisdiction to try the appellant in respect of offences which did not form part of the extradition judgment by which he was brought to India — that is, whether the rule of speciality had been violated.
  2. Whether he had been extradited only under the International Convention for the Suppression of Terrorist Bombings, and could therefore be tried only for offences covered by that Convention.
  3. Whether the Extradition Act, 1962 applied to Portugal at all, given the absence of a treaty, and what the effect of Chapter III's exclusion was.
  4. Whether section 21(b) of the Act permits the trial of an extradited person for a "lesser offence" disclosed by the facts on which the extradition was secured, and what "lesser offence" means.
  5. Whether the order separating his trial from the main trial deprived him of the opportunity to cross-examine the witnesses already examined, and was therefore illegal.

4. Arguments of the parties

The appellant (Abu Salem) argued that he had been extradited under the International Convention for the Suppression of Terrorist Bombings and could therefore be tried only for offences related to that Convention; that India had given a solemn sovereign assurance — contained in the letter dated 25 May 2003 of the Indian Ambassador to Portugal — that he would not be prosecuted for offences other than those for which extradition had been sought and would not be re-extradited to any third country; that the charges framed went beyond the extradition decree and violated the rule of speciality, which he said is a universally recognised principle of international law and partakes of the doctrines of double criminality and reciprocity; that the Extradition Act, 1962 had no application to Portugal because there was no treaty and Chapter III had been excluded from the notification; that the trial could not be for offences under TADA, the Explosive Substances Act, 1908 and the Arms Act, 1959; and that the separation of his trial would deprive him of the opportunity of cross-examining the witnesses examined in the main trial.

The respondents (the State of Maharashtra and the Union of India) argued that the extradition had not been granted solely on the basis of the Convention: the requisition itself showed that the primary consideration had been the principle of reciprocity, and the notification of 13 December 2002 had made the Extradition Act, 1962 applicable to Portugal (save Chapter III) precisely in order to give effect to that principle; that the Court of Appeals at Lisbon, the Supreme Court of Justice and the Constitutional Court of Portugal had all approved the applicability of the Act; that the appellant had throughout been treated as a "fugitive criminal" within section 2(f) of the Act; that section 21(b) of the Act permitted the trial of an extradited person not only for the offence for which he was surrendered but also for a lesser offence made out by the facts on which the surrender was secured, and that the additional offences were lesser in punishment and arose from the same set of facts placed before the Government of Portugal; that the Portuguese courts, aware of section 21, had placed no bar on the trial of lesser offences; and that the grievance about the separation of the trial had been met by the order dated 24 August 2009 permitting the appellant to submit a list of witnesses examined in the main trial for cross-examination. The Solicitor General relied in particular on the earlier decision in the Suman Sood case, where it had been held that since extradition had been allowed for a crime punishable with a higher offence (section 364A IPC), the prosecution and trial for a lesser offence (section 365 IPC) could not be held to be without authority of law.

5. The judgment of Sathasivam J.

  1. The Convention was not the sole basis. The Court held that the assumption that the appellant had been extradited only under the Terrorist Bombings Convention "cannot be sustained". If that claim were accepted it would be contrary to the judgment of the Constitutional Court of Portugal, and it ignored the notification of 13 December 2002 applying the Extradition Act, 1962 to Portugal. The Convention was relied upon, but it was not the sole basis of the request; the primary consideration was the assurance of reciprocity, and the notification had been issued under section 3 of the Act keeping that principle in view.
  2. The Act applied, and so did section 21. There being no treaty between India and Portugal, the request had been made under the provisions applicable to non-treaty States, and the appellant had been treated as a fugitive criminal under section 2(f). Because the Act had been applied to Portugal (except Chapter III), "provisions contained in Section 21 of the Act would come into operation while conducting the trial".
  3. What section 21 permits. A bare reading showed that the appellant could be tried for the offences for which he had been extradited; in addition he could be tried for a lesser offence or offences in view of section 21 of the Act, disclosed by the facts proved for the purpose of securing his surrender. "Lesser offence" means an offence which is made out from the proved facts and which provides a lesser punishment as compared with the offences for which the fugitive has been extradited; it must be an extradition offence as defined in section 2(c)(ii) of the Act (an offence punishable with imprisonment of not less than one year under Indian law or the law of a foreign State). The Court added the interpretative point that carries most marks: a lesser offence "cannot be equated with the term minor offence as mentioned in Section 222 of the Code of Criminal Procedure"; the legislature had deliberately used the word "lesser" in section 21(b) rather than "minor", so that "the punishment provided for the offence is relevant and not the ingredients".
  4. Article 2 of the Convention. The contention that the appellant could be tried only for offences under Article 2(1) of the Convention was misconceived: Article 2 covers not only those who commit the substantive offences defined in Article 2(1) but also conspirators and those with constructive liability under Article 2(3); and Article 1(3)(d) shows that "explosive or lethal device" includes a weapon or device designed, or having the capability, to cause death, serious bodily injury or substantial material damage — which covers the AK-56 rifles the appellant was charged with possessing, transporting and distributing.
  5. No violation of the rule of speciality or of the assurance. The Court was satisfied that there had been no violation of the rule of speciality or of the solemn sovereign assurance given by the Ambassador of India on 25 May 2003. The offences additionally charged were lesser than the offences for which the appellant had been extradited; they were disclosed by the same set of facts placed before the Government of Portugal; and the Portuguese courts had placed no bar on the trial of lesser offences, in accordance with section 21(b) of the Act, although they were aware of those provisions.
  6. The separation of trial. The grievance that separation would deprive the appellant of the opportunity to cross-examine witnesses had been dealt with in separate proceedings, and the order dated 24 August 2009 had granted him the opportunity to submit a list of witnesses examined in the main trial for cross-examination; there was therefore no basis for the apprehension.
  7. Result. The appeals and the writ petition were dismissed, and the Designated Court was directed to proceed with the trial expeditiously.

6. The concurring judgment of Ganguly J. — the conceptual map of extradition

Because Sathasivam J. had disposed of the case on the statute, Ganguly J. added a judgment setting out the international law, and it is this part of the decision which is most often quoted. Its twelve points are these:

  1. Extradition is a complex jurisprudential zone, because it contains conflicting ideas: the sovereignty of the State of refuge, the interest of the requesting State, and the rights of the individual.
  2. The problem it solves. A State's criminal jurisdiction generally extends over offences committed within its geographical boundaries, but an offender who flees to another country escapes conviction, which "poses a threat in all civilized countries to a fair adjudication of crime and sustaining the Constitutional norms of Rule of Law".
  3. The remedy. Extradition has been evolved "by way of International treaty obligation which ensures a mode of formal surrender of an accused by the one country to another based on reciprocal arrangements".
  4. Definition. India's Extradition Act, 1962 does not define extradition; the judgment adopts the definition of Chief Justice Fuller in Terlinden v. Ames (184 U.S. 270, at 289) — "the surrender by one nation to another of an individual accused or convicted of an offence outside of its own territory, and within the territorial jurisdiction of the other, which, being competent to try and to punish him, demands the surrender" — and notes that it echoes the formulation of Professor Bassiouni, for whom extradition is "a formal process through which a person is surrendered by one State to another by virtue of a treaty, reciprocity or comity as between the respective States".
  5. Extradition distinguished from deportation and exclusion. "But extradition is different from deportation by which competent State authorities order a person to leave a country and prevent him from returning to the same territory. Extradition is also different from exclusion, by which an individual is prohibited from staying in one part of a Sovereign State." As a result of such orders, deserters or absentees from the armed forces are sometimes returned to the custody of the country to which they belong.
  6. Consent is the dividing line. "Both deportation and exclusion basically are non-consensual exercise whereas extradition is based on a consensual treaty obligation between the requesting State and the requested State."
  7. Seriousness and expedition. Extradition "is only to be resorted to in cases of serious offences", and Lord Templeman's observation in Re Evans, (1994) 3 All E.R. 449, at 450–451, is approved: extradition treaties and legislation are designed to combine speed and justice.
  8. The human-rights balance. In the post-war period two divergent trends — the expansion of human-rights protection and the need to curb transnational crime — must be reconciled, and the leading statement is that of the European Court of Human Rights in Soering v. United Kingdom, (1989) 11 E.H.R.R. 439: there is "a search for a fair balance between the demands of the general interest of the community and the requirements of the protection of the individual's fundamental rights", and "the establishment of safe havens for fugitives would not only result in danger for the State obliged to harbour the protected person but also tend to undermine the foundations of extradition".
  9. Extradition law is an amalgam of international and national law: the procedure is governed by the municipal law of the requested State, even though the obligation is international.
  10. The rule of non-inquiry. "Normally in extradition law the requested State is to follow the rule of Non-Inquiry which means that the requested State is not to normally make inquiry about the nature of criminal justice system in the requesting State. That is why in this case, on a complaint being made by Abu Salem in the Court of the requested country, the Courts of Portugal await the decision of this Court." The rule is "a well developed norm both in Canada and in America", and the judgment quotes La Forest J. in Canada v. Schmidt, (1987) 1 S.C.R. 500: "I see nothing unjust in surrendering to a foreign country a person accused of having committed a crime there for trial in the ordinary way in accordance with the system for the administration of justice prevailing in that country simply because that system is substantially different from ours with different checks and balances. The judicial process in a foreign country must not be subjected to finicky evaluations against the rules governing the legal process in this country."
  11. The fugitive's standing. Whether the extradited person has any standing to complain about the judicial process in the requesting State, independently of the position taken by the requested State, "is a debatable issue"; it is part of the larger debate about the individual as a subject of international law. Abu Salem's complaint — that he was tried for offences described as "lesser offences", and that his trial was separated — was answered by the rule of non-inquiry.
  12. But non-inquiry is not absolute. "In a given situation, the requested State may question the procedures in the requesting State if they are prima facie contrary to fundamental principles of justice and there is a high risk of the fugitive being prejudiced by the process of extradition."

7. The principle evolved

  • The rule of speciality, as applied in India, permits trial for lesser offences. Section 21(b) of the Extradition Act allows an extradited person to be tried for an offence lesser than the offences for which he was surrendered, provided it is made out by the facts proved for the purpose of securing the surrender and is itself an extraditable offence. "Lesser" is measured by punishment, not by the ingredients of the offence, and is not the same as a "minor offence" under section 222 of the Code of Criminal Procedure.
  • The Act may be applied to non-treaty States by notification under section 3, on the basis of reciprocity. The surrender of a fugitive does not require a bilateral treaty if the domestic statute can be applied by notification and the requesting State offers reciprocity.
  • A multilateral convention may furnish the basis of the request. The Terrorist Bombings Convention was relied upon; the Court's point was not that it was unavailable, but that it was not the sole basis.
  • Assurances bind the requesting State and are justiciable to the extent of the rule of speciality. The "solemn sovereign assurance" of 25 May 2003 was treated as a term on which the surrender rested; on the facts, the additional charges did not breach it.
  • The rule of non-inquiry. The requesting State's system and conduct of the trial will not ordinarily be reviewed by the requested State or by the courts of the requesting State at the fugitive's instance — but the rule yields to fundamental principles of justice and to a high risk of prejudice.
Postscript — what happened afterwards (context only; verify before citing)

The judgment of 2010 was not the end of the matter. In 2012 Abu Salem and Monica Bedi were convicted in a passport case; in 2017 the special court trying the 1993 blasts case sentenced Abu Salem to life imprisonment in the blasts case. In 2015 a Portuguese court annulled the extradition in proceedings in Portugal, and India declined to return him, taking the position that its own courts would deal with him — an outcome which illustrates Lord Templeman's warning about the cost of delay and the practical durability of the rule in the Savarkar award. In 2023 and 2024 there were press reports of arbitral proceedings and awards (dated 5 September 2023 and 3 July 2024) arising out of the alleged breach of the assurances given to Portugal, and of continuing appellate litigation in India. These developments are noted as illustration only: they are not part of the case material, and their details should be verified from the current record before being used in an answer.

G. Case 39 — Arrest and Return of Savarkar (France v. Great Britain), P.C.A., 1911

Arrest and Return of Savarkar (Vinayak Damodar Savarkar) — the "Savarkar case"

Permanent Court of Arbitration, The Hague: Arbitration Agreement between the United Kingdom and France signed at London on 25 October 1910; Award delivered 24 February 1911 · Printed in the DU case material at pp. 186–189, together with the text of the Agreement and the Award · Reported at Hague Court Reports, p. 275; discussed in S.K. Verma's Chapter 9 (extradition), in the class notes and in the Exam Capsule

Provenance. The account below follows the Agreement and the Award as printed in the case material, together with the summaries in S.K. Verma, Chapter 9, the class notes and the Exam Capsule. Quotations in this block are taken from the Award's own text as printed.

1. Why the case is in the syllabus

Savarkar is the first great international case about the manner in which a fugitive is recovered. It is prescribed because it answers a question which arises the moment the machinery of extradition is bypassed: if a State obtains custody of a person irregularly — without a request, without a warrant, without the forms of extradition, and by the act of a foreign official who has no authority to hand him over — is it obliged to give him back? The Tribunal said no. The case is therefore the mirror image of Abu Salem: Abu Salem shows what happens when extradition is followed and the requesting State must respect its terms; Savarkar shows what happens when extradition is not followed at all. And it is the authority cited in the very footnote in which Verma discusses the abduction of Eichmann — the point at which this topic meets the law of universal jurisdiction.

2. Facts

(a) The background. Vinayak Damodar Savarkar was an Indian revolutionary and, at the material time, a British Indian subject who had been living in London. He was to be sent to India to be prosecuted for "abetment of murder etc." By a letter of 29 June 1910, the Commissioner of the Metropolitan Police in London informed the Directeur de la Sûreté générale at Paris that Savarkar was about to be sent to India and that he would be on board the vessel Morea, touching at Marseilles on 7 or 8 July.

(b) The French arrangements. In consequence of that letter, the Ministry of the Interior informed the Prefect of the Bouches-du-Rhône by telegram dated 4 July 1910 that the British police were sending Savarkar to India on board the steamship Morea; the telegram stated that some "révolutionnaires hindus" then on the Continent might take advantage of this to further the escape of the foreigner, and asked the Prefect to take the necessary measures to guard against any attempt of that kind. The Directeur de la Sûreté générale replied by letter dated 9 July 1910 that he had given the necessary instructions to guard against any incident during the presence of Savarkar at Marseilles on board the Morea.

(c) The escape and the arrest. The Morea arrived at Marseilles on 7 July 1910. On the following morning, between 6 and 7 o'clock, Savarkar succeeded in effecting his escape: he got out of a porthole of the steamer, threw himself into the sea and swam ashore to the quay, and began to run. He was arrested by a brigadier of the French maritime gendarmerie and taken back to the vessel; three persons who had come ashore from the vessel assisted the brigadier in taking the fugitive back. The Morea left Marseilles with Savarkar on board on 9 July.

(d) The brigadier's account. From the statements made by the French brigadier to the police at Marseilles it appeared that he saw the fugitive, who was almost naked, get out of a porthole, throw himself into the sea and swim to the quay; that at the same moment some persons from the ship, shouting and gesticulating, rushed over the bridge leading to the shore in order to pursue him; that a number of people on the quay began to shout "Arrêtez-le"; and that the brigadier at once went in pursuit of the fugitive and, coming up to him after running about five hundred metres, arrested him. The brigadier declared that he was altogether unaware of the identity of the person with whom he was dealing, thinking only that the escaping man was one of the crew who had possibly committed an offence on board the vessel. As to the assistance afforded him by one of the crew and two Indian policemen, the explanations showed that these men came up after the arrest and that their intervention was only auxiliary to the action of the brigadier. The brigadier seized Savarkar by one arm to take him back to the ship; the prisoner went peaceably with him; the brigadier, assisted by the others, did not relax his hold until they reached the half-deck of the vessel; and he said that he did not know English. The incident occupied only a few minutes.

(e) The contested circumstances. It was alleged that the brigadier who effected the arrest was not ignorant of Savarkar's presence on board the vessel, and that his orders, like those of all the French police and gendarmes, were to prevent any Hindu from coming on board who had not got a ticket. The Tribunal found that these circumstances showed that the persons on board in charge of Savarkar "might well have believed that they could count on the assistance of the French Police". It was established that a Commissaire of the French police came on board the vessel shortly after her arrival at the port and, in accordance with the orders of the Prefect, placed himself at the disposal of the Commander in respect of the watch to be kept; that the Commissaire was accordingly put in communication with the British police officer who, with other police officers, was in charge of the prisoner; and that the Prefect, by telegram of 13 July 1910 to the Minister of the Interior, stated that he had acted in the matter in accordance with the instructions given by the Sûreté générale to make the necessary arrangements to prevent the escape of Savarkar.

(f) The diplomatic claim and the reference to arbitration. France demanded that Great Britain restore Savarkar — the demand being that he should be returned so that the rules of extradition could be complied with — and Great Britain refused. The two Governments therefore concluded the Agreement signed at London on 25 October 1910, submitting to arbitration "the questions of fact and law raised by the arrest and restoration to the mail-steamer Morea at Marseilles, on the 8th July 1910, of the British Indian Savarkar, who had escaped from that vessel where he was in custody; and the demand made by the Government of the French Republic for the restitution of Savarkar". By Article 1 of the Agreement the question for the Tribunal was: "Should Vinayak Damodar Savarkar, in conformity with the rules of international law, be restored or not be restored by His Britannic Majesty's Government to the Government of the French Republic?" By Article 2 the Tribunal was to be composed of five arbitrators chosen from the members of the Permanent Court at The Hague, the composition to be settled by the two Contracting Parties, each of which might choose as arbitrator one of its nationals. A note of the same date from Sir Edward Grey recorded the understanding that any points arising in the course of the arbitration not covered by the Agreement would be determined by the International Convention for the Pacific Settlement of International Disputes signed at The Hague on 18 October 1907.

3. Issues

  1. Whether, on the facts, the arrest of Savarkar by a French gendarme and his delivery to the British police on board the Morea involved anything in the nature of a violation of the sovereignty of France;
  2. Whether the brigadier's act was authorised, ratified or acquiesced in by his superiors, and what inference the British authorities were entitled to draw from the silence of the French authorities before the vessel sailed;
  3. Whether, assuming an irregularity, a rule of international law obliged the State which had custody of the prisoner to restore him because of the mistake of the foreign agent who had delivered him up; and
  4. The consequential question raised by the Agreement — "be restored or not be restored" — which required the Tribunal to state the legal position definitively, and not merely to apportion blame.

4. Arguments of the parties

France contended that the arrest and the delivery of Savarkar to the British authorities had been effected without any of the forms of extradition; that a French agent had, by his own act, exercised authority over a person who was on board a British vessel and had handed him over to the British; that the manner of the recovery was therefore an irregular exercise of French authority, done without the authority of the French Government, which had the effect of depriving France of the opportunity to deal with the case according to law; and that international law required that the person be restored so that the position could be regularised — in particular so that a proper request for extradition could be considered. France also relied on the surrounding circumstances — the presence of a French Commissaire on board, the instructions given to the Prefect, and the suggestion that the brigadier knew, or ought to have known, who Savarkar was and that his orders included preventing such persons from coming ashore — in order to show that the act was not a mere private misadventure but an act with an official colour.

Great Britain contended that there had been no violation of French sovereignty: the French authorities had themselves made arrangements to prevent Savarkar's escape and the British police were entitled to assume that the arrest, made by a French officer in the execution of his duty, was regular; that all concerned had acted in good faith and without any intention of doing anything unlawful; that the case was not one of recourse to fraud or force to obtain possession of a person who had taken refuge in foreign territory; and that even if the arrest and delivery were irregular, no rule of international law required the restitution of a prisoner in those circumstances — the remedy, if any, being satisfaction and not restoration.

5. The Award

The Tribunal began with the question as stated in the Agreement and then stated the facts at length. On the legal question it reasoned as follows, in the passages which the case material prints:

"Whereas, having regard to what has been stated, it is manifest that the case is not one of recourse to fraud or force in order to obtain possession of a person who had taken refuge in foreign territory, and that there was not, in the circumstances of the arrest and delivery of Savarkar to the British Authorities and of his removal to India, anything in the nature of a violation of the sovereignty of France, and that all those who took part in the matter certainly acted in good faith and had no thought of doing anything unlawful."

"Whereas, in the circumstances cited above, the conduct of the brigadier not having been disclaimed by his chiefs before the morning of the 9th July, that is to say before the Morea left Marseilles, the British Police might naturally have believed that the brigadier had acted in accordance with his instructions, or that his conduct had been approved."

"Whereas, while admitting that an irregularity was committed by the arrest of Savarkar, and by his being handed over to the British Police, there is no rule of International Law imposing, in circumstances such as those which have been set out above, any obligation on the Power which has in its custody a prisoner, to restore him because of a mistake committed by the foreign agent who delivered him up to that Power."

"FOR THESE REASONS: The Arbitral Tribunal decides that the Government of His Britannic Majesty is not required to restore the said Vinayak Damodar Savarkar to the Government of the French Republic. Done at The Hague, at the Permanent Court of Arbitration, February 24th, 1911."

6. The principle evolved

  1. Irregular recovery does not defeat the receiving State's jurisdiction. The "Savarkar principle", as it is usually called, is that a State which has custody of a person is not bound under international law to restore him merely because he was delivered up irregularly or as the result of a mistake by an agent of the surrendering State. The principle is the ancestor of the modern male capto bene judicato rule and of the American decisions in Yunis and Alvarez-Machain.
  2. But the irregularity is an irregularity. The Tribunal expressly "admitted that an irregularity was committed". The absence of a duty to restore is not a licence: a serious violation of the sovereignty of the surrendering State — a recourse to fraud or force, as the Tribunal was careful to say this case was not — would engage the international responsibility of the recovering State. In Eichmann, the abduction from Buenos Aires produced Security Council Resolution 138 (1960) and a formal settlement with Argentina, even though the Israeli courts could lawfully try him.
  3. Good faith and the appearance of authority matter. The Tribunal attached decisive weight to two facts: that all concerned acted in good faith and without any thought of doing anything unlawful, and that the brigadier's conduct had not been disclaimed by his superiors before the vessel sailed, so that the British police "might naturally have believed" that he acted on instructions or that his conduct had been approved. The case therefore also illustrates how a State's silence and inaction can estop it, or at least affect the legal assessment of what was done in its name.
  4. It is a case about extradition by its absence. The whole dispute arises because no extradition procedure was used. Verma cites the case for that reason in the chapter on extradition: there is no duty to extradite in the absence of a treaty, "but if extradited, even in an irregular manner, the receiving State is not bound to return the fugitive". The class notes make exactly the same point: "international law does not impose any obligation upon the State whereby on the above ground the criminal may be returned." The modern rule of law has qualified the Savarkar principle in two ways — by the human-rights limits on the treatment of persons once surrendered (Soering, Chahal) and by the treaty clauses which require a State to prosecute where it does not extradite — but the core proposition remains good law, and it is the reason why abductions are met with diplomatic protest rather than with orders for restitution.
  5. The procedural model. The case is also worth remembering as an example of the peaceful settlement of disputes: a difference between two Great Powers about the treatment of an Indian revolutionary — a matter on which public feeling in India ran high — was resolved by an arbitration agreement of two articles, a five-member tribunal drawn from the panel of the Permanent Court of Arbitration, and an award in eleven months, under the 1907 Hague Convention for the Pacific Settlement of International Disputes.

Two collateral facts complete the record. Savarkar was thereafter tried in India, convicted and transported — the case which bears his name is the last legal act in a political drama, and it was tried at The Hague, not in London or Calcutta. And the award was one of the early decisions of the Permanent Court of Arbitration, which is why the case is cited in the casebook under the heading "Arrest and Return of Savarkar (France v. Great Britain) PCA, 1911" and why the case material prints the Agreement of 25 October 1910 and the Award of 24 February 1911 together.

Common error to avoid in the Savarkar question

Candidates often write that the Tribunal "held that France had violated international law" or that "Great Britain was guilty of an unlawful arrest". Neither is correct. The Tribunal found no violation of French sovereignty, and it admitted only that "an irregularity was committed by the arrest of Savarkar, and by his being handed over to the British Police". The operative finding is expressed as a negative duty on the State with custody: there is no rule imposing on it an obligation to restore the prisoner because of a mistake committed by the foreign agent who delivered him up. Note also the shape of the arbitration: the question put was framed as a choice (should he be restored or not be restored), so the award had to be, and was, categorical.
