H. Deportation and the Statutory Machinery of Expulsion

The Indian law of expulsion and entry control

Deportation in Indian law is not an international-law remedy at all; it is the exercise of the sovereign power to control the entry and residence of aliens, and it rests on a cluster of statutes and subordinate legislation:

  • The Foreigners Act, 1946 — the central instrument. Section 3 confers on the Central Government the power, by order, to make provision for prohibiting, regulating or restricting the entry of foreigners into India, their presence, and their departure; to require a foreigner to depart from India within a specified time, and to prohibit his return without permission; and to provide for his removal from India. The Act's definition of a "foreigner" is the key: a person who is not a citizen of India (s. 2(a)). The Foreigners Order, 1948 made under it regulates entry, residence and departure; and the Foreigners (Tribunals) Order, 1964 (as amended in 2019) provides for tribunals to decide whether a person is a foreigner — the procedure at the centre of the Assam nationality litigation.
  • The burden of proof. Sections 8 and 9 of the Foreigners Act place on the person concerned the burden of proving that he is not a foreigner — a reversal which the Supreme Court has upheld as reasonable in the context of infiltration, and which explains why a writ of habeas corpus by a detained person will ordinarily fail unless he establishes citizenship.
  • The Passport (Entry into India) Act, 1920 and the Registration of Foreigners Act, 1939 with the Registration of Foreigners Rules, 1992 — the documentary and registration controls; the Passport Act, 1967 and the Passports Rules for the issue, impounding and revocation of passports (relevant because cancellation of a fugitive's passport is often the prelude to his deportation); and the Bharatiya Nagarik Suraksha Sanhita, 2023 (successor to ss. 41 and 57 of the Code of Criminal Procedure) for the police power to arrest and the outer limit of detention.
  • Judicial review. Deportation is an executive act within the police power of the State, and the courts do not review it on the merits, but it is subject to Article 21 (procedure established by law, and the right against arbitrary action) and to the rule that India will not deport a person to a State where he faces a real risk of torture — the point at which deportation meets the non-refoulement principle discussed in section K.
  • Historical lesson — the IMDT Act. In Sarbananda Sonowal v. Union of India, (2005) 5 SCC 665, the Supreme Court struck down the Illegal Migrants (Determination by Tribunals) Act, 1983 for placing an impossible burden on the complainant and thereby making the detection and deportation of illegal migrants impossible; the Court held that the right to the integrity of the nation is a facet of Article 21 and that the Act was unconstitutional. The case shows how closely the machinery of expulsion is bound up with citizenship and constitutional law, and why the Foreigners Act's burden-of-proof provisions matter.

Extradition and deportation — the six differences that examiners want

  1. Source. Extradition rests on a treaty, a convention treated as a treaty under s. 3(4), or a notified arrangement; deportation rests on the sovereign power over aliens, exercised under the Foreigners Act, 1946 and the orders made under it.
  2. Request and initiative. Extradition requires a requisition from the requesting State (s. 4) and a judicial inquiry (s. 7); deportation is initiated by the executive of the State where the alien is, without any request from another State.
  3. Purpose. Extradition is for trial or punishment of a specific offence and is confined to that offence by the speciality rule; deportation removes a person from the territory, and is indifferent to the criminal law of the destination State.
  4. Safeguards. Extradition is attended by the prima facie requirement, the political-offence exception, the time-bar rule and the speciality rule; deportation has none of these, which is why deportation is used for persons who could not be extradited (including those who may face ill-treatment) — and why the practice is criticised.
  5. Consequences. A person who resists extradition fights the request in the requested State; a person deported is simply removed, and may be prosecuted in the receiving State or not, as that State chooses.
  6. Legal character. Extradition is the performance of an international obligation and its breach engages State responsibility; deportation is the exercise of a domestic power whose abuse may violate the human rights of the individual and, in the case of refoulement to torture, the Torture Convention and customary law.

↑ top

I. The Political-Offence Exception

Every extradition system contains an exception which is as old as extradition itself: a person who has taken refuge abroad for political reasons will not be handed back to the State he has offended politically. The exception is the point at which the interests of the two States are displaced by the interests of the individual and by the principle of non-intervention in another State's political conflicts. It is also the most elastic concept in the whole subject, and the examinable questions are therefore: what is a political offence, who decides, and what has happened to the exception in the age of terrorism?

1. The two theories and the four tests

  1. Absolute (or pure) political offence. An offence which is purely political — sedition, treason, rebellion, espionage, illegal political association — is never extraditable, whatever its consequences.
  2. Relative (or mixed) political offence. An ordinary crime committed in the course of, or for the purposes of, a political struggle — the murderer or robber who acts as an insurgent — is protected if the political element predominates. The difficulty is precisely the measurement of predominance.
  3. The incidence test — Re Castioni, [1891] 1 Q.B. 149. Castioni shot a government official during a political riot in the Swiss canton of Ticino. The English court refused extradition because the act was "incidental to and forming part of a political disturbance" — an act done in the course of, and in furtherance of, a political movement. The test asks whether the offence is incidental to a political struggle; and it is the ancestor of the American "incidence test" applied in Eain v. Wilkes, 641 F.2d 504 (7th Cir. 1981), and refined in Quinn v. Robinson, 783 F.2d 776 (9th Cir. 1986).
  4. The anti-social test — Re Meunier, [1894] 2 Q.B. 415. The anarchist Meunier, whose bomb attack caused deaths, was held not to be protected, because "the offence must be committed in the course of a struggle between two parties in the State for political power"; an attack aimed at all government, or at society at large, has no political character. This is the beginning of the modern restriction of the exception.
  5. The purpose test — R. v. Governor of Brixton Prison, ex parte Kolczynski, [1955] 1 Q.B. 540. Seven members of the crew of a Polish trawler, who had seized the ship and put to sea to escape their Communist authorities, were held not extraditable: the "test is whether the offence is of a political character… whether as between the requesting State and the accused there was a conflict of a political nature". The purpose and the context of the whole episode decided the case, not the physical character of the acts.
  6. The relative-proportionality refinement — Cheng v. Governor of Pentonville Prison, [1973] A.C. 931. The House of Lords limited the exception where the alleged offence was, on the requesting State's own case, an ordinary crime (there, drug trafficking and intimidation) rather than a political act; the court must look at the actual conduct alleged, not the labels, and the exception will not protect a person whose motive is private gain. The modern European practice adds a proportionality element, most explicitly in the "relative" version of the theory, and the Supreme Court of Canada in United States v. Shephard, [1977] 2 S.C.R. 1067, and the UK courts in T. v. Secretary of State, [1996] 2 All E.R. 865, narrowed it further.

2. The attentat clause — the first statutory exception

Because the absolute theory would shelter the assassins of crowned heads, Belgium introduced in 1856 an exception — the clause attentat — by which an attack on the life of a foreign Head of State or of his family ceased to be a political offence for extradition purposes. The clause, born of the attempt on the life of Napoleon III, spread through the extradition treaties of Europe, and it is the historical model for the schedules of non-political offences which every modern treaty contains.

3. The modern narrowing — treaties and the Schedule

Three techniques have reduced the exception without abolishing it:

  • Express declaration in the treaty. The Genocide Convention, Art. VII ("Genocide and the other acts enumerated in article III shall not be considered as political crimes for the purpose of extradition"); Resolution 3074 (XXVIII) of 1973 of the General Assembly on the principles of international cooperation in the detection, arrest, extradition and punishment of persons guilty of war crimes and crimes against humanity, which declares that such offenders are to be treated as extraditable even if the requesting State has no treaty with the requested State.
  • The sectoral conventions: the Hague Convention, 1970; the Montreal Convention, 1971; the Hostages Convention, 1979 (which, in Art. 9, includes the "purpose" safeguard — a request made to prosecute a person on account of his race, religion, nationality, ethnic origin or political opinion is not a ground for extradition, though the convention excludes the political-offence defence); the Torture Convention, 1984 (Art. 1 gives its true target — the acts must be done "by or at the instigation of or with the consent or acquiescence of a public official", and torture is not to be characterised as a political offence by implication from Art. 7); the Terrorist Bombings Convention, 1997 (Art. 11); the SUA Convention (Art. 11); the UNTOC (Art. 16(14)) and the UNCAC (Art. 44(15)), both of which preserve a "purpose safeguard" of this kind.
  • The recast of the offence list. The European Arrest Warrant abolished the political-offence exception altogether between the member States of the Union, and a modern treaty will normally list the offences for which extradition may not be refused (murder, kidnapping, hostage-taking, explosive offences, terrorism and offences under the suppression conventions).

The Indian statute uses the third technique, with a list of its own:

Section 31(1)(a) and the Schedule to the Extradition Act, 1962

The fugitive criminal shall not be surrendered "if the offence in respect of which his surrender is sought is of a political character or if he proves to the satisfaction of the magistrate or court before whom he may be produced or of the Central Government that the requisition or warrant for his surrender has, in fact, been made with a view to try or punish him for an offence of a political character" (s. 31(1)(a)). Section 31(2) provides that "the offence specified in the Schedule shall not be regarded as offences of a political character"; s. 31(3) empowers the Central Government to add to or omit from the list by notified order; and s. 32 makes ss. 29 and 31 apply to every foreign State without modification.

The Schedule (as substituted by Act 66 of 1993) lists: (1) offences under the Anti-Hijacking Act, 1982; (2) offences under the Suppression of Unlawful Acts against Safety of Civil Aviation Act, 1982; (3) offences within the scope of the 1973 Convention on Internationally Protected Persons (diplomatic agents); (4) offences within the scope of the Hostages Convention, 1979; (5) culpable homicide and murder (IPC ss. 299–304); (6) voluntarily causing hurt or grievous hurt by a dangerous weapon or means (ss. 321–333); (7) offences under the Explosive Substances Act, 1908; (8) possession of a firearm or ammunition with intent to endanger life (s. 27, Arms Act, 1959); (9) use of a firearm to resist arrest (s. 28, Arms Act, 1959); (10) causing loss or damage to public utility property with intent to endanger life (ss. 425 and 440); (11) wrongful restraint and wrongful confinement (ss. 339–348); (12) kidnapping and abduction including hostage-taking (ss. 359–369); (13) offences relating to terrorism and terrorist acts under TADA, 1987; and (14) abetment, conspiracy, attempt, incitement or complicity in any of those offences. Note for the present day: the Schedule still carries its 1993 references. The offences it lists now arise under the Anti-Hijacking Act, 2016 and the Unlawful Activities (Prevention) Act, 1967 in place of the 1982 and 1987 Acts and, so far as the Penal Code is concerned, under the corresponding provisions of the Bharatiya Nyaya Sanhita, 2023. Section 31(3) is the mechanism by which the list is kept current, and the substance of the exception survives for all offences not listed.

The second limb of s. 31(1)(a) is often forgotten: even if the offence is not political in character, the fugitive is protected if he proves that the requisition was in fact made with a view to try or punish him for an offence of a political character — the "disguised extradition" safeguard, which is the same idea as the purpose clause in the modern conventions.

4. India's practice and the reason the exception survives

In Indian practice the political-offence exception is applied by the magistrate under s. 7(2) and s. 31(1)(a) — the fugitive is expressly entitled to lead evidence to show that the offence is of a political character — and, at the last stage, by the Central Government under s. 29, which may decline to surrender "for political reasons or otherwise". The exception has been most discussed in respect of political offenders from neighbouring States; and it is the reason why States which would not surrender a political fugitive sometimes remove him instead by deportation: the removal of an alien requires no offence at all, and therefore engages no exception. That practice — the "deportation in lieu of extradition" of a political opponent — is the principal criticism of the deportation device in international human-rights law, and it explains why the European Court of Human Rights and the UN Human Rights Committee insist that the manner of removal cannot be used to circumvent the safeguards of extradition.

J. Asylum — Territorial and Extra-Territorial (Diplomatic)

Asylum is the protection which a State grants, within its territory or in a place under its control, to a person who has fled from another State. The class notes put it in one line: "Asylum means the protection or refuge granted by a State… Asylum is linked with extradition inasmuch as asylum stops where extradition or rendition begins… Therefore the concept of asylum and extradition are opposite to each other." The class notes add the two propositions which the examiner expects: (i) the offender has no right of asylum as against a State — asylum is the right of the State to grant protection, and the reasonable corollary is that the individual has, under Article 14(1) of the Universal Declaration of Human Rights, "the right to seek and to enjoy in other countries asylum from persecution"; and (ii) the General Assembly adopted the Declaration on Territorial Asylum in 1967 (Resolution 2312 (XXII) of 14 December 1967), Article 1 of which states that asylum granted by a State in the exercise of its sovereignty "shall be respected by all other States" and Article 3 of which provides that no person may be subjected to measures such as rejection at the frontier or expulsion to a State where he may be persecuted.

1. The two kinds of asylum

Table 6.11 · Territorial and extra-territorial (diplomatic) asylum
PointTerritorial asylumExtra-territorial / diplomatic asylum
DefinitionRefuge given by a State to a person within its own territory — on land, or on board its ships and aircraft, or in places under its jurisdiction.Refuge given to a person who remains within the territory of another State but is received in a diplomatic mission, consulate, warship or international premises.
Legal basisAn attribute of territorial sovereignty: the State may admit, refuse or remove aliens within the limits of international law (the Foreigners Act, 1946 in India).No general basis. Diplomatic asylum is a regional (Latin American) institution based on treaty; outside it, the mission is inviolable but is not permitted to shelter persons against the local State: Article 41(3) of the Vienna Convention on Diplomatic Relations, 1961 requires the premises to be used only for functions compatible with the mission.
InstrumentsThe 1951 Refugee Convention and its 1967 Protocol; the 1967 UNGA Declaration on Territorial Asylum; the OAU Convention, 1969; the Cartagena Declaration, 1984; the Global Compact on Refugees, 2018; the ILC's Draft Articles on the Expulsion of Aliens, 2014 (see §6.5 K).The Havana Convention on Asylum, 1928; the Montevideo Convention on Political Asylum, 1933; the Caracas Convention on Diplomatic Asylum, 1954; the practice of Latin American States.
Leading authorityThe Refugee Convention's definition (Art. 1) and the principle of non-refoulement (Art. 33); Hirsi Jamaa v. Italy (2012) for the reach of the principle beyond the frontier.Asylum Case (Colombia v. Peru), I.C.J. Reports 1950, p. 266; Haya de la Torre (Colombia v. Peru), I.C.J. Reports 1951, p. 71.
Modern examplesTibetan and Sri Lankan refugees in India; Rohingya in Bangladesh and India; Syrian refugees under the UNHCR mandate and the 1951 Convention in Europe and Jordan; the "external processing" arrangements of States (which raise the same questions as interception).Cardinal Mindszenty in the United States legation in Budapest (1956–1971); the 1980 Peruvian embassy in Havana; Julian Assange in the Ecuadorian embassy in London (2012–2019); the practice of grants of "safe passage" instead of asylum.

2. The Asylum and Haya de la Torre cases — the truth about diplomatic asylum

Facts. In October 1948 a military rebellion in Peru failed and proceedings were begun against its leader, Víctor Raúl Haya de la Torre, of the American People's Revolutionary Alliance (APRA). Warrants were issued for his arrest, and on 3 January 1949 he sought refuge in the Colombian embassy at Lima. He remained there for five years. Colombia asserted the right, as the State granting asylum, to "qualify" the offence unilaterally as a political offence, and asked Peru for a safe-conduct to allow him to leave. Peru refused to recognise the asylum, and the two States referred the dispute to the International Court of Justice.

The Asylum Case (Colombia v. Peru), I.C.J. Reports 1950, p. 266. The Court held, on Colombia's claim of a regional custom obliging Peru to respect asylum granted by Colombia: the facts disclosed "so much contradiction and fluctuation" in the practice of the Latin American States, "so much inconsistency in the rapid succession of conventions on asylum, ratified by some States and rejected by others, and the practice [was] so much influenced by considerations of political expediency", that no such custom could be established; the rule of unilateral qualification ("that the State granting asylum is competent to qualify the nature of the offence by a unilateral and definitive decision") was not proved to exist; Peru was not bound to grant the safe-conduct; and the asylum had therefore been granted without legal authority, and Colombia was under a duty to terminate it. The Court did accept, however, that a grant of asylum made in accordance with the conventions would be respected, and it recorded the undertaking that Haya de la Torre would not be surrendered or expelled.

The Haya de la Torre Case (Colombia v. Peru), I.C.J. Reports 1951, p. 71. Colombia then asked how the asylum was to be terminated, and in particular whether it was obliged to surrender Haya de la Torre; Peru counter-claimed that he should be handed over. The Court, by thirteen votes to one, declined to order surrender: it held that the obligation to terminate the asylum did not entail an obligation to surrender the refugee to the local authorities, and that the choice among the means of termination was left to the parties' discretion, exercised in the manner contemplated by the Havana Convention and by international courtesy. Diplomatic protection and negotiation filled the gap: Haya de la Torre left the embassy in 1954 and was allowed to go into exile. The two judgments together are the leading authority on three propositions which examiners test: (i) diplomatic asylum is not a general rule of customary international law but a treaty institution of the Latin American States; (ii) the State granting asylum may not unilaterally qualify the offence, unless the treaty so provides; and (iii) even where the asylum is irregular, international law does not require the surrender of the refugee to the territorial State, and the mode of terminating the asylum is a matter for negotiation and comity. Both cases are also in the syllabus of Topic 5's companion head "Territorial Asylum" (see Smart Notes: "Diplomatic Asylum; Asylum Case and Haya de la Torre") and are therefore worth cross-referring in the answer.

Asylum and the absent State — the Indian position in one paragraph

India grants asylum as a matter of executive policy, not of statute: it is not a party to the 1951 Refugee Convention or the 1967 Protocol, has no asylum legislation, and deals with refugees under the Foreigners Act, 1946 and administrative instructions issued by the Ministry of Home Affairs. The practice includes the grant of refuge to Tibetan refugees from 1959 onwards and to Sri Lankan Tamils from 1983 onwards, the presence of UNHCR offices and their role in status determination for some groups, the registration arrangements for certain refugee populations, and long-standing government undertakings not to return particular groups. The legal questions which arise — whether non-refoulement is part of Article 21, how far the executive may deport a person who claims refugee status, and whether there is an obligation to regularise the status of long-staying refugees — are dealt with in §6.5 K and §6.5 L.

K. Non-Refoulement and the Refugee Regime

The refugee regime is the limit which international law places on both extradition and deportation. It answers the question: even where the requested State may lawfully surrender or remove a person, is there a class of persons whom it may not send back?

1. The 1951 Convention and its 1967 Protocol

The definition (Art. 1A(2)). A refugee is a person who, "owing to well-founded fear of being persecuted for reasons of race, religion, nationality, membership of a particular social group or political opinion, is outside the country of his nationality and is unable, or owing to such fear, is unwilling to avail himself of the protection of that country". The definition has five moving parts — well-founded fear, persecution, a Convention ground, being outside the country, and the absence of (or unwillingness to use) national protection.

The cut-off dates and the 1967 Protocol. Article 1B limited the Convention to persons who became refugees "as a result of events occurring before 1 January 1951" and allowed States to declare whether they meant events in Europe only; the 1967 Protocol removed both limitations, and it is the Protocol that makes the Convention a universal instrument. Article 1C (cessation) and Article 1D–1E (other categories) complete the definitions.

The exclusion clauses (Art. 1F). The Convention does not apply to a person who has committed a crime against peace, a war crime, or a crime against humanity; a serious non-political crime outside the country of refuge before admission as a refugee; or acts contrary to the purposes and principles of the United Nations. This is the point at which the law of asylum and the law of universal jurisdiction meet: the persons who are the objects of universal jurisdiction are the persons excluded from refugee protection — and the two ideas are the joint product of the same post-war settlement.

Status and rights. Non-discrimination (Art. 3); religious freedom (Art. 4); the prohibition of penalties for illegal entry (Art. 31 — "The Contracting States shall not impose penalties, on account of their illegal entry or presence, on refugees who, coming directly from a territory where their life or freedom was threatened… enter or are present in their territory without authorization, provided they present themselves without delay to the authorities and show good cause for their illegal entry or presence"); the prohibition of expulsion except on national-security or public-order grounds and only "in pursuance of a decision reached in accordance with due process of law" with a right of appeal (Art. 32); access to the courts (Art. 16), wage-earning employment (Art. 17), public education (Art. 22), identity and travel documents (Arts. 27–28), and the obligation of States to facilitate assimilation and naturalisation (Art. 34). The 1951 Convention is a Charter of rights for the refugee; but the practical core of the whole régime is one article, the next one.

2. Non-refoulement — the single most important rule

Article 33(1) of the Refugee Convention: "No Contracting State shall expel or return ('refouler') a refugee in any manner whatsoever to the frontiers of territories where his life or freedom would be threatened on account of his race, religion, nationality, membership of a particular social group or political opinion."

Article 33(2): "The benefit of the present provision may not, however, be claimed by a refugee whom there are reasonable grounds for regarding as a danger to the security of the country in which he is, or who, having been convicted by a final judgment of a particularly serious crime, constitutes a danger to the community of that country."

Every examinable proposition about non-refoulement follows from the text and the case law:

  1. "In any manner whatsoever" is the key phrase. It reaches rejection at the frontier, interception on the high seas, return through a third State, "push-backs", and every informal device of removal, including the deliberately mislabelled removal of a person as an "illegal migrant". The principle is a limit on the effect, not on the legal form.
  2. The prohibition is subject to the exception in Art. 33(2), but the parallel prohibition of return to torture is not. Article 3 of the Torture Convention — "No State Party shall expel, return ('refouler') or extradite a person to another State where there are substantial grounds for believing that he would be in danger of being subjected to torture" — admits of no exception, not even on grounds of national security or terrorism. So also the prohibition in Article 7 of the ICCPR, which the Human Rights Committee has treated as including "an obligation not to expose individuals to the danger of torture" (General Comment No. 31, para. 12), and Article 3 of the European Convention, which the Strasbourg Court has described as "one of the most fundamental values of democratic society", admitting of no derogation and no balancing against security (the Chahal line, reaffirmed in Saadi v. Italy, 2008).
  3. It binds irrespective of the refugee's status. Article 33 protects a person who satisfies the definition of a refugee; it is not limited to persons lawfully present or formally recognised, which is why the refusal to make the minimum evaluation required by the principle is itself a violation. The corollary is that the determination of refugee status is not a licence to be withheld: the assessment must be individual and fair.
  4. The protection may be extended by treaty and, on the better view, by custom. Regional instruments — the OAU Convention governing the Specific Aspects of Refugee Problems in Africa, 1969 (which adopts a wider definition, adding "external aggression, occupation, foreign domination or events seriously disturbing public order"), the Cartagena Declaration, 1984, the Arab Convention on Regulating Status of Refugees, 1994 — reproduce or extend non-refoulement. The UNHCR's Executive Committee has stated the principle in standing conclusions since 1977 (Conclusion No. 6) and reaffirmed it in Conclusion No. 25 (1983) as "a fundamental principle… which is not subject to any geographical limitation". The weight of opinion (including the 2001–02 Global Consultations) is that non-refoulement has become a rule of customary international law; the qualification is that the customary rule is understood as a rule of the Refugee Convention's content, with Art. 33(2)'s exception, whereas the prohibition of return to torture is a distinct and absolute customary rule.1
  5. It has an extraterritorial reach. The leading modern authority is Hirsi Jamaa and others v. Italy, Application No. 27765/09, Grand Chamber judgment of 23 February 2012, arising out of the interception on the high seas on 6 May 2009 of migrants sailing from Libya, who were transferred to the Libyan authorities under the Italy–Libya Treaty of Friendship. The European Court of Human Rights held: (i) Italy had jurisdiction under Article 1 of the Convention because the applicants had been under the de facto control of the Italian authorities on board Italian ships — "whenever the State through its agents exercises control and authority over an individual, and thus jurisdiction"; (ii) there was a violation of Article 3, because the Italian authorities knew or ought to have known that the migrants would be exposed to treatment contrary to Article 3 in Libya and to the risk of repatriation to Somalia and Eritrea; (iii) there was a violation of Article 13 because the applicants had no effective remedy; and (iv) the transfer of the whole group, without any examination of individual circumstances, was a "collective expulsion" contrary to Article 4 of Protocol No. 4. The decision is the meeting point of this topic and Topic 5: a State's enforcement jurisdiction at sea does not carry with it the freedom to remove persons to a place of danger, and the principle reaches interception on the high seas.
  6. The ILC codified the general law in 2014. The Draft Articles on the Expulsion of Aliens adopted by the International Law Commission in 2014 (Report of the sixtieth-sixth session, A/69/10) provide in Article 23 that no alien shall be expelled to a State where his or her life would be threatened on grounds such as race, colour, sex, language, religion, political or other opinion, national, ethnic or social origin, property, birth or other status; in Article 24 for an unconditional prohibition on expulsion where there are substantial grounds for believing that the alien would be subjected to torture or to cruel, inhuman or degrading treatment or punishment; and in Article 6 for the non-refoulement of refugees. The Commission also provided (Art. 6(3)) that the refugee's protection does not apply to a person whom there are reasonable grounds for regarding as a danger to the security of the country or who, having been convicted of a serious crime, constitutes such a danger — the mirror of Art. 33(2). The General Assembly took note of the draft articles in 2014 (resolution 69/119) and commended them to Governments; no convention has followed, but the texts are the best statement of the state of the law and a good authority to cite in an answer.

1 In an examination, the safe formulation is: non-refoulement under Article 33 of the Refugee Convention is treaty law for States parties; its customary status is widely asserted but the more secure ground for an unconditional prohibition is Article 3 of the Torture Convention, Article 7 of the ICCPR and regional human-rights law.

3. The modern controversies

  • Externalisation of protection. The 2024 EU Pact on Migration and Asylum — adopted in April–May 2024, in force from 11 June 2024 and applying in the member States from June 2026 — recasts the Union's asylum system around screening, the asylum procedures regulation, the asylum and migration management regulation, Eurodac, and the crisis regulation, and by common consent codifies a degree of flexibility for "safe third country" and "safe country of origin" arrangements. The United Kingdom's Rwanda policy (the Illegal Migration Act, 2023 and the Safety of Rwanda Act, 2024) was declared unlawful by the UK Supreme Court in AAA v. Secretary of State for the Home Department, [2023] UKSC 42, on 15 November 2023 because of the risk of refoulement of asylum-seekers by Rwanda, and the scheme was abandoned in 2024. Australia's offshore processing, the Italian arrangements with Albania, and the United States' interdiction practice under the 1996 arrangements are further examples of the same legal question: whether a State may shift the place where a protection claim is heard. The safe answer is that externalisation is not unlawful as such — States may direct claims to a genuinely safe third State — but that the return must not expose the individual to a real risk of prohibited treatment, and that each case requires an individual assessment.
  • Mass influx and climate displacement. Article 1A(2) requires an individual fear of persecution, which the OAU Convention and the Cartagena Declaration relax for regional emergencies; there is as yet no generally accepted status for persons displaced by the effects of climate change, and the question whether they are protected rests on the wider reading of "persecution" or on the instruments on disaster displacement (the Nansen Initiative's Protection Agenda, 2015, and the Platform on Disaster Displacement, which India is not a member of).

L. India and Asylum — Practice, Policy and Case Law

The legal position in three propositions. (i) India is not a party to the 1951 Refugee Convention or the 1967 Protocol, and it has no domestic asylum statute: the entry, residence and removal of refugees — as of all foreigners — is governed by the Foreigners Act, 1946, the Passport (Entry into India) Act, 1920, the Registration of Foreigners Act, 1939 and the rules and orders made under them. (ii) India has all the same granted protection to very large populations over many decades as a matter of executive policy. (iii) Because the grant of protection is an executive act, the courts have had to decide what remains: the answer has been that a person who is not a citizen cannot claim the fundamental rights which belong to citizens, but that the State remains bound by Article 21 in its treatment of him, and the question whether non-refoulement is part of Article 21 has been raised, but not finally decided, in the Rohingya litigation.

The practice

  • Tibetan refugees. Following the events of 1959, India permitted the Dalai Lama and thousands of Tibetans to enter and settle; their residence, documentation and welfare have been administered through executive arrangements and registration certificates rather than by a statute, and legislation governing their institutions was enacted separately.
  • Sri Lankan Tamils. From 1983 onwards refugees crossed the Palk Strait and were accommodated in camps in Tamil Nadu, with arrangements administered by the State Government and the Central Government; the population has fluctuated with the situation in Sri Lanka.
  • Chakmas and Hajongs. Arrivals from the Chittagong Hill Tracts from 1964 onwards were settled in Arunachal Pradesh; the grant of citizenship was some time later, and the Supreme Court's decision in State of Arunachal Pradesh v. Khudiram Chakma, (1994) 1 SCC 615, is the leading authority on their legal position.
  • Rohingya. Following large arrivals from Rakhine State in and after 2017, the Government's stated position has been that India is not a party to the Refugee Convention and that Rohingya are illegal migrants to be deported in accordance with law; several hundred have been detained, and groups have been deported to Myanmar since October 2018. The United Nations and the Supreme Court have been engaged with the question throughout.
  • UNHCR and the "City of Joy"/mandate arrangements. The UNHCR carries out status determination in India under its mandate for some nationalities; but the status it confers is not a right of residence in Indian law, because India has not incorporated the Convention.

The case law

  1. State of Arunachal Pradesh v. Khudiram Chakma, (1994) 1 SCC 615. The Supreme Court held that the Chakma refugees in Arunachal Pradesh were "foreigners" within the Foreigners Act, 1946 and could not claim the rights which Article 19 guarantees only to citizens; but the Court nevertheless directed the State to protect their life and personal liberty, permitted them to remain until their citizenship applications were decided, and observed that if they were not entitled to citizenship a reasonable procedure for their removal based on law would have to be followed. The decision is the source of the proposition taught in India: refugees are not outside the protection of Article 21, even though they are aliens.
  2. Sarbananda Sonowal v. Union of India, (2005) 5 SCC 665. Striking down the Illegal Migrants (Determination by Tribunals) Act, 1983, the Court held that the Act's procedures made the detection of illegal migrants from Bangladesh practically impossible and that the right to reside and settle in India, and the integrity of the nation's borders, are facets of Article 21; the Foreigners Act, 1946 with its reversed burden of proof was the appropriate legislation. The case is indispensable for any answer on the legal machinery of expulsion in India.
  3. The Rohingya litigation — Mohammad Salimullah v. Union of India. In 2017 the Supreme Court was moved to restrain the deportation of Rohingya refugees; on 18 October 2018 it declined to interfere with the deportation of seven persons already detained, and in its order of 2021 it refused interim relief while leaving the government's power to deport subject to the final decision and to the consideration of international obligations, including the principle of non-refoulement. The constitutional question — whether Article 21 incorporates non-refoulement, so that a person may not be sent to a country where he faces a real risk of persecution or torture — has therefore been argued but not finally answered. For an examination, the correct statement is that the Supreme Court has left the question open while treating it as a serious one, and that in the absence of legislation the power of removal remains executive but is not unconstrained.
The statutory limits on removal in Indian law

Three domestic limits on the power to remove are worth naming: (i) the requirement of a lawful order and a fair procedure — removal under the Foreigners Act, 1946 must be on the basis of an order made under s. 3 and, where citizenship is in dispute, after the determination prescribed by the Foreigners (Tribunals) Order, 1964; (ii) the rule that a person cannot be deprived of life or liberty except according to procedure established by law (Art. 21), which imports the requirements of fair hearing and reason; and (iii) the specific human-rights prohibitions which bind India: Article 7 of the ICCPR, to which India is a party and whose prohibition of torture and cruel, inhuman or degrading treatment is non-derogable under Article 4 of the Covenant, and which the Human Rights Committee reads as including an obligation not to expose a person to the danger of such treatment (General Comment No. 31, para. 12); the customary prohibition of torture and of return to torture; and, on the Indian side, Article 21 itself. India signed the Torture Convention on 14 October 1997 but has not ratified it, so Article 3 of the Convention is not, strictly, treaty law for India — a point which should be stated precisely in an answer, because examiners notice it. The conclusion is unaffected: a well-argued answer does not depend on India's accession to the Refugee Convention or to the Torture Convention, because the prohibition on return to torture can be founded on the ICCPR, on customary international law and on Article 21.

Leading illustrationsAbu Salem, Savarkar, Rana (2025)The 274 fugitives returned to India 2019–2026, mostly by deportationEichmann (1960), Alvarez-Machain (1992)Tibetan and Sri Lankan refugees in India; Hirsi Jamaa (2012) as the limit on removal at sea

M. Examination Angle for 6.5

The five question-types and the skeleton for each

(1) "Extradition is a matter of courtesy" / "is there a duty to extradite?" — Refute the absolute form of the proposition while conceding its historical core: (a) the classical rule (Factor v. Laubenheimer; Verma; the class notes) that extradition is treaty-based and there is no customary duty; (b) the qualifications: treaties create legal duties; the sectoral conventions create the duty to extradite or prosecute even without a bilateral treaty; s. 3(4) of the Indian Act permits a convention to be treated as a treaty by notification; and the practice of surrender on reciprocity and comity (Abu Salem) shows that courtesy is a fallback, not the foundation; (c) the role of municipal law — the International obligation is discharged through the domestic statute, and every step (requisition, inquiry, committal, surrender) is governed by it; (d) the practical form of the question — India's 48 treaties and 12 arrangements, and the 2025 Cabinet decisions extending the network; (e) conclusion: courtesy is the residue where no duty exists; where a treaty or convention applies, the surrender is a legal obligation, and its breach engages State responsibility.

(2) "Is extradition possible where there is no treaty?" — Yes, on four bases: (i) s. 3(1)–(3) of the Extradition Act, by notified order on the basis of reciprocity (Abu Salem, GSR 822(E), 13 December 2002); (ii) s. 3(4) — a multilateral convention may be treated as an extradition treaty for the offences specified in it; (iii) the conventions themselves, which by their own terms (Genocide Art. VII, CAT Art. 7, Hague Art. 8, Montreal Art. 7, Hostages Art. 9, Terrorist Bombings Art. 11) make the offence extraditable between the parties; and (iv) an ad hoc or special arrangement, or surrender as an executive act of comity. Add the caution that the absence of a treaty affects the duty and not the power to surrender, and that the requesting State may in any event prosecute (s. 34A) or try the fugitive if he returns voluntarily.

(3) "Political offenders are not extraditable." — Structure: definition and the absolute/relative distinction; the four tests (Castioni, Meunier, Kolczynski, Cheng); the attentat clause; the modern statutory and treaty narrowing (the Schedule to the Indian Act; Genocide Art. VII; the sectoral conventions and their purpose safeguards); s. 31(1)(a) and s. 29; and the evaluation — the exception protects political dissent but must not become a shelter for terrorism, which is why the schedule mechanism and the purpose clauses have grown.

(4) "Distinguish extradition, deportation and asylum" (or "extradition and deportation are the same in effect — discuss"). — Use Table 6.12 and the six differences in §6.5 H; illustrate with the Indian data (274 returned between 2019 and 2026, mostly by deportation) and with the two casebook cases: Abu Salem (extradition, with the speciality safeguard) and Savarkar (neither extradition nor deportation, but irregular recovery). Then add the human-rights limit: the manner of removal cannot defeat non-refoulement (Hirsi Jamaa; ILC Arts. 23–24).

(5) "Discuss the case of X" — for case 38 and case 39 use the digests at §6.5 F and §6.5 G: facts, issues, arguments, decision, principle. The three most-quoted points: the rule of speciality permits trial for lesser offences (s. 21(b)); the rule of non-inquiry is not absolute; and there is no duty to restore a prisoner irregularly recovered.

If the question is on asylum — cover: the definition and the two kinds; the individual has no right of asylum but the right to seek it (UDHR Art. 14); the 1967 Declaration on Territorial Asylum; the Asylum and Haya de la Torre cases; non-refoulement and its instruments; India's practice and the absent statute; and the constitutional question left open in the Rohingya litigation. If the question is on diplomatic asylum, the whole answer is the Asylum case plus Art. 41(3) of the VCDR (Topic 7).

↑ top

6.6Limits and Bridges — Immunity, Concurrency and Cyberspace

The five earlier heads describe how a State acquires jurisdiction. This section describes what happens next: the three things that stop a State from exercising a jurisdiction it lawfully has (immunity, the priority of another State's claim, and the practical impossibility of enforcing abroad), and the bridges of cooperation by which the system turns the impossibility into an ordinary administrative process.

A. Immunity — Jurisdiction Without Power to Exercise It

The single most important proposition of this topic is the one the International Court of Justice stated in the Arrest Warrant case: "the rules governing the jurisdiction of national courts must be carefully distinguished from those governing jurisdictional immunities: jurisdiction does not imply absence of immunity, while absence of immunity does not imply jurisdiction." Four immunities have to be kept apart, and each has its own instrument, its own extent in time, and its own waiver rule.

  1. Immunity of the State and of its property. A State may not be made a defendant before the courts of another State without its consent. India's law on the subject is still the colonial-era rule in the Code of Civil Procedure: sections 86 and 87 require the consent of the Central Government, certified in writing by a Secretary to the Government, before a foreign State may be sued, and s. 86(2) lists the exceptions in which such consent may be given (the foreign State has itself instituted the suit; the proceeding relates to immovable property in India in its possession or enjoyment; the matter is commercial; and so on); waiver of the privilege must be shown by conduct amounting to submission to the jurisdiction. The modern statement of the law is the United Nations Convention on Jurisdictional Immunities of States and Their Property, 2004 (General Assembly resolution 59/38 of 2 December 2004), which codifies the restrictive theory: immunity for acts jure imperii but no immunity for commercial transactions (Art. 10), contracts of employment (Art. 11), personal injury and damage to property in the forum State (Art. 12), ownership and possession of immovable property in the forum State (Art. 13), industrial and intellectual property (Art. 14), and participation in companies (Art. 15), with a separate régime in Arts. 18–21 for immunity from measures of constraint (pre-judgment and post-judgment). The Convention is not yet in force; India has not ratified it; but its scheme is the standard formulation and is worth citing. The leading decision is Jurisdictional Immunities of the State (Germany v. Italy: Greece intervening), I.C.J. Reports 2012, p. 99, in which the Court held that State immunity is a procedural bar attaching to the exercise of jurisdiction and is not affected by the gravity of the alleged violations of international law; that there is no exception for war crimes or crimes against humanity; and that Italy had violated its obligations both by permitting civil claims against Germany in its courts and by enforcing Greek judgments against it. The corollary, stated repeatedly in the Court's judgment to answer the "last resort" argument: the bar of immunity is independent of whether an alternative remedy exists.
  2. Immunity of the troika — Head of State, Head of Government, Foreign Minister. An incumbent of one of these three offices enjoys full immunity from the criminal jurisdiction of foreign courts and inviolability, ratione personae, while in office, without distinction between acts done in an official or a private capacity and without any exception for international crimes (case 37). The immunity ceases with the office, but the former official continues to enjoy immunity ratione materiae for acts performed in an official capacity (the point on which the Court in the Arrest Warrant case, para. 61, said it could not decide without more information, and which the House of Lords and the French Court of Cassation had addressed in the other direction in the Pinochet litigation for the specific offence of torture).
  3. Diplomatic and consular immunity. The Vienna Convention on Diplomatic Relations, 1961 (Topic 7) confers on the diplomatic agent inviolability of person, premises, papers and correspondence and immunity from the criminal, civil and administrative jurisdiction of the receiving State (Art. 31), subject to the three exceptions in Art. 31(1)(a)–(c) and to waiver by the sending State (Art. 32); the members of the diplomatic mission enjoy a lesser immunity (Art. 37); the mission's premises are inviolable (Art. 22). Consular officers under the Vienna Convention on Consular Relations, 1963, enjoy immunity in respect of acts performed in the exercise of consular functions (Art. 43 and Art. 41 for inviolability), which is a functional and not a personal immunity — the VCCR's Art. 36 notification requirement being the point which produced the LaGrand and Avena judgments (Topic 7's material). Special missions are governed by the 1969 Convention; and the officials of international organisations by the general instruments and headquarters agreements.
  4. Waiver and the consequences of the distinction. Immunity is a right of the State, not a privilege of the individual, and it may be waived — expressly (Art. 32 VCDR for diplomats; CPC s. 86 in India) or by conduct amounting to submission. The consequences of the distinction, all of them examinable, are: (a) a foreign State may itself prosecute its own official, and the official may be immune abroad and liable at home; (b) an international tribunal may enjoy a jurisdiction which national courts lack (the very point made by the ICJ in case 37 for the ICC); (c) immunity from jurisdiction may be lost without immunity from execution being lost, which is why judgments against States remain paper (the second half of the Germany v. Italy judgment); and (d) waiver must be clear — the taking of a step in the proceedings by a State's lawyer may amount to waiver, while a plea of immunity made at the first opportunity preserves it.

B. Concurrency — Two or More States, One Set of Facts

Because the five bases overlap (a hijacking on a foreign aircraft causing the death of an Indian national, planned in a third State, by a national of a fourth, produces six possible claimants), the international system must decide which State's claim prevails. International law does not rank the bases in a hierarchy; it manages the overlap in six ways:

  1. The strength of the link. The State with the most substantial connection is expected to act; the practice of restraint on the basis of comity is the qualification which the class notes and Verma attach to the Lotus dictum.
  2. Subsidiarity. Universal jurisdiction in particular is understood as a subsidiary basis: the State whose territory, nationality or security is primarily affected should ordinarily act, and the universal-jurisdiction State should act only where the primary custodial State is unwilling or unable — the argument developed in §6.3 I.
  3. Treaty allocation. The clearest examples are the law of the sea (jurisdiction over ships apportioned among flag, coastal and port States — UNCLOS Arts. 27, 28, 73, 92, 94, 97), the Tokyo Convention (jurisdiction over offences on board aircraft), the treaty crimes (all parties competent, with the custodial State obliged to prosecute or extradite), and the newest: the UN Convention against Cybercrime, adopted by consensus on 24 December 2024 (General Assembly resolution 79/243) and opened for signature at Hanoi on 25 October 2025, which allocates jurisdiction among the parties for cyber-enabled offences and channels the obtaining of electronic evidence through mutual legal assistance and a 24/7 network rather than unilateral access.
  4. The obligation to prosecute or extradite. Where extradition is refused, the requested State must submit the case to its own authorities — the allocative device of the sectoral conventions and, in India, of s. 34A of the Extradition Act, 1962.
  5. Complementarity and admissibility. In the international criminal system, the allocation between the ICC and national courts is governed by Article 17 of the Rome Statute: a case is inadmissible where it is being investigated or prosecuted by a State with jurisdiction, "unless the State is unwilling or unable genuinely to carry out the investigation or prosecution", the criteria of unwillingness being stated in Art. 17(2) (shielding, unjustified delay, lack of independence) — the principle of complementarity. India is not a party to the Rome Statute, but the principle is the clearest modern technique of jurisdictional allocation and should be named in any answer on concurrency.
  6. Immunity and the priority of the forum. Where both States are competent and no priority can be agreed, the availability of the accused decides the case: jurisdiction follows custody, which is why extradition, surrender and cooperation are the practical instruments of concurrency and why the rule in Savarkar matters.

C. Cyberspace — the Old Rules Applied to a New Medium

Cyberspace is not a law-free zone: no rule of general international law exempts conduct on the internet from the ordinary bases of jurisdiction. The International Group of Experts who produced the Tallinn Manual 2.0 on the International Law Applicable to Cyber Operations (2017) state the position in their rules on jurisdiction — the territorial, nationality, protective and universal bases apply to cyber operations as they apply elsewhere — and, crucially, restate the Lotus divide for the new medium: a State may not exercise enforcement jurisdiction in the territory of another State without that State's consent, so that remotely accessing data stored on a server abroad, or collecting evidence there, is an exercise of enforcement jurisdiction which requires consent, treaty or another permissive rule. That single proposition explains the whole architecture of cyber cooperation, and every contemporary development in the field is an attempt either to obtain such consent in advance (by treaty) or to avoid the need for it (by blocking or localising the data).

  1. India's prescriptive reach. Section 75 of the Information Technology Act, 2000 applies the Act to an offence or contravention committed outside India by any person if the act or conduct involves a "computer, computer system or computer network located in India" — an effects-based provision; s. 1(2) with s. 1(3) deals with the Act's application; s. 69 confers the interception and monitoring powers and s. 69A the power to block access to information, exercised through the Blocking Rules of 2009; the intermediary guidelines of 2021 impose due-diligence and traceability obligations on significant social media intermediaries; and the Digital Personal Data Protection Act, 2023 applies to the processing of digital personal data outside India where the processing is in connection with the offering of goods or services to data principals within India (s. 3(b)) — a textbook exercise of the objective territorial principle and of the effects doctrine.
  2. Localisation as a jurisdictional remedy. Where a State cannot compel the production of data held abroad, it may require the data to be kept at home. The Reserve Bank of India's Storage of Payment System Data direction of 6 April 2018 requires payment system data to be stored in India; the DPDP Act, 2023 permits the Government to notify categories for which only domestic storage will be allowed; and CERT-In's directions of 2022 require logs to be retained for 180 days and incidents to be reported within six hours. The technique is a legitimate exercise of sovereignty but it exports the jurisdictional conflict into the market: it is why the same issue is argued as a question of trade and of privacy rather than of jurisdiction alone.
  3. The foreign counterparts. Article 3(2) of the EU's General Data Protection Regulation, 2016 applies the Regulation to controllers and processors not established in the Union who offer goods or services to, or monitor the behaviour of, data subjects in the Union — the clearest modern assertion of the effects doctrine in the private-law field. The United States v. Microsoft litigation (the "Microsoft Ireland" case), in which a warrant issued in New York was resisted because the data were held on a server in Dublin, was rendered moot by the enactment of the CLOUD Act, 2018, which permits the United States to require data held abroad by a United States service provider and authorises the executive to conclude bilateral agreements with foreign States to channel such requests — the first serious attempt to replace unilateral enforcement with executive agreements, first concluded with the United Kingdom in 2019 and with Australia in 2021. The European Union adopted its own "e-evidence" package in 2023 (the Regulation on European Production and Preservation Orders and the accompanying Directive), which allows an order issued by a judicial authority in one member State to be served directly on a service provider in another — an intra-Union solution which the rest of the world does not enjoy.
  4. The treaty solution — and India's position. The Budapest Convention on Cybercrime, 2001 (with its Additional Protocols) was long the only binding instrument; India declined to accede to it. The United Nations Convention against Cybercrime, concluded under the aegis of UNODC, was adopted by consensus on 24 December 2024, opened for signature at Hanoi on 25 October 2025 (at which 72 States signed) and will enter into force 90 days after the fortieth ratification. As of the latest reporting, India has not signed it, although it participated actively in the negotiations and advocated stronger provisions on critical-infrastructure protection and on data-sharing; the stated concerns are the compatibility of the treaty's data-sharing obligations with the right to privacy recognised in K.S. Puttaswamy v. Union of India, (2017) 10 SCC 1, and with India's data-protection framework. The convention is therefore the outstanding example of the present state of the law of jurisdiction: the rules are territorial, the medium is not, and the only durable answer is a treaty — which is the reason the question of cyber jurisdiction is now a question about treaties.

D. Other Frontiers, and the Bridges

Outer space. Article VIII of the Outer Space Treaty, 1967, gives the State of registry jurisdiction and control over an object launched into outer space and over its personnel while in outer space or on a celestial body; the Registration Convention, 1975, makes the registry public; and the Moon Agreement, 1979, which India has not ratified, would treat lunar resources as the common heritage of mankind. Launching States also bear responsibility for activities by non-governmental entities (Art. VI) and liability for damage (Art. VII) — jurisdiction, responsibility and liability in one instrument.

Submarine cables. UNCLOS Articles 113 to 115 require every State to adopt laws making the breaking or injury of a submarine cable or pipeline beneath the high seas by a ship flying its flag, or by a person subject to its jurisdiction, a punishable offence, and to provide for the indemnification of the owner who sacrifices an anchor or net to avoid injury. In India the subject is dealt with under the law relating to telegraphs and telecommunications, and the provision illustrates a useful general lesson: where a State cannot police a medium, it legislates over its own ships and nationals — the nationality principle used as an enforcement device.

The bridges. Because unilateral enforcement abroad is prohibited, the system depends on a set of cooperative devices, and a well-answered question on jurisdiction should name them: mutual legal assistance treaties (India has a wide network and is a party to the mutual-assistance chapters of the UNTOC, 2000, and the UNCAC, 2003, and to the SAARC and BIMSTEC conventions); letters rogatory under s. 166A of the Code of Criminal Procedure, 1973 (with the Central Government as the channel) and their civil counterparts under Order V of the Code of Civil Procedure; the Interpol system of notices, under which a Red Corner Notice is a request for provisional arrest and circulation, not a warrant; joint investigation teams, now expressly contemplated by the UNTOC, the UNCAC and the 2024 Cybercrime Convention; the recognition and enforcement regimes for foreign judgments and arbitral awards (§6.4 F); and, as the last resort, international adjudication — the ICJ, ITLOS and inter-State arbitration, which is how disputes about jurisdiction are ultimately settled when diplomacy fails (the Enrica Lexie arbitration, the Arrest Warrant case and the Savarkar arbitration itself).

This section collects, in one table, every authority used in these notes — the three prescribed cases first, then the supporting cases in the order in which they appear in the text. It is designed for the last hour of revision: the "Where" column tells the reader where the full treatment is, and the "One-line holding" column is written so that it can be reproduced directly in an answer.

Case No. 2016-35 (India–Netherlands B.I.T.; award of 25 September 2020)The investment-treaty proceedings arising from the retrospective tax amendment — with the setting-aside proceedings in Singapore — followed by the withdrawal of the retrospective charge by the Taxation Laws (Amendment) Act, 2021; the sequence illustrates how a jurisdictional overreach becomes a question of State responsibility.§6.4 G
§6.5 Extradition, deportation and asylum
Terlinden v. Ames184 U.S. 270 (1902)The classical definition of extradition as the surrender by one State to another of an individual accused or convicted of an offence outside the surrendering State's territory but within the requesting State's jurisdiction.§6.5 A; §6.5 F
Factor v. Laubenheimer290 U.S. 276 (1933)"The legal right to demand his extradition and correlative duty to surrender him to the demanding country exist only when created by treaty" — the foundation of the proposition that extradition rests on treaty and not on customary law, with the consequence that obligations under an extradition treaty are to be liberally construed so as not to defeat the purpose of the treaty.§6.5 A, C
United States v. Rauscher119 U.S. 407 (1886)The rule of speciality: a person extradited can be tried only for the offence for which he was surrendered, until he has had an opportunity to return.§6.5 C
R. v. Corrigan[1931] 1 K.B. 527The rule of speciality is not violated where the extradited person is tried on the same facts for a different offence from the one for which he was surrendered.§6.5 C
Rambabu Saxena v. StateA.I.R. 1950 S.C. 155Where the treaty authorises the surrender of "any other offences" besides those named, the surrendered person may be tried for offences not named in the treaty.§6.5 C
Daya Singh Lahoria v. Union of India(2001) 4 S.C.C. 516A fugitive brought into India under an extradition decree can be tried only for the offences mentioned in the decree and for no other offence, unless the speciality rule is satisfied; the courts have no jurisdiction to try him for other offences.§6.5 D
State of Maharashtra v. Suman Sood(S.C., 2007)Where extradition was granted for a more serious offence (kidnapping for ransom), the trial of the extradited person for a lesser offence (kidnapping) was not without authority of law — the precursor of the reasoning in Abu Salem.§6.5 F
Canada v. Schmidt[1987] 1 S.C.R. 500The rule of non-inquiry: the judicial process in a foreign country is not to be subjected to "finicky evaluations" against the rules of the requested State's legal process.§6.5 E, F
Soering v. United Kingdom(1989) 11 E.H.R.R. 439Extradition to a real risk of torture or inhuman or degrading treatment violates Article 3; the "death row phenomenon" may cross the threshold; the balance between community interest and individual rights.§6.5 E
Chahal v. United Kingdom(1996) 23 E.H.R.R. 413The prohibition of inhuman treatment is absolute; the risk cannot be balanced against national security; assurances do not dispense with an independent examination.§6.5 E
Saadi v. Italy; Othman (Abu Qatada) v. United Kingdom(2008) 49 E.H.R.R. 30; (2012) 55 E.H.R.R. 1Diplomatic assurances are relevant but must be tested; the conditions of sufficiency are good faith, specificity, and an objective, effective and verifiable protection system.§6.5 E
Re Castioni; Re Meunier; R. v. Governor of Brixton Prison, ex parte Kolczynski; Cheng v. Governor of Pentonville Prison[1891] 1 Q.B. 149; [1894] 2 Q.B. 415; [1955] 1 Q.B. 540; [1973] A.C. 931The political-offence exception: the incidence test (Castioni), the anti-social-act limitation (Meunier), the political-purpose test (Kolczynski), and the exclusion of ordinary crimes committed for private gain (Cheng).§6.5 I
Asylum Case (Colombia v. Peru); Haya de la Torre caseI.C.J. Reports 1950, p. 266; I.C.J. Reports 1951, p. 71Diplomatic asylum is not a general customary rule; the asylum-granting State may not unilaterally qualify the offence; but international law does not require surrender of the refugee, and the mode of terminating the asylum is a matter for the parties.§6.5 J
Hirsi Jamaa and others v. Italy(2012) 55 E.H.R.R. 21; App. No. 27765/09Interception on the high seas and delivery of migrants to Libya engaged Italy's jurisdiction under the Convention; violations of Art. 3, Art. 13 and Art. 4 of Protocol No. 4 — non-refoulement reaches the high seas.§6.5 K
State of Arunachal Pradesh v. Khudiram Chakma(1994) 1 S.C.C. 615Refugees who are foreigners cannot claim the rights of citizens, but the State is bound to protect their life and personal liberty under Art. 21; their removal must follow a procedure established by law.§6.5 L
Sarbananda Sonowal v. Union of India(2005) 5 S.C.C. 665The Illegal Migrants (Determination by Tribunals) Act, 1983 was struck down as unconstitutional for making the detection of illegal migrants impossible; the integrity of the nation and the right to reside and settle are facets of Article 21; the Foreigners Act, 1946 is the appropriate law.§6.5 H, L
Mohammad Salimullah v. Union of IndiaSupreme Court, orders of 2017–2021 (pending)The deportation of Rohingya refugees was not restrained; the government's power was left subject to the final decision and to the consideration of India's international obligations, including non-refoulement — the constitutional question remains open.§6.5 L
K.S. Puttaswamy v. Union of India(2017) 10 S.C.C. 1Privacy is a fundamental right; the reason the UN Cybercrime Convention's data-sharing obligations require scrutiny in India.§6.6 C

The Seven Cases the Question Bank Singles Out

The casebook's own question bank (item 21, "State the facts and the decision in…") includes seven cases which belong to this topic: the Lotus (item 6), Eichmann (item 4), Savarkar (item 5), the Asylum case and Haya de la Torre (item 3), Piracy Jure Gentium (item 9), the Franconia (item 10), and Mighell v. Sultan of Johore (item 7). All seven are digested in these notes; the remaining items on that list (Corfu Channel, Nottebohm, Palmas Island, West Rand, Anglo-Norwegian Fisheries, Barcelona Traction) belong to other topics and are treated in the notes for those topics.
