B. Model Answers

Answer 1 — Q.3 of the 2025 paper (20 marks)

Question. "Extradition is a matter of courtesy. Discuss the underlying principles of the Extradition under International Law and the role of municipal law provisions in extradition proceedings. Is extradition possible to those states with whom a Country has no bilateral or multilateral extradition treaty, if they are party to an international convention? Argue with the help of decided cases."

Answer. The proposition with which the question begins is true of the past and false of the present; the answer is to explain both, and then to answer the two specific questions which follow.

1. The proposition and its history. In the classical law extradition had no foundation in customary international law. There is no duty to surrender a fugitive in the absence of a treaty; the American Supreme Court said in Factor v. Laubenheimer, 290 U.S. 276 (1933), that "the legal right to demand his extradition and correlative duty to surrender him to the demanding country exist only when created by treaty"; the Delhi University course material states the same rule ("it is based upon bilateral treaty law and does not exist as an obligation upon States in customary law"); and the result is that where no treaty exists the surrender, if it takes place at all, is an act of courtesy, reciprocity or comity. Professor Bassiouni's definition — adopted by Ganguly J. in Abu Salem — describes surrender as taking place "by virtue of a treaty, reciprocity or comity", and the Indian Extradition Act, 1962 itself contemplates the treaty-less case, because section 3(1) permits the Central Government by notified order to apply the Act to a foreign State, and section 3(4) permits a Convention to be treated as an extradition treaty. So far the question's premise survives — as the description of the residual case.

2. Why courtesy is not the modern foundation. Three developments have displaced it. First, extradition is today governed by an immense network of treaties: India alone has extradition treaties with 48 States and extradition arrangements with 12 (Ministry of External Affairs, reply in Parliament of 19 December 2025), and it has recently added Kenya, Kazakhstan, the Kyrgyz Republic and Seychelles. Where a treaty applies, the duty to surrender is a legal duty, and its breach engages the responsibility of the requested State. Secondly, the multilateral suppression conventions create duties which do not depend upon a bilateral treaty at all: genocide (Art. VII of the 1948 Convention), torture (Art. 7 of the 1984 Convention), hostage-taking (Art. 9 of the 1979 Convention), offences against the safety of civil aviation (Arts. 7 and 8 of the Hague Convention, 1970 and Art. 7 of the Montreal Convention, 1971), terrorist bombing (Art. 11 of the 1997 Convention), and unlawful acts against the safety of maritime navigation (Art. 11 of the 1988 SUA Convention). Thirdly, the law imposes the alternative duty to prosecute where surrender is refused — the aut dedere aut judicare formula of all those conventions, which the International Court of Justice enforced in Belgium v. Senegal, I.C.J. Reports 2012, p. 422. Where a legal duty exists, courtesy is not the explanation of the surrender; it is the name we give to the absence of a duty.

3. The underlying principles of extradition. Six should be stated, each with its authority: (i) no extradition without a treaty in principle, with the qualifications above; (ii) the rule of double criminality — the conduct must be criminal in both States, tested substantially and not by identity of punishment or of nomenclature (Re Nielsen (1984), as cited in the Delhi University materials); (iii) the rule of speciality — the surrendered person may be tried only for the offence for which he was surrendered, or for a lesser offence disclosed by the same facts, or with the consent of the surrendering State: United States v. Rauscher, 119 U.S. 407 (1886); section 21 of the Extradition Act, 1962; and the decision of the Supreme Court in case 38, Abu Salem Abdul Qayoom Ansari v. State of Maharashtra, where it was held that section 21(b) permits a trial for lesser offences, "lesser" being measured by punishment and not by the ingredients of the offence; (iv) the political-offence exception, now much narrowed but still living in section 31(1)(a) and the Schedule to the Act, and excluded altogether for the offences listed in the Schedule and for the treaty crimes; (v) the prima facie case requirement, section 7(3) of the Act, which entitles the fugitive to test the requesting State's evidence; and (vi) the human-rights limits, which are the modern addition to the list — no surrender to a real risk of torture or inhuman treatment (Soering v. United Kingdom, (1989) 11 E.H.R.R. 439; Chahal v. United Kingdom, (1996) 23 E.H.R.R. 413), with assurances to be scrutinised and not accepted at face value (Saadi v. Italy (2008); Othman (Abu Qatada) v. United Kingdom (2012)), and with the death-penalty problem dealt with in India by statute (section 34C, assuring life imprisonment where the surrendering State does not provide the death penalty — the assurance actually given to Portugal in Abu Salem on 25 May 2003).

4. The role of municipal law. Extradition is a two-level operation: the obligation is international, the procedure is municipal. The judgment in Abu Salem states the point plainly: "Though extradition is granted in implementation of the international commitment of the State, the procedure to be followed by the courts in deciding whether extradition should be granted and on what terms is determined by the municipal law of the land." Municipal law performs four functions. It supplies the legal basis: no Indian court or executive can surrender a fugitive except under the Extradition Act, 1962, and the Act applies either because a treaty State has been notified under section 3 or because a Convention has been treated as a treaty under section 3(4). It supplies the procedure: requisition under section 4, the Central Government's order for inquiry under section 5, the magistrate's warrant under section 6, the inquiry with evidence on both sides and the prima facie test under section 7, committal under section 7(4), and the executive warrant of surrender under section 8. It supplies the grounds of refusal: political character, time-bar, the requirement of reciprocal speciality, the priority of Indian proceedings and the fifteen-day rule in section 31(1); the discretionary power to refuse for triviality, want of good faith, political or other reasons in section 29; and the discharge of a person not conveyed out within two months under section 24. And it supplies the consequences of surrender: section 21 (speciality), section 34 (deemed extra-territorial commission), section 34A (prosecute where extradition is refused) and section 34C (life imprisonment instead of the death penalty). The same dual structure exists in every other system: the United Kingdom's Extradition Act 2003, the Portuguese law applied in Abu Salem, and the Framework Decision of 13 June 2002 establishing the European Arrest Warrant, which replaced extradition with "surrender" between the member States of the Union on the basis of mutual recognition.

5. The treaty-less case — the third limb of the question. Extradition is possible even without a bilateral or multilateral extradition treaty, if the two States are parties to an international convention, on four distinct bases: (a) section 3(4) of the Extradition Act, 1962, which allows a Convention to be treated as an extradition treaty by notified order for the offences specified in it; (b) the terms of the sectoral conventions themselves, which declare the offences extraditable between the parties and make the convention the legal basis where no treaty exists (Genocide Art. VII; Torture Art. 7; Hostages Art. 9; Hague Art. 8; Montreal Art. 7; Terrorist Bombings Art. 11; SUA Art. 11); (c) a notification applying the domestic statute on the basis of reciprocity, which is what India did in Abu Salem: there was no extradition treaty between India and Portugal, so the Government issued Gazette Notification GSR 822(E) of 13 December 2002 applying the Extradition Act, 1962 (except Chapter III) to Portugal on the basis of reciprocity, the Court of Appeals of Lisbon recognised the principle of reciprocity and the applicability of the Act, and the Supreme Court of Justice of Portugal granted extradition on 27 January 2005; and (d) an ad hoc arrangement or an act of comity, which is the residual case with which the question begins. The same point can be made from case 39, the Savarkar arbitration, which shows that where no form of extradition is used at all, the receiving State is under no obligation to restore the person irregularly recovered (P.C.A. Award of 24 February 1911).

6. Conclusion. Extradition is not a matter of courtesy in the modern law; it is a matter of treaty and of statute, in which courtesy survives only as the explanation of the cases in which no legal duty exists. The principles — double criminality, speciality, the political-offence exception, the prima facie case, and the human-rights limits — are applied through municipal machinery, and the municipal statute itself bridges the treaty-less case by permitting a Convention to be treated as a treaty and by permitting surrender on reciprocity, as Abu Salem demonstrates.

Answer 2 — Non-extradition of political criminals (Q.19)

Introduction. Every extradition treaty contains an exception for political offenders. Its rationale is that a person who has offended against the political order of a State should not be handed back to that State, because the trial would not be fair and because it is not the business of the asylum State to police another State's politics. The exception is thus an exception of public policy, an assertion of the asylum State's own values, and a qualification of the principle of comity.

Meaning and kinds. A political offence is an offence committed in the course of, or for the purposes of, a political struggle. The pure or absolute political offence (treason, sedition, rebellion, espionage, membership of a banned political organisation) is never extraditable. The relative or mixed political offence — an ordinary crime committed with a political object, such as the killing of a public official or a bank robbery to finance a revolution — is protected only where the political element predominates. The measurement of predominance is the whole difficulty of the subject.

The tests evolved by the courts. Four tests are standard. (i) The incidence test (Re Castioni, [1891] 1 Q.B. 149): the offence must be incidental to and form part of a political disturbance. (ii) The anti-social test (Re Meunier, [1894] 2 Q.B. 415): the offence must be directed against a State or a party in a struggle for power, not against society in general — the anarchist's bomb is not a political offence. (iii) The political-purpose test (R. v. Governor of Brixton Prison, ex parte Kolczynski, [1955] 1 Q.B. 540): the question is whether, as between the requesting State and the accused, there is a conflict of a political nature. (iv) The ordinary-crime limitation (Cheng v. Governor of Pentonville Prison, [1973] A.C. 931): the exception does not extend to conduct which is, on the requesting State's case, an ordinary crime, or which is committed for private gain. The Supreme Court of Canada in United States v. Shephard, [1977] 2 S.C.R. 1067, and the English courts in T. v. Secretary of State, [1996] 2 All E.R. 865, narrowed the exception further.

The attentat clause and the modern restrictions. Belgium's clause attentat of 1856 excluded attacks on the life of a foreign Head of State from the exception; the technique has since become universal in the form of lists of non-political offences. The Genocide Convention (Art. VII) declares that genocide is not a political crime; the General Assembly's Resolution 3074 (XXVIII) of 1973 requires the extradition of war criminals and those guilty of crimes against humanity; and the anti-terrorism conventions — the Hague Convention 1970 (Art. 8), the Montreal Convention 1971 (Art. 7), the Hostages Convention 1979 (Art. 9), the Torture Convention 1984 (Art. 7), the Terrorist Bombings Convention 1997 (Art. 11) and the SUA Convention 1988 (Art. 11) — declare the offences extraditable and exclude the political defence, while retaining a "purpose safeguard" that a request made to punish a person on grounds of race, religion, nationality, ethnic origin or political opinion will not be granted.

The Indian position. Section 31(1)(a) of the Extradition Act, 1962 prohibits surrender if the offence is of a political character, or if the fugitive proves that the requisition or warrant was in fact made with a view to try or punish him for an offence of a political character. Section 31(2) with the Schedule neutralises the exception for fourteen heads of offence — hijacking, aviation offences, crimes against internationally protected persons, hostage-taking, culpable homicide and murder, grievous hurt with a dangerous weapon, offences under the Explosive Substances Act, 1908, the two firearm offences under the Arms Act, 1959, damage to public utilities, wrongful restraint and confinement, kidnapping and abduction, TADA offences and abetment of any of them — and section 31(3) empowers the Government to amend the list by notification. Section 29 gives the Central Government a further power to refuse surrender "for political reasons or otherwise", and section 7(2) requires the magistrate to take evidence on the question whether the offence is political. The practical significance of the distinction between extradition and deportation is here at its highest: a State which is unwilling to surrender a political offender may yet deport him under the Foreigners Act, 1946, and that is why the human-rights law insists that the manner of removal must not be used to defeat the safeguards of extradition.

Evaluation. The exception protects the political refugee, but its elasticity is its danger: "one man's terrorist is another man's freedom fighter". The modern response has not been to abolish the exception but to channel it: the Schedule, the sectoral conventions and their purpose safeguards, and the proportionality test. The test of a good legal system is that it protects political dissent while refusing shelter to those who commit crimes against humanity — which is exactly the balance that the Schedule and the Genocide Convention's Article VII strike.

Answer 3 — The bases of State jurisdiction (recurring, 20 marks)

Introduction. Jurisdiction is the power of a State under international law to regulate or otherwise affect people, property and circumstances. It is the legal expression of sovereignty, and it has two aspects — the power to prescribe (legislative or prescriptive jurisdiction) and the power to enforce (executive and judicial jurisdiction). The two must never be confused: Lotus lays down that a State may not exercise its power in the territory of another State without a permissive rule, but that in its own territory it may exercise jurisdiction over acts which have taken place abroad, subject only to prohibitive rules (P.C.I.J., Series A, No. 10, p. 18).

The five bases. (i) Territoriality, subjective (conduct begun in the State) and objective (conduct completed in the State, including the effects felt there — the effects doctrine, applied by India in section 32 of the Competition Act, 2002 and section 75 of the Information Technology Act, 2000, and litigated in the Vodafone case). (ii) Nationality, active (a State's own nationals abroad: IPC section 4(1); Blackmer v. United States, 284 U.S. 421 (1932)) and passive (the nationality of the victim: Cutting, 1886; now chiefly treaty-based, as in Article 5(1)(d) of the Hostages Convention, 1979). (iii) The protective principle, which covers acts abroad threatening the State's security or vital interests (Joyce v. D.P.P., [1946] A.C. 347; G.B. Singh, A.I.R. 1973 S.C. 2667) — codified in India in the Unlawful Activities (Prevention) Act, 1967, the Official Secrets Act, 1923 and section 4(3) of the Penal Code. (iv) Universal jurisdiction, over piracy jure gentium, war crimes, genocide, crimes against humanity, torture, apartheid and the treaty crimes; and (v) treaty (or auxiliary) jurisdiction, by which the parties allocate competence, most clearly in the law of the sea, the Tokyo Convention, and the 2024 UN Convention against Cybercrime.

The constitutional and statutory position in India. Article 245(2) of the Constitution provides that no law of Parliament shall be invalid on the ground of extra-territorial operation; the Abdul Cader case (A.I.R. 1977 Mad. 386, F.B.) explains that the expression is used in two senses. India's penal law reaches conduct abroad through sections 3 and 4 of the Penal Code (now the corresponding provisions of the Bharatiya Nyaya Sanhita, 2023), with the sanction of the Central Government required under section 188 of the Code of Criminal Procedure (now section 210 of the Bharatiya Nagarik Suraksha Sanhita, 2023). Beyond the penal law, sections 86 and 87 of the Code of Civil Procedure deal with the immunity of States, section 44A with the enforcement of foreign decrees from reciprocating territories, and the special statutes — the Competition Act, the Income-tax Act, the IT Act, the Passport Act — with their own heads of extra-territoriality.

Limits and conclusion. Every basis is subject to the limits discussed in these notes: immunities (case 37; Germany v. Italy, 2012), the prohibition of enforcement abroad, the requirement of a genuine link (Nottebohm, 1955), the comity-based practice of restraint, and the human-rights limits in extradition and expulsion. The answer should conclude with the two-part formula from Lotus: prescription may travel; enforcement may not — and everything else in this topic, from extradition to mutual legal assistance, is machinery for making the first effective without the second.

Answer 4 — Case 37, the Arrest Warrant case (recurring, 20 marks)

Introduction. In Arrest Warrant of 11 April 2000 (Democratic Republic of the Congo v. Belgium), I.C.J. Reports 2002, p. 3, the International Court of Justice held that the incumbent Foreign Minister of the Congo enjoyed immunity from the criminal jurisdiction of Belgium, that the issue and circulation of an arrest warrant against him had violated Belgium's obligation to respect that immunity and his inviolability, and that Belgium must cancel the warrant and inform the authorities to whom it had been circulated. The judgment is the leading modern authority on the immunity of the "troika" — the Head of State, the Head of Government and the Minister for Foreign Affairs.

Facts. Belgium's Law of 16 June 1993, as amended on 19 February 1999, conferred on Belgian courts jurisdiction over grave breaches of the Geneva Conventions and their Protocols and over crimes against humanity, wherever committed. On 11 April 2000 a Belgian investigating judge issued an international arrest warrant in absentia against Mr Abdoulaye Yerodia Ndombasi, then the Congolese Minister for Foreign Affairs, for offences alleged in 1998 consisting of speeches inciting racial hatred and violence; the warrant was circulated internationally through Interpol from June 2000. The Congo applied to the Court on 17 October 2000, seeking a declaration that Belgium had violated the principle of sovereign equality and the immunity of a Foreign Minister, and asking for the annulment of the warrant; Belgium raised four preliminary objections, which the Court rejected (operative paragraph 78(A), by 15 votes to 1).

Issues. (i) Whether the issue and international circulation of an arrest warrant against a serving Foreign Minister violated the immunity to which the Congo was entitled; (ii) whether Belgium's claim to exercise universal jurisdiction was lawful; (iii) whether the case had become moot when Mr Yerodia ceased to hold office; and (iv) whether Belgium's exercise of jurisdiction could be justified on the basis of the seriousness of the crimes.

Reasoning. The Court held that the immunity of an incumbent Foreign Minister is complete: he enjoys inviolability and full immunity from the criminal jurisdiction of other States, ratione personae, without any distinction between acts performed in an official or a private capacity and without any distinction between acts performed before or during the term of office. The rule is customary (not merely a rule of the Vienna Convention), and the "mere issue" of a warrant — which contains an order to the authorities of all States, "to execute at the request of the Belgian authorities" — violated that immunity, as did the subsequent international circulation, which was liable to hamper the Minister's ability to travel and to perform his functions. The Court rejected the argument that an exception exists for war crimes or crimes against humanity: after reviewing State practice, the House of Lords' decision in Pinochet, the French Court of Cassation's decision in the Qadaffi case, and the statutes of the Nuremberg and Tokyo Tribunals, the ICTY, the ICTR and the ICC (whose Article 27 excludes immunity only before the international court itself), it found that practice did not support any such exception. Two propositions of general importance were stated: that jurisdiction does not imply absence of immunity, and absence of immunity does not imply jurisdiction (para. 59), and that immunity from jurisdiction is not equivalent to impunity: the immunity of an incumbent Minister "does not mean that they enjoy impunity in respect of any crimes they might have committed, irrespective of their gravity" (para. 60), because four doors remain open — prosecution in the official's own State, waiver of immunity, prosecution after the person ceases to hold office in respect of acts of a private nature or of acts committed before or after the term, and prosecution before an international criminal court with jurisdiction. The Court additionally held that France's cause célèbre was no answer (the warrant had been issued in breach of the law of nations as applied by the Court), and that Congo's claims as a State needed no proof of individual damage.

Separate and dissenting opinions. Several judges wrote separately: Judge Guillaume argued that incumbent Foreign Ministers enjoy absolute immunity and former ones enjoy immunity only ratione materiae; Judges Higgins, Kooijmans and Buergenthal used their joint opinion to argue for a careful but real place for universal jurisdiction in civil claims; Judge Al-Khasawneh and, most famously, Judge Van den Wyngaert criticised the majority for failing to qualify the immunity in the way the House of Lords had qualified it in Pinochet — she wrote that immunity should never apply to crimes under international law, and warned that the judgment would give the impression of reintroducing an absolute theory of sovereign immunity after Pinochet.

Conclusion. The case establishes that the immunity of the troika is inviolable while in office, that jurisdiction and immunity are separate questions, and that immunity does not amount to impunity. It is the necessary starting point for any answer on universal jurisdiction, since it explains why universal-jurisdiction prosecutions must be directed at persons who do not enjoy personal immunity — as in Eichmann and Pinochet — or be brought before international courts, where Article 27 of the Rome Statute excludes immunity altogether.

Answer 5 — Extradition, deportation and asylum distinguished (recurring)

Extradition is the surrender by one State to another of a person accused or convicted of an offence, for trial or punishment in the requesting State. It is based on treaty (or a convention treated as a treaty, or a notified arrangement), on requisition by the requesting State, on a judicial inquiry and a prima facie case, and it is subject to the rule of speciality, the political-offence exception, the time-bar rule and the human-rights limits. Deportation (or expulsion) is the removal of an alien from the territory by the State in which he is, in exercise of its sovereignty over the admission and residence of foreigners. It requires no request, no charge, no judicial inquiry and no treaty; in India it is governed by the Foreigners Act, 1946, the Foreigners Order, 1948 and the Foreigners (Tribunals) Order, 1964, with the burden of proving that he is not a foreigner placed on the person concerned (ss. 8–9). Its legal limits are Article 21, the requirement of a lawful order, and the prohibition on removal to a real risk of torture. Asylum is the protection granted by a State, in its territory or in a place under its control, to a person who has fled another State: territorial asylum is an attribute of territorial sovereignty; diplomatic (extra-territorial) asylum is a Latin American treaty institution, and the International Court of Justice in the Asylum case (1950) held that it is not a rule of general customary law and that the granting State may not unilaterally qualify the offence, while Haya de la Torre (1951) held that the irregular asylum did not have to be terminated by surrender.

The practical significance of the distinctions. Deportation is faster, simpler and free of the safeguards of extradition — which is why it is used where extradition is unavailable, and why it is dangerous: it can be used to return a person to persecution or torture. The evidence of Indian practice makes the point: of the 274 fugitives brought back to India from 36 countries between 2019 and 2026, the majority came back through deportation rather than extradition. The human-rights law has therefore insisted that the manner of removal cannot defeat the substance of protection: Hirsi Jamaa and others v. Italy (2012) held that the interception of migrants on the high seas and their delivery to Libya engaged Italy's jurisdiction and violated Article 3, Article 13 and Article 4 of Protocol No. 4; and the International Law Commission's Draft Articles on the Expulsion of Aliens (2014) provide an unconditional prohibition of expulsion to a State where the alien would be subjected to torture (Art. 24) and a prohibition where his life would be threatened on discriminatory grounds (Art. 23). The conclusion is that the three institutions are legally distinct but functionally linked: extradition is the rule-governed surrender of a fugitive; deportation is the non-consensual removal of an alien; asylum is the refusal to remove; and the limits imposed by human-rights law apply to whatever form the removal takes.

Answer 6 — Universal jurisdiction, Eichmann and Pinochet (recurring)

Universal jurisdiction is the competence of a State to try an offence committed abroad by a foreign national against a foreign victim, where no other link — territory, nationality, protection or treaty — is required, because the offence is one which every State has an interest in suppressing. It is exceptional; it is a permissive rather than an obligatory rule (Lotus); and in its modern form it is largely treaty-based (torture, hostage-taking, hijacking, terrorist bombing, maritime terrorism, genocide, war crimes and crimes against humanity), with piracy jure gentium as the one classical customary crime (In re Piracy Jure Gentium, [1934] A.C. 586).

Eichmann (Israel, 1961–62). Adolf Eichmann, the head of the Jewish affairs section of the Reich Security Main Office, was abducted from Argentina by Israeli agents in 1960 — an act that produced Security Council Resolution 138 (1960) and a formal settlement between Israel and Argentina. Tried in Jerusalem under the Nazis and Nazi Collaborators (Punishment) Law, 1950, he pleaded that Israel lacked jurisdiction. The District Court held that jurisdiction rested on two cumulative sources: the universal character of the crimes charged (crimes against humanity and war crimes, for which every State is entitled to punish), and the protective principle (the crimes were committed against the Jewish people, on whose behalf the State of Israel was entitled to act). The Supreme Court (36 I.L.R. 277) affirmed, and added the further point that the manner of the accused's capture did not affect the jurisdiction of the court: male capto bene judicato — a person properly tried is not to be released because he was improperly seized. The case is the classic illustration of universal jurisdiction in practice, and its limits: the offence was committed against the State's own people, so that the protective and universal bases coincided.

Pinochet (United Kingdom, 1998–2000). Senator Augusto Pinochet, the former Head of State of Chile, was arrested in London on a Spanish request for extradition on charges of torture committed during his rule. The House of Lords held: (i) that a former Head of State could not claim immunity ratione materiae in respect of acts of torture, because torture is prohibited by a rule of international law of universal application (the Torture Convention and customary law) and immunity ratione materiae cannot attach to conduct which international law criminalises; and (ii) on the second appeal (No. 3), by 6:1, that the extradition could proceed only in respect of conduct after 8 December 1988, when section 134 of the Criminal Justice Act 1988 — which alone gave the English courts jurisdiction over extraterritorial torture — came into force; and it was ultimately the Home Secretary's decision on medical grounds, and not the courts, that ended the proceedings. The case is the ancestor of the modern European universal-jurisdiction prosecutions, and it is the counterpoint to case 37: the sitting Foreign Minister is absolutely immune, but a former Head of State is not immune for torture and like crimes.

Is universal jurisdiction a threat to sovereignty? The critique (advanced by the African Union, and supported by the criticism of the Arrest Warrant majority by Judge Van den Wyngaert's opposite concern) is that it allows the powerful to judge the weak, that it disturbs the orderly allocation of competence, and that it is applied selectively. The modern answer — reflected in the principle of subsidiarity, in the custodian's obligation to prosecute or extradite, and in the complementarity principle of Article 17 of the Rome Statute — is that universal jurisdiction is a subsidiary device available where the primary States are unwilling or unable to act, and that the remedy for its abuse is not its abolition but its ordering: a universal-jurisdiction State must show a genuine link with the accused, must give priority to the State with custody and must respect the personal immunities of the troika. In that qualified form, universal jurisdiction is the last resort of a system of sovereign States which takes some crimes seriously enough to make them everyone's business.

C. Short Notes

Note 1 — the Lotus case

The steamship Lotus, a French mail vessel, collided with the Turkish collier Boz-Kourt on the high seas on 2 August 1926; eight Turkish sailors died. When the Lotus reached Constantinople, Turkey prosecuted the French officer of the watch, Lieutenant Demons, for manslaughter, and the Permanent Court of International Justice held, by the President's casting vote, that Turkey had not violated international law. The ratio is twofold and both halves are examinable: (i) a State may not exercise its power in the territory of another State, absent a permissive rule — but (ii) it may exercise jurisdiction in its own territory over acts done abroad, because restrictions on the independence of States cannot be presumed. From the judgment come objective territoriality, the effects doctrine and the modern test of the "genuine link"; the special rule on collision jurisdiction which the case left open was later written into Article 11 of the 1958 High Seas Convention and now Article 97 of UNCLOS.

Note 2 — the Savarkar case

Vinayak Damodar Savarkar, a British Indian subject being carried to India on the Morea for trial, escaped at Marseilles on 8 July 1910, swam ashore and was arrested by a French gendarmerie brigade which was unaware of his identity, and handed back to the British police, who took him on to India. France demanded his restoration; the dispute was referred to a five-member tribunal of the Permanent Court of Arbitration under the Agreement of 25 October 1910, which delivered its award on 24 February 1911. The Tribunal held that the case involved no recourse to fraud or force and no violation of French sovereignty, that all concerned had acted in good faith, and — while "admitting that an irregularity was committed by the arrest of Savarkar and by his being handed over to the British Police" — that "there is no rule of International Law imposing… any obligation on the Power which has in its custody a prisoner, to restore him because of a mistake committed by the foreign agent who delivered him up to that Power". The principle is the ancestor of the male capto bene judicato rule.

Note 3 — In re Piracy Jure Gentium

Chinese pirates attacked a Chinese cargo vessel, the Hai Sang, but fled before completing the robbery, and the question was whether the offence of piracy jure gentium had been committed so as to found jurisdiction under the Hong Kong legislation. The Privy Council held that piracy is a crime against the law of nations, that the essence of the offence is the intent to commit piracy ( animus furandi) combined with an act of aggression on the high seas, and that an actual robbery or a completed taking is not an essential element: an act done with the intention of committing piracy is itself piracy, and every State may try the offender. The case is the foundation of the modern law that piracy is the only classical crime of universal jurisdiction, and it explains why UNCLOS Articles 100 to 107 impose duties on all States to cooperate in its repression.

Note 4 — the Asylum and Haya de la Torre cases

After a failed rebellion in Peru in October 1948, its leader Víctor Raúl Haya de la Torre took refuge in the Colombian embassy at Lima on 3 January 1949. Colombia claimed the right to grant diplomatic asylum and to qualify the offence unilaterally as political, and sought a safe-conduct. In Asylum (Colombia v. Peru), I.C.J. Reports 1950, p. 266, the Court held that no regional custom obliged Peru to respect the asylum, because the practice of the Latin American States was inconsistent and influenced by political expediency; that the granting State could not unilaterally qualify the offence; that Peru was not obliged to grant safe-conduct; and that the asylum had been granted without legal authority, so that Colombia must terminate it. In Haya de la Torre (Colombia v. Peru), I.C.J. Reports 1951, p. 71, the Court held that the duty to terminate the asylum did not involve a duty to surrender the refugee, and that the manner of termination was for the parties to settle — which they did in 1954. The two cases are the leading authorities on diplomatic asylum, on the limits of regional custom, and on the difference between a right of asylum and a right to have a particular qualification accepted.

Note 5 — the Franconia case

The Franconia, a German vessel, collided with the British steamer Strathclyde in the English Channel within three miles of the English coast; a British subject was drowned, and the German captain, Keyn, was tried for manslaughter. In R. v. Keyn, (1876) 2 Ex. D. 63, the Court for Crown Cases Reserved held, by seven judges to six, that the common law courts had no jurisdiction over the territorial sea, which was not part of the realm; the indictment failed and the accused was discharged. The decision created a gap which Parliament closed by the Territorial Waters Jurisdiction Act, 1878, which declared that the territorial sea is within the jurisdiction of the Crown and that a foreigner on a foreign ship may be tried for an offence committed there — the statutory origin of the modern rule that a State's criminal jurisdiction extends to its territorial sea, and a striking illustration of the pre-Lotus law.

Note 6 — hijacking

Aircraft hijacking is the most successful example of treaty-based universal jurisdiction. The Tokyo Convention, 1963, requires the parties to take measures against unlawful interference on board and allocates jurisdiction, but its weakness is that it treats the offender as a fugitive rather than an offender; the Hague Convention for the Suppression of Unlawful Seizure of Aircraft, 1970, obliges every party to criminalise the seizure of an aircraft in flight by force or threat of force and to establish jurisdiction (Art. 4), and provides that the offence is deemed an extradition offence in every extradition treaty between the parties and that the convention itself may be the legal basis for extradition (Art. 8), with a duty to submit the case to prosecution where extradition is refused (Art. 7); the Montreal Convention for the Suppression of Unlawful Acts against the Safety of Civil Aviation, 1971, and its Protocols (1988, 2005) extend the offences to sabotage and other acts of violence, and the Beijing Convention and Protocol of 2010 modernise the régime. India implements the Hague Convention through the Anti-Hijacking Act, 1982, which was replaced by the Anti-Hijacking Act, 2016 — a statute enacted to align India's law with the Beijing Convention and Protocol of 2010, which confers jurisdiction on Indian courts over hijacking offences committed outside India, applies the "prosecute or extradite" rule, and provides for imprisonment for life and, where the offence results in the death of any person, for the death penalty. The Indian statute is thus the clearest example in Indian law of jurisdiction created by treaty over conduct abroad, unconnected with any Indian element.

Note 7 — diplomatic asylum

Diplomatic (or extra-territorial) asylum is refuge granted to a person within the territory of the territorial State but on premises under the control of the asylum-granting State — a diplomatic mission, consulate, warship or international institution. It is not a general institution of customary international law: the International Court of Justice so held in the Asylum case (1950), and the Vienna Convention on Diplomatic Relations, 1961 requires the premises of the mission to be used only for functions compatible with the mission (Art. 41(3)) and gives the receiving State the right at any time to require the recall of a diplomat. Diplomatic asylum is, however, a treaty institution in Latin America: the Havana Convention on Asylum, 1928, the Montevideo Convention on Political Asylum, 1933, and the Caracas Convention on Diplomatic Asylum, 1954, under which the granting State may qualify an offence as political, subject to the territorial State's right to demand that the fugitive leave the country within a reasonable time. Outside the region the practice is one of tolerance rather than right — as in the case of Cardinal Mindszenty in the United States legation in Budapest (1956–1971) or Julian Assange in the Ecuadorian embassy in London (2012–2019).

Note 8 — Mighell v. Sultan of Johore

Miss Mighell sued the Sultan of Johore in the English courts for breach of promise of marriage, alleging that the defendant had promised to marry her while travelling incognito in England under the name "Albert Baker". The Sultan pleaded that he was an independent sovereign and therefore immune from suit, and the Court of Appeal upheld the plea, holding that a foreign sovereign's immunity from the jurisdiction of the English courts extends to civil proceedings and to acts which could be described as private or commercial in character; the court accepted the statement of the sovereign's own authority, as communicated through the appropriate channel, that he possessed the requisite status for immunity. The case is the classical English authority on the immunity of a foreign Head of State and on the procedure by which the court ascertains sovereign status — and thus the ancestor of the modern statute (the State Immunity Act, 1978 in the United Kingdom) and of the restrictive theory now adopted in the United Nations Convention on Jurisdictional Immunities of States, 2004.

Note 9 — the effects doctrine and extra-territorial jurisdiction

The effects doctrine is a generous form of the objective territorial principle: conduct abroad is treated as committed within the State because its substantial effects are felt there. Its classic statements are the judgments of the Permanent Court in Lotus (1927) and of the United States Supreme Court in United States v. Aluminium Co. of America, 148 F.2d 416 (2d Cir. 1945) (the Alcoa case, on foreign cartels with effects in the United States); its most contested applications have been in competition and securities law, where the European Union and the United States have each applied their law to conduct abroad, provoking the "blocking statutes" of the United Kingdom (Protection of Trading Interests Act, 1980) and other States. The modern standard of restraint is section 403 of the American Law Institute's Restatement (Third) of the Foreign Relations Law, which permits the exercise of prescriptive jurisdiction only where it is "reasonable", weighing the links, the character of the activity, the interests of other States and the likelihood of conflict. In India the doctrine appears in section 32 of the Competition Act, 2002 (extraterritorial application where the agreement or abuse has, or is likely to have, an appreciable adverse effect on competition in the relevant market in India), section 75 of the Information Technology Act, 2000, and section 4(3) of the Penal Code — and its constitutional and international limits were discussed in the Vodafone case, (2012) 6 S.C.C. 613.

Note 10 — non-refoulement

Non-refoulement is the prohibition on returning a person to a State where he would be at risk of persecution or of prohibited ill-treatment. Its treaty source is Article 33(1) of the Refugee Convention, 1951: "No Contracting State shall expel or return ('refouler') a refugee in any manner whatsoever to the frontiers of territories where his life or freedom would be threatened on account of his race, religion, nationality, membership of a particular social group or political opinion", subject to the national-security exception in Article 33(2). The phrase "in any manner whatsoever" means the principle reaches rejection at the frontier, interception at sea and informal removal — as the European Court of Human Rights held in Hirsi Jamaa v. Italy (2012), which held Italy responsible for handing intercepted migrants back to Libya. An inseparable and absolute parallel prohibition arises under Article 3 of the Torture Convention (no expulsion or extradition where there are substantial grounds for believing that the person would be subjected to torture) and Article 7 of the ICCPR; the International Law Commission's Draft Articles on the Expulsion of Aliens (2014) state the general prohibition (Arts. 23 and 24). India is not a party to the Refugee Convention and has no asylum statute; nor has it ratified the Torture Convention (it signed on 14 October 1997), so the prohibition on return to torture binds India through Article 7 of the ICCPR, the customary prohibition of torture and inhuman treatment, and — it may be argued — Article 21; the Supreme Court has left open, in the Rohingya litigation, the question whether non-refoulement is part of Article 21.

Six rules for writing these answers

1. Open with the two-part Lotus formula (prescribe/enforce) and use it in every answer. 2. Name the provision and the case together — "section 21(b), Abu Salem" — and never a case without a proposition. 3. In a problem question, run the four steps: basis → power (custody, sanction, forum) → immunity → remedy. 4. Quote one line from the case material (the Savarkar award, Ganguly J. in Abu Salem, or paragraphs 59–61 of the Arrest Warrant judgment) — an examiner can see instantly that the primary source has been read. 5. Add the modern instance: 48 treaties and 12 arrangements with the date of the parliamentary reply; 274 fugitives returned between 2019 and 2026 mostly by deportation; the Rana extradition of April 2025; the July 2026 MAPA convictions under §6.3. 6. Conclude by returning to the syllabus head, so that the answer reads as an answer to the question asked and not as a general essay.

CAppendix C · Glossary

Table 6.19 · Glossary of jurisdiction, extradition and asylum
TermMeaning as used in these notes
JurisdictionThe power of a State under international law to regulate or otherwise affect people, property and circumstances; in this topic, prescriptive and enforcement jurisdiction are distinguished throughout.
Prescriptive (legislative) jurisdictionThe competence to make law applying to a person, act or thing; under Lotus a State may prescribe for conduct abroad, subject to prohibitive rules.
Enforcement (executive) jurisdictionThe competence actually to exercise State authority — arrest, search, seizure, investigation, service and execution; it may not be exercised in another State's territory without consent.
Adjudicative jurisdictionThe competence of courts to decide; distinct from both the prescription and the enforcement, and subject to immunity.
Subjective territorialityJurisdiction over conduct begun within the territory but completed abroad.
Objective territorialityJurisdiction over conduct begun abroad but completed within the territory.
Effects doctrineThe extended form of objective territoriality: jurisdiction because substantial effects are felt in the State (Competition Act s. 32; IT Act s. 75).
Active nationality principleJurisdiction over a State's own nationals for conduct abroad (IPC s. 4(1)).
Passive personality principleJurisdiction by reference to the nationality of the victim (now chiefly treaty-based).
Protective principleJurisdiction over acts abroad threatening the State's security, currency, public order or vital interests.
Universal jurisdictionJurisdiction over certain crimes regardless of any territorial, national or protective link; permissive, not obligatory, and subsidiary.
Treaty (auxiliary) jurisdictionCompetence created by treaty allocation, as in the law of the sea, the aviation conventions and the anti-terrorism conventions.
Aut dedere aut judicareProsecute or extradite; the duty of a custodial State under the sectoral conventions and s. 34A of the Extradition Act.
Male capto bene judicatoA person properly tried is not released because he was irregularly captured; the principle of Savarkar, Eichmann and Alvarez-Machain, qualified by Bennett.
RenditionTransfer of a person outside the forms of extradition, whether by arrangement or by irregular means.
Immunity ratione personaeFull personal immunity of an incumbent Head of State, Head of Government or Foreign Minister, without distinction of capacity; ceases with office.
Immunity ratione materiaeImmunity attaching to official acts; continues after office, and the point decided against in Pinochet for torture.
InviolabilityThe freedom of a person, premises, archives or correspondence from interference; a distinct and often wider protection than immunity from jurisdiction.
WaiverThe State's renunciation of immunity, which must be express or established by clear conduct.
ExtraditionThe surrender of a person accused or convicted of an offence by the State where he is to the State which claims him, in order that he be tried or punished.
Double criminalityThe rule that the conduct must be criminal in both the requesting and the requested State.
Rule of specialityThe rule that a surrendered person may be tried only for the offence for which he was surrendered (or a lesser offence on the same facts, or with consent): s. 21 of the Extradition Act.
Political-offence exceptionThe refusal to surrender a person for an offence of a political character, or where the request is in fact made to punish him for a political offence: s. 31(1)(a).
Attentat clauseThe exclusion of attacks on the life of a foreign Head of State from the political-offence exception (Belgium, 1856), ancestor of the Schedule.
Prima facie caseThe case which the requesting State must establish before the magistrate under s. 7(3) of the Extradition Act as a condition of committal.
Rule of non-inquiryThe practice by which the requested State does not examine the fairness of the requesting State's justice system; not absolute.
AssurancesUndertakings by the requesting State (for example, that the death penalty will not be imposed), to be tested for sufficiency and reliability.
Deportation / expulsionRemoval of an alien from the territory by the State where he is, under the Foreigners Act, 1946 and its orders; distinct from extradition.
AsylumProtection granted by a State to a person fleeing another State; territorial (a matter of sovereignty) and extra-territorial or diplomatic (a treaty institution in Latin America).
RefugeeA person with a well-founded fear of persecution for a Convention reason who is outside his country and unable or unwilling to return — Art. 1A(2).
Non-refoulementThe prohibition of return to a State where life or freedom would be threatened (Art. 33) or where there is a real risk of torture (Torture Convention Art. 3; ICCPR Art. 7).
Collective expulsionThe removal of a group without individual examination; prohibited, in the European system, by Article 4 of Protocol No. 4 (Hirsi Jamaa).
ComityThe practice of mutual accommodation among States, which explains the restraint with which jurisdiction is ordinarily exercised.
SubsidiarityThe principle that the State with the stronger link should act, and that universal jurisdiction is a reserve device.
ComplementarityThe ICC's allocation rule (Rome Statute Art. 17): a case is inadmissible where a State genuinely investigates or prosecutes.
Blocking statuteA law prohibiting compliance with a foreign State's extra-territorial orders and providing for recovery of judgments paid (the United Kingdom's Protection of Trading Interests Act, 1980).
Genuine linkThe requirement, drawn from Lotus and Nottebohm, that there be a real connection between the State and the person or conduct it regulates.

6.5 AsylumTerritorial asylum is a matter of sovereignty; diplomatic asylum is a Latin American treaty institution and not general custom1967 Declaration on Territorial Asylum; Caracas Convention 1954Asylum (1950); Haya de la Torre (1951)No right to unilateral qualification

EAppendix E · Provenance of Sources and Note on Verification

1. The primary source of the project. The Delhi University course material for Public International Law (paper LB-205), IInd Term Public International Law LB205 2022, is the primary source for these notes. The Topic-6 portion of that material runs through the casebook's treatment of State Jurisdiction and consists of: the syllabus list of cases 37 to 39; the judgment of the Supreme Court in Abu Salem Abdul Qayoom Ansari v. State of Maharashtra (casebook pp. 177–185, including the concurring judgment of Ganguly J.); and the Arrest and Return of Savarkar materials (casebook pp. 186–189, including the text of the United Kingdom–France Arbitration Agreement of 25 October 1910 and the Award of 24 February 1911). Where the notes use the casebook, they say so.

2. Case 37 and the provenance note. The DU case material does not print a digest of case 37, Arrest Warrant of 11 April 2000 (Democratic Republic of the Congo v. Belgium), I.C.J. Reports 2002, p. 3; it appears in the syllabus by citation only. The treatment in §6.3 G is therefore derived from the Court's judgment and its own official summary (the summary published by the Registry, available at the Court's website), with the separate and dissenting opinions noted by name. Nothing in §6.3 G is presented as casebook material. The same convention is followed for modern developments which are reported from press or institutional sources rather than from a judgment or a statute: the source is identified in the text, and the material is used for illustration only.

3. The other sources used, in order of weight. (i) The Delhi University class notes for Topic 6 (PIL Notes, "5 Extradition, Deportation and Asylum" and following), used for the definitions and the framework of the class teaching; (ii) the Exam Capsule and the Smart Notes issued with the course, used for the examination framework, the question list and the supporting authorities cited there; (iii) S.K. Verma, An Introduction to Public International Law (2nd edn), Chapters 7 (State Jurisdiction) and 9 (State and Individual), used for the general principles, the case law not printed in the casebook, and the Indian statutory discussion; (iv) the official texts of the instruments — the Extradition Act, 1962, the Foreigners Act, 1946, the Code of Civil Procedure, 1908, the Indian Penal Code and its successor statutes, UNCLOS, the Refugee Convention and the other treaties — consulted through the bare-act and treaty texts; (v) the decisions themselves, in the reports cited in the Table of Cases; and (vi) where a current figure or a very recent development is given, the institutional source is named in the text — the Ministry of External Affairs' reply in Parliament of 19 December 2025 for the treaty and request figures, and the reporting of the Union Cabinet's decisions of August 2026 for the newest treaty approvals.

4. Matters which the reader should verify before relying on them in an examination or in practice. The following are stated in the notes on the footing of the sources described, and should be checked against the current position if precision is important:

  1. Post-judgment developments in case 38. The Portuguese annulment proceedings of 2015, the arbitration between Abu Salem and the Union of India and the reported awards of 2023 and 2024 are drawn from public reporting, not from the case material or a judgment in the project files. They are noted in §6.5 F as context only.
  2. Numbers. The figures for India's extradition treaties (48), arrangements (12), requests (137 over five years), successful extraditions (25) and the 274 persons returned between 2019 and 2026 are those given in Parliament and in the press in 2025–26 and change with each new treaty; the date is stated wherever a figure appears.
  3. Status of instruments. India's position on the Torture Convention (signed, not ratified), the Refugee Convention (not a party), the Rome Statute (not a party), the UN Cybercrime Convention (not signed as at the latest reporting) and the Additional Protocols to the Geneva Conventions (not asserted here) is stated as at the dates of the sources; these positions can change.
  4. Pending litigation. The Rohingya litigation (Mohammad Salimullah v. Union of India) is described as the courts have left it — interim orders declining relief, with the constitutional question open. The C.A.A. challenges and other pending matters are mentioned only in general terms.
  5. The Schedule to the Extradition Act, 1962. The Schedule still carries its 1993 references (the Anti-Hijacking Act, 1982; TADA, 1987; the Penal Code). The notes explain the modern equivalents (the Anti-Hijacking Act, 2016; the UAPA, 1967; the Bharatiya Nyaya Sanhita, 2023) but the reader should check whether the Government has exercised the power under section 31(3) to amend the list.
  6. Texts. Quotations from judgments and instruments are reproduced from the casebook, from the official summaries and from bare-act texts; where a quotation is a translation, or where several editions of an instrument differ (as with the ILC's draft articles, where the numbering of the non-refoulement provisions has varied between the draft and the adopted text), the version relied on is that of the final adopted text as published by the institution concerned.

5. A last word on method. The purpose of these notes is to make the Topic-6 syllabus completely answerable from one document: every head of the syllabus, every prescribed case in the form required by the standing instruction (facts, issues, arguments of both sides, decision and the principle evolved), every provision inserted at the point where it arises, and every question asked in the examination with a model answer. Where the notes go beyond the casebook they say so; where they describe practice rather than law they say so; and where a proposition rests only on the class notes or on a single text, the authority is named in the text itself.
